# Bushido Coda

_The blade does not lie. Only the hand holding it._

## Part I: Teeth

[Beat 1]
She said his name wrong. Three times, and Kyle didn't correct her. The apartment smelled like camphor and fear-sweat and something frying in old oil three doors down. Her husband sat in the corner with his jaw wired shut into a brutalist art installation. He breathed through his nose like a man struggling to learn a new woodwind instrument. She pressed the credstick into Kyle's hand — a physical transfer, warm chip, slightly damp. He felt what she'd paid. He felt what she hadn't kept.

[Beat 2]
He took the job at half rate. He told himself it was the jaw. The jaw, wired shut, a man reduced to breathing and waiting. He didn't think about the girl standing in the hallway behind the curtain, watching him the way children watch things they've already decided to memorize.

[Beat 3]
The building was less a structure than a welded disagreement between two towers, twelve stories slotted in and forgotten by the corponation property registry sometime in the 2190s. Kyle had ridden the freight elevator up because the resident elevator had been repurposed as a makeshift dump and an open-air toilet. Looking out the apartment's one window: the canyon between the towers, the rain coming straight down in the cold pillar of air between two structures that had not been designed to leave a gap and had been forced to leave one anyway. On the underside of the maintenance rail bridging the gap, someone had chalked hash marks in tidy groups of seven, the last group stopped at seven and started fresh, never run to eight. A VTOL crossed the gap forty stories up, running lights amber, moving toward something on the south face. Some board director was late to dinner. Some kid was getting their first cyberware. Someone in the Glooms was falling in love for the first time, or being kidnapped for the first time. Kyle filed the running lights and didn't look up again.

[Beat 4]
The motorcycle was parked under an overhang that mostly kept it dry. The overhang was an Axiom cable gantry — the power corponation's hardware, if not its attention — half the block spliced into this forgotten artery because some automated system had never flagged the bleed as significant enough to warrant a repair crew. The math just wasn't there. Kyle's bike was a custom job. Matte black, unbranded, plate magnetized to the underside of the rear cowl for easy swap out. The kind of bike that disappears into the visual noise of any GLMZ block; the kind that's there when you get back because you didn't make it interesting enough for anyone to steal.

[Beat 5]
At Cermak and Loomis the light did the thing it sometimes did at Cermak and Loomis — a quality of flatness, a moment pressed between two surfaces, the shadows of the elevated signage falling at the wrong angle for the time of day and then immediately not. His hardware clocked the anomaly, noted it, and declined to offer an explanation. The GLMZ was full of places like this: intersections where the physics had been used badly at some point in the previous century and had not fully recovered. A 19Hz hum buzzed from no direction and every direction at once. Somewhere down the block a bellwether rat sounded its two-tone cry, high then low, twice, and along the wet storefronts people stepped back into their doorways and stood there saying nothing, and stayed standing there as he passed. Some of them were in the databases. Most weren't. You learned them the way you learned everything useful in this city — by riding through them until they stopped surprising you.

[Beat 6]
He rode south to the Gray Zone's eastern strut. The route was twenty-two minutes of switchbacks down through tiers the architecture had stopped pretending were planned somewhere around the fortieth floor. By the time he was at the bottom of the strut the rain was warmer and the buildings overhead had closed the sky to a strip. He put a hand on the strut wall to steady the bike on the last grade and the ferrocement was warm under his palm, an hour past the last of the light, the way it always was down here. He didn't think about that either. He thought about the jaw. He thought about the camphor smell. He thought about what half-rate meant when half-rate had been pressed warm and damp into your palm by a stranger's hand.

[Beat 7]
He parked the bike a block out and walked the rest. The crew on the loading dock would not be expecting him. Surprise was free.

The loading dock sat at the bottom of the Gray Zone's eastern strut, where the architecture stopped imitating architecture and became what it actually was: welded industrial grief. Kyle came in through the front. The personnel door beside the roll-up had a steel pull handle worn mirror-bright in one long oval, the exact shape of ten thousand hands closing on it in the same place, while the rest of the door went on rusting around it. No hood. No approach angle. He walked in through the front because he wanted the moment — that specific, clarifying moment when six people who have spent weeks feeling like a force of nature looked up and saw the weather approaching without pretense.

[Beat 8]
Kyle is six feet of nothing. That's the first thing. A swimmer's build stripped past lean into something the body shouldn't sustain. His cheekbones throw shadows in low light. His forearms, emerging from rolled sleeves, are rope and tendon and zero forgiveness — no softness anywhere, no insulation, as if the hardware in his skull has been running a going-out-of-business sale on his body mass for years. Thirty-two thousand electrodes in his brain don't come free. The Neuretics lattice bills him in calories and he has been paying the invoice in flesh since he was sixteen.

[Beat 9]
The katana across his back is matte black — a forty-inch draw, hilt over the right shoulder, the ito wrapping dark with use. The blade has a name; that name is Silence. He has, when pressed, a vague recollection of where the name came from, and does not press. Because digging around in old wounds with dirty fingernails is just asking for trouble.

[Beat 10]
On his low left hip, holstered but not secured, sits the Torii TSS-3 — bird's-head grip, no stock, the silhouette of an oversized revolver under a coat. Five 410 slugs in the cylinder. Cacophony. This one he did name himself. Silence was for the rooms he could cross. Cacophony was for the ones he couldn't — the distance the body couldn't eat fast enough, the angle the blade couldn't reach. He kept the count the way other men kept time. Five shells was five chances to discover you should have planned better, and Kyle planned for a living, which meant Cacophony mostly stayed quiet, which suited the joke of the thing — the loudest object in his loadout was the one he most preferred not to use.

[Beat 11]
Kyle stepped across the threshold that separates the weather from the room, and stood still, and let them look.

One of them had a chrome arm — a full replacement job, the shoulder-socket kind, myomer-bundled, hydraulic-assisted, capable of punching through light vehicle plating. Across the table from him sat a woman the array flagged before Kyle's eyes found a reason to: slight, still, her weight already on the balls of her feet, and hardware nested at every hinge of her — actuator housings at the wrists, the elbows, the knees, the toes of her boots. Retractable blades. A body renovated joint by joint into a knife block, by someone with skill, for someone with intent, and nothing about the work decorative. One had sub-dermal thoracic plating, the seams visible at his collar like a man wearing himself wrong. One had a facial modification at the jaw that had been done badly enough to pull the skin tight on the left side — someone who'd needed it fast and cheap, who had a history of getting his face broken and had run down the original parts. The fifth was younger, chrome at the knuckles only — discharge plates, the kind that spit sparks on contact and let a twenty-year-old hit like a forklift, provided whatever he hit agreed to stand still. He was already rolling his shoulder. The sixth was the one Kyle tracked first — no visible augmentation, which meant internal, which meant expensive, which meant he was the one who'd made the decisions.

[Beat 12]
They were playing cards. Drinking something amber from unlabeled bottles. The laughter died at the pace that laughter always dies when the math changes in a room.

[Beat 13]
Kyle's chest warmed — the array. A deep, cellular warmth behind the sternum, like a coal blown to life, the firmware coming up from idle into the resolution it kept for rooms like this. Thirty-two thousand electrodes, two hundred and fifty-six threads, processing at a speed his mouth could not follow.

The room rendered itself. Six bodies. Heat signatures. The capacitor whine of knuckle plates waking up at the card table. The doubled click of wrist housings unlocking under a woman's sleeves, discreet as a watch being wound. The boy's shoulder telegraphing its intention before the intention had finished forming.

*Ballistic precognition* — the NeoCortex program's term, which Kyle had always found imprecise. The array did not predict. It read the half-second already in motion and put it on the page where he could read it too, and his body was already moving before the thought resolved. He had stopped trying to explain the distinction to anyone who asked.

It rendered one thing more, and rendered it the way it rendered nothing else: the plumbing. Every major artery in the room laid in as a bright forbidden line, the body's own load-bearing infrastructure annotated in light. Chrome was fair game. The lines were law. Cut everything; cross nothing. That was the whole of the code, and the array kept the map so the hand could keep the promise.

[Beat 14]
Silence cleared the saya in one continuous draw — right hand over the right shoulder, the steel-carbon-nanotube meta alloy blade alive in the air at the bottom of the motion. No flourish. The kawagane was matte black and stayed matte black and the dock's amber overheads found nothing on the blade to reflect because reflection was not what the blade did. He brought it across his body and settled into the stance and his right foot slid back a quarter step into the line Seo had drilled him into for nine years before letting him work alone.

The stance was the stance. The specific afternoon the foot-line had been corrected for the last time; the exact memory he could not locate, which had stopped bothering him at some point he also could not locate.

[Beat 15]
Cacophony stayed on his hip.

The room was crossable. That was the whole calculus, run before his boot crossed the threshold: twenty-some feet of dock floor, six bodies, a card table, and a route between them the array had drawn in the half-second before anyone thought to stand. The gun was for plans that failed. He had not failed to plan.

Silence moved. What the room heard was almost nothing — a chair going over, a bottle ringing on concrete, the wet exhale of men understanding that the weather had already arrived. The loudest sound on the dock was still chrome hitting the floor. The blood made no sound at all. It simply went where the cuts sent it, and every cut had been told where to stop.

[Beat 16]
The boy threw first, because the boy had thrown first his whole short career. The haymaker came up out of the chair with his hips and his heart in it, knuckle plates discharging on the upswing so that the fist swung round trailing sparks like a grinder kissing steel. It was a proven punch. It had ended arguments. It had broken jaws — older jaws, slower jaws, jaws attached to men who had asked for one more week and stood there hoping while it wound up — and every jaw it broke had taught the boy the same wrong lesson, which was that the punch worked.

The punch worked on people who could not afford to move. Kyle had already moved. He stepped inside the arc — not away from it, inside it, into the dead zone where a haymaker is nothing but a man falling in a circle — set his palm against the boy's shoulder blade as the mass went by, and helped. Physics handled the rest. The card table took him at the waist and folded; cards and amber liquor went up; the sparking knuckles hit the wet floor and ground their charge out in it, fizzing like the end of a fuse. And somewhere inside that fall, almost in passing, Silence had opened the boy's forearm along the flexor line — an inch and a half, clean, deep enough to unstring the fist, wide of the radial artery by the width of a decision — so that when he put his hand down to rise, the hand declined. He sat back down in the wreck of the table, holding his arm, watching his blood feather into the spilled liquor, and learned what every collector learns the night the arithmetic reverses: the punch had never been the weapon. The fear had been the weapon, and Kyle hadn't brought any.

[Beat 17]
The second cut found the sub-dermal plating man as he rose — but he rose shooting. A compact auto off his belt, three rounds inside the half-second the array needed to close the line: two into Kyle's lower back as he turned through the second man's lane, a third skipping off at an angle that burned a furrow above his hip. The mesh under his skin took the two square hits the way a net takes a diver — total arrest, no penetration, and a cost the net does not advertise. The breath went out of him in one flat concussion. His line shifted a handspan off its mark, and for the duration of one shortened step the room contained the possibility of a different ending.

The plating man had organized his entire fighting philosophy around the assumption that nothing would penetrate his vitals, and nothing did. Silence took the power coupling at his collar seam instead — the thumb-wide gap where the thoracic plate drank from his spinal bus — and the stroke's exit wrote a thin red line across the trapezius above it, shallow as a caution, blood welling up over armor that had kept every promise except the one that mattered. Sixty pounds of plate went from asset to anchor between one heartbeat and the next. He folded under his own chrome and stayed folded, the pistol skating away from him across the corrugated steel. He'd been in fights before. He knew he was out of this one before it had properly begun. His only hope now was to stay still. And quiet. Still and quiet, and bleeding politely, and the only thing of his that died tonight would be hardware.

[Beat 18]
The chrome-arm man was already swinging by then, which was the correct response from a fighter with hydraulic-assisted reach and a head start on the geometry. The arm came around flat and hard at the height where Kyle's collarbone had been, torque enough behind it to cave a vehicle door, and the array read the motor bus the way it read all chrome — clean electromagnetic bloom, full lateral extension, intercept at the throat.

You do not block an arm like that. You audit it. Kyle stepped under the arc and let the better part of a decade of financing swing through the space he had stopped occupying, and as it passed overhead he brought Silence up through the shoulder seam — the thumb-wide service gap where the actuator cabling surfaced between housing and socket, the exposed wire bundle every full-replacement rig carries and every owner forgets — and severed the bus in the time the swing took to finish believing in itself. The arm completed its arc without instructions. The mass that powered it kept going, took the man's balance along as a courtesy, and laid him out across a stack of crates with the limb still flexing on residual hydraulic pressure — formidable, severed, salvage. He stared at it with the precise expression of a man watching eleven years of payments declare themselves a total loss, and he did not get back up.

[Beat 19]
The fourth cut took the badly-done jaw man's jaw — the rig, not the jaw, though the jaw paid its tithe. Kyle had saved it for the count's last beat because the array had already mapped what would happen: the anchor line of the cheap facial armature, the shear angle, the way the hardware would skid into the drainage channel when it let go. The array had been correct in the way the array was always correct about the physics of falling chrome. The rig tore free with the sound of cheap work surrendering, and the gums the bad surgery had conscripted as mounting posts came away bleeding around the anchor points, and the man went down to his knees with both hands at his face, spitting red into the runoff — alive, awake, and ruined in the same cheap way he had already been ruined once and had financed rather than fixed. The teeth went with the rig, scattering across the floor like a macabre game of dice.

[Beat 20]
Four seconds. Four cuts. Four down and not getting up, and every one of them alive, and the dock floor already threading thin red lines toward the drainage channels — every line of it placed. The array held the map of what a body could lose and keep living, and Silence had gone wide of every bright forbidden line by margins Kyle no longer had to think about, because the thinking had been done years ago, in a dojo he could not entirely locate, and the array merely kept the books.

He stood in the middle of it with two points of deep percussion blooming under the mesh in his lower back and a wet line above his hip where the third round had opened skin, the torn jacket showing a square inch of the dull grey weave underneath — the lattice doing the one thing it existed to do, which was not the same as doing it for free. He had read the plating man as armor and missed the pistol. That was the mistake, and he was wearing it now, and he would be wearing it tomorrow. The array had not seen it coming because the array read chrome — motor buses, power couplings, the electromagnetic bloom of hardware declaring its intentions — and the gun had been nothing but a gun.

Cacophony's cylinder still held five, and the count stayed unspent, because the woman had not rushed him. That was the thing worth knowing about her, and it had been worth knowing since the threshold: while her crew spent itself, she had stepped back out of the spray, chosen clean floor, and let the blades out.

[Beat 21]
They came out of her the way good hardware comes out of good cabinetry — the wrists first, twin lengths of pale-edged alloy running back along the forearms; then the elbows, short and triangular, built for the turn; and when she rose onto the balls of her feet the boot toes opened and showed steel, and a flexed knee showed more. Eight edges on a slight frame, every housing flush, every deployment silent except for that doubled click she hadn't bothered to disguise. The array lit her up like a switchboard — actuator blooms at every joint — and priced her honestly: faster than the boy, smarter than the swing, the only fighter on this dock besides the seated man who had ever trained against people who knew what they were doing.

She came in on an angle that took the card table out of his footwork and put the crates at his back, which meant she had been reading the dock as a fighting floor while her crew had been reading it as furniture. Kyle revised her price upward and gave ground exactly once, to put the spilled liquor between them, because wet floor taxes whoever lunges.

[Beat 22]
The first pass was hers. Wrist blade, backhand line; a feint off the elbow that became the knee; three edges inside a second and a half, and the array read each actuator fire its half-second early, and Kyle was never quite where the edges arrived. He didn't counter. A counter is a bid, and he was still pricing the table. She spun out of the third strike clean, reset her angle, and came again — committed this time, full extension, her whole slight mass behind the wrist blade, because from where she stood the half-second was invisible and a man who keeps almost being hit looks like a man getting lucky, and you press luck before it settles.

The commitment was the door he had given ground to build. Kyle turned his hips off the line, received her forearm on his free palm the way you receive something handed to you, and added four inches to a lunge that had been measured to the inch. Past full extension, a blade is cargo. While she paid for those four inches, Silence traced the underside of her forearm — not the flesh line, the service line: the shallow channel where the wrist actuator's cable bundle surfaced between housing and grip, the maintenance gap her conversion clinic had promised her no one would ever find in a live fight. The bundle parted with a static pop. The wrist blade died at half-extension, locked, a dead weight bolted to a living arm.

She was good; she converted without a breath, rolling through into the elbow blade on the turn — but it was the same turn the array had now watched twice, and Kyle was already under it, and Silence crossed the back of her thigh above the knee housing. A hand's width of opened muscle, depth governed to a hair, wide of the femoral line by the same deliberate margin as every cut tonight, and exactly deep enough that the leg resigned. She went down onto the knee whose toe blade scraped one bright useless arc in the corrugated steel, and she did not scream. She exhaled — long, furious, professional — put her free hand flat on the floor, and retracted everything that still answered, one joint at a time: the surrender of someone who understood precisely what had been left intact, and that it had been left on purpose.

[Beat 23]
Silence found the seams, and where there were no seams it found the depth — the exact depth at which a body stops fighting and keeps living. That was the whole instrument: a sword in a room full of chrome, a scalpel in a room full of meat, and a hand that knew which room it was standing in from one cut to the next. Five down, and not one of them dying. Not because the blade had been gentle — because the blade had been exact, and exactness was the only mercy on offer. The dock floor looked like a chop shop after a raid — an arm, a forearm, a jaw rig, a wrist blade locked at half-mast — and through the salvage the blood ran thin and honest toward the channels, every line of it survivable, every line of it placed there on purpose, which was the part the survivors would think about later, in the quiet, with the lights off.

[Beat 24]
Kyle was already turning when the boss stood up. He had not drawn during the four-cut count. He had not drawn while the bladed woman was being dismantled an edge at a time. He had stood up with the unhurried economy of a man who had sat at a table and watched his crew dismantled and had decided, calmly, that it was now his turn — and the hand that went to his hip was not reaching for a sidearm. He carried his own katana. Kyle had not registered the weapon earlier because the saya was inside the long coat, no protrusion, no tell, no detail the array had a category for in a room full of chrome. The boss stepped clear of the table, chose his ground with his eyes before his feet took it, and set his heels on the one stretch of dock floor that held neither cards nor chrome nor blood. House manners. Old ones.

[Beat 25]
The boss drew. Clean. The blade came out at the right angle in the right tempo, point neither high nor low, weight settling into a guard that was not borrowed from any school Kyle could name and which therefore was not a school's guard at all but a man's. He had been trained — specifically, somewhere expensive, for a duel he had no reason to believe would ever be granted to him. Kyle could see it in the way his weight stayed off his front foot, in the way he held the line: a man spending something he had been saving for a long time.

Then the boss did the thing the century had no category for: he bowed. Shallow, eyes up, hands where they were — the bow of a man acknowledging the other half of his own education. Around them the dock dripped and an amber running light crawled across the gap forty stories up and five people lay quietly bleeding into the concrete — blood and money both — and inside all of it the two swords made a small, older room. Kyle returned the bow to the inch. You honor the form, or you are just a man holding a museum piece.

[Beat 26]
*The duel. He has been waiting for the duel. The chrome was the ticket price.*

[Beat 27]
Neither moved. The rain worked the roof. Somewhere under the dock a pump cycled, and the severed forearm relaxed its fingers with a sound like a dropped glove, and neither man's eyes went to it.

Then they closed.

[Beat 28]
First exchange — the boss tested the line. A short measured cut at Kyle's right shoulder, no commitment in it, the kind of strike a swordsman uses to read his opponent's reach and tempo and the specific way he holds his weight. Silence rose to meet it. The blades touched edge to flat at a shallow angle. Steel rang, a clean sustained note in the concrete, and the force came back through Kyle's wrist into Kyle's shoulder into Kyle's stance the way force had been coming back through swords since the iron age. The boss recognized the read he had been given — Kyle's reach, Kyle's tempo — and stepped back without expression. He had wanted that information and he had paid one exchange for it. His face showed nothing. His weight stayed off the front foot. But his guard moved a quarter inch in a way that the array catalogued and filed.

[Beat 29]
*Patient. Correct. Rehearsed. He has fought this duel a thousand times in training and never once against a stranger. Use it.*

[Beat 30]
Second exchange — Kyle pressed. A two-step advance, the cut coming high and right, and the boss met it with a parry that was not a parry but a deflection, his blade sliding along Silence's flat to redirect the energy past his shoulder. Smart. Conservative. He had watched four cuts cross a room in three seconds and he was not going to bet his line against that speed; he would spend himself carefully or not at all. Kyle adjusted. The boss had told him something in return: *I will not let you charge me cleanly. I will work the angles.*

[Beat 31]
*He's fighting the duel he rehearsed. You are fighting him. These are different fights.*

[Beat 32]
Third exchange — the boss came in committed. He had read enough. He cut high left, fast, and Kyle's parry was a fraction late because the array's micro-tremor read on the boss had been clean but the boss's body had been clean in return, no telegraph, no shoulder bloom, just a man who had decided to commit and committed. Silence took the cut at the proper angle but the boss had already let the blade slide off the parry into a follow-through, and the follow-through grazed Kyle's left forearm — three inches of skin opened, not deep, the cut shallow enough that the subdermal mesh registered the pressure and decided not to engage. Blood came up immediately. Kyle's interior catalogue tagged it without comment: *vein not artery, three-quarters of an inch lateral of the radial, will not bleed out, will impair grip on the shotgun side in approximately ninety seconds.*

Kyle stepped out of measure and dipped his point an inch — the old acknowledgment, the dojo's nod: the touch is yours. The boss received it without expression, but his shoulders came down a hair, the way a man settles when the stranger across from him turns out to speak his language.

[Beat 33]
The boss saw the blood. He understood what the cut meant — that he was inside Kyle's range, that he had landed first, that the fastest thing he had ever seen cross a room could be touched. A man can be forgiven for letting that knowledge move his weight forward. Confidence travels downhill, from the eyes to the hands to the feet, and the boss's feet were already a half-step more committed than they had been an exchange ago.

[Beat 34]
*Laugh or you'd cry. One touch and he is already writing the story of the night. He has stopped studying you and started believing the rehearsal. Let him.*

[Beat 35]
Fourth exchange — the boss thrust. A straight committed thrust at Kyle's center mass, the kind of strike that asked everything of the blade and the arm and the line, and Silence came down to meet it not at the edge and not at the flat but at the seppa — the small collar between the tsuba and the blade, the part of the sword that classical schools said you should never use — and the thrust caught there, blade to blade, locked. Kyle drove forward. He kept the lock. He let Silence's edge ride along the flat of the boss's blade like a skate on ice, the edge tracking down the boss's steel from the tip toward the middle toward the tsuba, and where the boss's blade met the boss's hand, the edge of Silence met the boss's right wrist.

[Beat 36]
The edge met the wrist — and the wrist was not flesh. Lab-grown dermis parted over an actuator collar; carbon-fiber carpals; a myomer bundle wound tighter than any tendon ever managed. Covert chrome, tower-grade, the kind that costs more for looking like nothing than other men's hardware costs for looking like war. The internal augmentation the array had priced from the doorway had been under the sword hand all along. Silence did not care about that either. The edge passed through the hand's architecture the way it passed through anything when the angle was right and the line was committed, because Silence was a sword, and what swords cared about was the geometry of where the edge met the joint, and the geometry was correct.

[Beat 37]
The boss's right hand and the boss's katana fell together to the corrugated steel. No blood followed them down. The stump glistened — a clean cross-section of sealed lines and silvered bone-analogue, engineering some tower surgical team had been very proud of — and the neural bus sent enough static up his arm that his knees went, not from any shock of the body but from the shock of the wiring. He took the fall the way he had taken his guard: correctly. He came down kneeling. He looked at his hand on the floor, still holding his sword. He looked at Kyle. He said something in a language Kyle did not speak — three words, very quiet, possibly a name, possibly a prayer, possibly an instruction to whoever had trained him — and then, from his knees, with his ruin in front of him, he bowed. Kyle bowed back. Deeper than the first time. The duel had been real. Both of them had paid for it honestly.

[Beat 38]
He did not bleed out. There was nothing to bleed. The array, which timed everything, priced it instead: more Φ lying on the corrugated steel in that one severed hand than the rest of the crew had spent on themselves combined, and no clinic in the Glooms able to reattach what a sword had cross-sectioned at that angle. Flesh heals for free. Chrome bills at list price. Everyone on that floor would walk out of the Gray Zone tonight, stitched and limping and alive, and everyone on that floor would spend years paying for it in installments. That was the sentence. The mercy was incidental.

[Beat 39]
Twenty-three seconds for the duel. Thirty-four on the dock floor end to end.

It should have been twenty. The duel he would replay for weeks was the duel as fought: half a beat slow on the third exchange, the breath not arriving where the form expected it, the rotation pulling short over the bruising in his lower back — and the boss had seen the short side, and gone at it, and been correct to. The follow-through that opened Kyle's left forearm lived inside that half-beat. He had won on line, not on speed. The difference had a price, and he was still bleeding it.

[Beat 40]
Kyle cleared Silence with one flat snap of the wrist — chiburi, the old form, a thin line of the crew's blood leaving the steel to join what they had already lost — and then wiped the blade properly along his sleeve, edge away, two passes, before the saya took it. A gray smear of actuator fluid went with the blood: the evening's full forensic record, written in two inks. He felt for the edge with three fingers along the flat as the blade seated. No new fractures. The boss's katana he left exactly where it lay, in the hand that had earned it — you do not take a swordsman's sword, even when you have taken everything it was for. Cacophony had never left the holster. Five in the cylinder. He registered the count the way other people registered the time: unspent.

[Beat 41]
He looked at the dock floor. An arm, a forearm, a jaw rig, a wrist blade locked at half-extension, a compact auto gone cold where the plating man had dropped it, and one hand still wrapped around a katana. Most of a decade of installment payments, laid out on corrugated steel, going dark. The blood was already finding the drainage channels. The blood would stop. The payments wouldn't.

[Beat 42]
Kyle's hands were steady. They would shake in four minutes. He had learned to use the window.

[Beat 43]
He moved through the bodies. Methodical. The badly-done jaw man was face-down beside the drainage channel where the fourth cut had unmade his afternoon of cheap surgery, cheek against the corrugated steel, breath fogging in the runoff. Kyle crouched. He found the jaw rig where it had skidded, and the teeth the bad work had conscripted as mounting posts — two lying loose in the runoff, one still seated in the dead hardware and requiring grip and twist and a specific application of force that Kyle performed without expression, against the rig, not the man. He collected all three in his left hand, wet and slick and smelling of saliva and machine oil, and he did not think about what he was doing in terms that required examination.

[Beat 44]
*Her father's jaw. These three teeth. The math of a correspondence.*

[Beat 45]
He was still crouched at the drainage channel when he registered the breathing behind the ventilation unit. Quick. Shallow. Not panic — panic doesn't hold that long without breaking. This was something controlled. Something stubborn. He'd tracked it since the threshold. He'd known it was there. He'd worked around it with the same automatic precision with which he'd worked around the drainage channels — the girl was infrastructure, she was fixed, she was not a variable.

[Beat 46]
Her father's daughter.

[Beat 47]
He stood. He walked to the ventilation unit. He did not go behind it. He stood where she could see him — a tall, emaciated shape in the dock's bad light, carrying more of his own blood than anyone else's — and he waited. The breathing stopped. Then a lean, slow movement. A face. Twelve, maybe thirteen, with her father's jaw and her mother's eyes and an expression that had no name because it was composed entirely of things a child her age should not have had to feel simultaneously.

[Beat 48]
Kyle opened his hand.

[Beat 49]
"Present these to your father. I think he will appreciate the gesture."

[Beat 50]
The teeth fell into her cupped palms. She shrieked — high, thin, a sound that bounced off every hard surface in the dock and came back changed — but she did not drop them. Her fingers closed. She looked at her closed hands. She bowed, a full-body convulsion of terror and something that was not gratitude exactly but was shaped like it, and then she was gone, swallowed by the dark beyond the door.

[Beat 51]
Kyle watched the dark for three seconds.

[Beat 52]
*She didn't drop them. Note that. She didn't drop them.*

[Beat 53]
He cleaned the blade again anyway — oil, paper, the full sequence. The tremor started in his right hand, moved to his left, became briefly bilateral — the crash, the array's invoice, thousands of calories called in twenty seconds and the body now presenting the bill. His stomach was a closed fist. He needed protein, carbohydrate, something hot in a bowl, something that required no decisions. He needed to sit somewhere that smelled like something other than what this smelled like.

[Beat 54]
He walked back to the bike. The Gray Zone's night-architecture closed around him — the flickering overheads, the drip of recycled water from the tier above, the distant music from some bar that had no business being optimistic. The stairwell he cut through to reach the street smelled of cheap incense and gun oil, and a sandalwood stick burned down to ash on a ledge by the rail. The towers leaned in close down here. A second VTOL crossed the gap forty stories up, running lights amber, moving north. Some board director was still late to dinner. The rich flew. Everyone else got rained on. The city had run on that arrangement for a century and wasn't going to renegotiate tonight.

[Beat 55]
The bike was where he had left it. The plate was the one he'd ridden down on; he'd swap it before he rode home, two streets short of West Town, where the Tier read changed and the plate that worked in the Gray Zone was the wrong plate for the surveillance grid the corponations ran across the Wicker boundary. He started the bike. He rode the long way north toward Chen's. Eight minutes. He was hungry. He was tired. He was alive.

[Beat 56]
At a stoplight outside a bar with no name and a hood lamp the same tired orange as every other hood lamp, two operators were arguing the way operators argued, loud enough that the array pulled the thread off the open band without being asked. One of them had taken a job that paid in Φ up front and had no operator listed on the board, no broker, no shell that resolved to a face — *posted itself,* the man kept saying, *the thing posted itself, you understand what I'm telling you* — and the other one was laughing at him for spooking at a clean contract. Kyle filed the shape of it the way he filed running lights and bad light at Cermak and Loomis. A job from nowhere. He had heard the shape before, twice now, and had not yet decided it was a shape. The light changed. He rode on.

[Beat 57]
Chen's stall is two blocks south of the freight tier and one alley off the main, the warm orange glow of its hood lamp visible through the rain from a block away. He has smelled what comes out of her kitchen once — the smell that pushed through the camphor in the apartment six hours ago, fried oil and chili and pork bone — but he has not been to the stall itself. From a block out he can hear the wok come down on the burner ring, a flat bright note like a struck bell, twice for every order and never more, and the queue moving to the count of it. He walks the bike to it. The bike rolls easy at walking pace — front wheel true, bearings clean.

[Beat 58]
She is at the counter when he arrives. The apron is fresh; the hands are washed; the small scar at the base of her left thumb catches the lamp light at an angle Kyle catalogues without making the catalogue mean anything yet. The daughter is somewhere he cannot see. The husband is somewhere he cannot see either. The teeth, the array confirms by absence — no second delivery to make — have already arrived where they were going. The smell of the stall is what the apartment smelled like, transposed into a place where the smell is the work and not the wound.

[Beat 59]
She does not greet him. She sets a bowl down on the counter — pork bone broth, the long-simmered kind, chili oil already added the way she will remember he likes it for the next four years. He sits on the stool. The stool is the right height for him. She has not adjusted it; he has not adjusted it; the geometry simply is what it is the first time, and the first time is the way it will be from now on.

[Beat 60]
He eats. The broth is hot. He eats the whole bowl and does not speak. She does not refill it. She does not move. When he sets the chopsticks down she is still standing exactly where she was when she set the bowl, and she is looking at him the way she had looked at the chips she had pressed into his hand at half rate six hours ago — she has more she wants to give and no language for the giving, and she is now finding the language.

[Beat 61]
Mandarin first, low. *Xie xie.* The two syllables doing the work of an entire ledger. Then, more deliberately, in the tongue she has chosen to run this stall in, the one she gives the customers because the customers do not all know hers: "Free. Always. For you. Forever." Her hands are flat on the counter beside the bowl. Her thumb with the scar is the closest thing to him on the wood. She has just offered him the only thing she owns.

[Beat 62]
His Neuretics catalogues her offer with the precision it catalogues exits. *She means it. She is not negotiating. She does not understand that she is offering you a thing you cannot accept.*

[Beat 63]
Kyle reaches into his coat and sets a credit chip on the counter beside her hand. Standard rate, exact.

[Beat 64]
"I pay," he says. His voice is level. He has practiced level for a long time. "Every bowl. Standard rate. Every time."

[Beat 65]
She does not pick up the chip. He does not pick up the bowl. Four seconds. The wok ticks behind her, cooling. Somewhere back in the queue somebody shifts their weight.

[Beat 66]
She tries again. Slower this time, the way you try again with a man you have decided not to insult by giving up on the first refusal. "You took the contract at half. You did the work whole. The bowl is nothing. Forever is nothing."

[Beat 67]
*Forever is everything,* his Neuretics flags, in the catalogue. *Forever is the largest thing she has, and she is offering it because she does not yet know how large it is.*

[Beat 68]
Kyle does not move the chip.

"If I do not pay, I do not eat. If I do not eat here, I do not eat. I would rather pay you than not see you again. The chip stays."

[Beat 69]
The four seconds become five. Then her hand closes over the chip the way her daughter's hand had closed over the teeth — not refusing the gift, accepting that the gift is the form the man in front of her needs the gift to take. She bows. Not the convulsive bow of the apartment six hours ago. A smaller bow. The bow of a vendor accepting a customer's price. The kind of bow she will give him every night, roughly three hundred nights a year — barring the occasional distant contract, grievous injury, or recon — for the next four years and longer.

[Beat 70]
He stands. He bows back, smaller still, the bow of a man who has just successfully refused a kindness larger than he can carry. "Tomorrow," he says. "Same bowl. Same price."

[Beat 71]
"Same price," she says, and turns back to the wok.

[Beat 72]
He turns and walks the bike out of the stall's lamp circle. The rain has not let up and will not let up tonight. The towers lean in overhead, the gap between them full of the city's wet patient light. He is between contracts, full of soup he paid for, twenty minutes from his own bed. By Glooms standards, a good night. He takes it.

[Beat 73]
*Laugh or you'd cry. He laughed.*

[Beat 74]
Quietly, to himself, walking the bike up the slope toward Wicker. Not a sound anyone could hear. A single tight exhalation, the laugh of a man who has just seen the joke and decided the joke is funny anyway.

[Beat 75]
Someone had been watching the loading-dock work. Someone had stayed to watch the walk back. Someone had watched the bike pick-up and would watch the route home, partially, before the route home crossed into a tier where their telemetry stopped working. The watching was professional — the camera position chosen for the angle, the timing chosen for the moment a man's discipline relaxes after the work is done. Kyle felt the watching the way you feel a draft you cannot find the source of. On a railing along the slope a row of birdcages had been hung out in the rain, a dozen of them, each cage hooded with a square of black cloth, and the residents stepping past them did so without a glance, the way you step past a thing that has always been there. He did not turn his head. Turning his head would tell the watcher what kind of read his array had performed and he was not yet willing to give the watcher that data. The boy from the Grind, who had spent his childhood being measured by people whose names he was never told, had grown into a man who did the measuring back without announcing he was doing it.

[Beat 76]
He knew the shape of the report being drafted. He had been the subject of enough of them to recognize professional patience — not a freelancer doing recon, who would have left after the dock. An organization. Someone who stayed through the walk and the stall and the first bowl of soup because organizations built complete pictures, and the complete picture was what they had come for. Kyle had given them one: economy, one drawn blade, five seams, the count unspent. Rounds fired, zero. Fatalities, zero. The bill in ruined chrome would run to more than a funeral's, which was the precise point, and whoever was writing the report would understand it or they would not. He had not hidden the work. He rode north through the rain and let them write whatever they needed to write, because a man whose discipline could not survive being read had the wrong discipline.

[Beat 77]
The watcher filed all of this in a report Kyle would never read. The report would be reviewed two months later by Hua, the south-arm cell captain, who would close the file and pass it to her gang lieutenant Mira along with a second one labeled *Bucktown — first interview — fourteen-piece*, and the second file would lead to the third, and the third to the fourth, and Kyle did not know any of that yet, riding north through the rain toward Wicker because the noodles had been the right temperature and the chip had stayed on the counter and the bike was running clean and the world was still fucked but it was, this evening, fucked at a tempo he could ride.

[Beat 78]
Kyle rode home. Behind him, from an angle he had never located, the report was still being written. He had given it a clean last page: rounds fired, zero; the chip still on the counter. Reports that clean never stayed in one drawer.

## Part I: The Ride Back

[Beat 79]
The rain had moved past percussion into resignation by the time Kyle was riding north out of the Gray Zone. The Pulse line that ran the eastern strut threw its low-frequency hum up through the road surface — a sound below sound, the kind of vibration you stopped noticing after a year of riding the surface routes and never stopped feeling. It kept a rhythm under everything, too deep to hear and regular as breathing, and when a slug ran the line the whole platform off his left shoulder unloaded a quarter inch at once, every waiting spine on it dropping together and rising again. The bike took it without complaint. The bike never complained.

[Beat 80]
The towers leaned over him as he climbed out of the strut. From the bottom of the canyon you could not see the sky; the sky was a private resource at this depth, available only to the residents of the upper hundred floors and to the VTOL traffic that crossed between them. From the elevation Kyle was now at — sixteenth-floor surface road, the tier they had cut into the side of the structure when the original road grid drowned in 2174 and the city had decided to keep going — the sky was a slot. A vertical slot of slate-grey weather between two buildings whose names he had never learned. You only needed the names if you worked in them. He didn't.

[Beat 81]
He rode the long way. The short way would have taken him up the West Town freight ramp and dropped him at the Damen lift in twenty minutes. The long way ran him through three tier crossings he did not strictly need to make, through the Wicker boundary surveillance grid he made a point of crossing with different plates each time, through the kind of residential block where the windows above the alley had laundry strung between them and somebody was always cooking something that did not smell like ration paste. The first crossing was an Arcturus Civil Security gate now — the thing that had replaced police, scanning for biometrics instead of plates, which made his different-plates habit cosmetic. He kept it up anyway. The second crossing wore a fresh corponation flag he did not recognize over a substation whose mounting collars were pure Axiom Industrial, the bolt pattern that was everywhere because the company that made it had been dead for thirty years and the substrate did not care who flew the flag above it. Somewhere along the block the streetlights cycled in sequence, a soft capacitor sigh running pole to pole. The long way was forty-eight minutes. He had never once regretted them.

[Beat 82]
The long way had pockets like this — a corner under a freight-band antenna junction, between the second crossing and the residential block, where the city's coverage had gone thin because the corponation overhead had rotated three times since the cameras were last provisioned. The rain came down different here, falling through the synthesis corridor a half-territory east before it reached the street, and it carried the corridor with it: solvent and pear drops up front and under that something cold and mechanical, the smell of an engine that had never been warm. Two of the kids below the stoop had their faces tipped up to catch it on their tongues. A man on a stoop. Beside him, a kid who could have been twelve or could have been fourteen, the way kids in this tier tended to be ambiguous about it, with a handful of small packets in a belt-clip organizer. Blue-dot stamped, Gray Zone origin — the standard Noopharma singles that moved by the half-Φ at every school-adjacent corner from here to the Spine. Two younger ones stood below the stoop with the patient attention of customers who had no reason to be anywhere else. The transaction took six seconds. The man watched neither his hands nor the kids; he watched the freight band. The kids looked at what they'd bought, pocketed it, and walked away with the easy economy of people conducting entirely routine business.

Kyle rode through it. He filed it in the part of his array that did not ask what to do with it — the same section as the abandoned surveillance cameras, the same section as the corponation flag he didn't recognize, the same section as the thirty-seven other things the long way had shown him tonight that he was not, in any immediately actionable sense, responsible for. The residential block was a thousand feet ahead. Someone up there was still cooking.

[Beat 83]
There was a stretch of the long way, between the second crossing and the residential block, that he had never gotten the measure of. A block that took eleven steps when the geometry said nine. A low hum that arrived before the recyclers that should have been making it, nineteen hertz or thereabouts, the same note the Pulse threw up through the ferrocrete but coming from no direction he could name. The rain landed here in three separate pitches at once — the slack drum of a stretched tarp, the hard bright snap off a hardshell awning, and under both of them, slower, the long hollow note of it striking a behemoth's hull half a territory away. Some nights the light at that one corner ran a half-shade wrong, like a screen calibrated by someone who had never seen the color it was trying to be. He had ridden through it enough times to be sure he had ridden through it before, and some nights he was not sure at all. He noted it. He filed it. He did not slow down. The long way had a few of these, and they were not the kind of thing you investigated.

[Beat 84]
Tonight the work was: he had collected three teeth from a man whose face was already wrong, delivered them to a twelve-year-old who screamed and did not drop them, and watched a woman behind a noodle counter try to give him a thing she could not afford to give and could not afford not to give. The work was not the loading dock. The work was carrying all of that home without having to put any of it down.

[Beat 85]
He did not put it down. He carried it. He rode.

[Beat 86]
The bike found the Damen lift at 22:01. On the way in he caught a thread of open comm traffic bleeding off the freight band — somebody asking after Stash, somebody else saying Stash had the Spine route locked for the rest of the month and to take a number — the ambient chatter of a half-dozen freelancers working the same stretch of city Kyle worked, none of them his problem tonight.

A courier was already at the lift gate when he rolled up, one boot on the rail, her breath still running ahead of her. Sponsorship legs — the full rebuild, hips down, better hardware than anything else she was wearing — and the chromatophore panels in her calves were finishing an ad rotation her stride had triggered somewhere back down the strut: fifteen seconds of amber liquor pouring itself for an audience of rain. She watched the lift numbers and not her own legs, with the practiced neutrality of someone keeping count of a number much larger than eighteen floors. Under both their boots the Pulse came up through the gate-plate, deep and even, and when a slug passed the line the platform settled a quarter inch and lifted again, the courier's boot riding the rail down and up without her looking at it. Kyle gave her the nod the trade gives the trade. She returned it and went back to the numbers.

He took the lift up to the eighteenth-floor surface (The Pivot's tier — somebody had hand-painted PIVOT in matte black on the lift's interior at some point in the building's earlier life and the corponation maintenance crew had never quite gotten around to scrubbing it). The doors opened on rain that had been waiting for him at this elevation specifically, a heavier rain than the strut had been throwing, the kind that hit the lift's threshold in flat percussion before he had even rolled the bike out onto the deck.

[Beat 87]
He rolled the bike out anyway. He walked it the last hundred and thirty feet to the alley behind The Pivot. Habit. The bike could ride that distance under its own power and Kyle was the only one who would know he had walked it instead, but the habit was the habit, and the habit was that you arrived home the way you wanted the room to know you were arriving. Quietly. With your weight off the engine. Without telling anyone above the third floor that anyone had come back.

[Beat 88]
The Pivot was a converted insurance archive that had stopped being an insurance archive in 2189. The building had a flat brick face and a service alley that ran along its east side and a fire-escape lattice that was older than half the operating buildings in West Town and structurally sounder than most of them. The window on the second floor at the back of the alley belonged to apartment 2E. The light was on. The kind of soft yellow light that came from the workbench lamp Pixel kept aimed at her current project. Music inside, very faint through the building's old skin, something with strings.

[Beat 89]
He kicked the stand down. He locked the wheel hub. He covered the plate. He walked the bike through the back service door and into the freight elevator, and the elevator's gate had not finished clattering on the second floor when Pixel's door opened.

[Beat 90]
Her window looked down the alley. The workbench lamp was on, the bench faced the glass, and a man walking a matte-black motorcycle past her sightline at 22:40 was not a mystery she needed instruments for. He had stopped trying to sneak in around the third month.

[Beat 91]
"Get in here."

[Beat 92]
She had said it before she saw the back of his shirt — before he was properly off the elevator. Not the bike; the bike only told her he was home. Him. The stride coming down the hall a half-step short. The left arm carried a few degrees off his side. The turn toward her door taken with the whole body instead of the waist, the way a man turns when his lower back has stopped cooperating. Four years of calibrating his hardware had given her his baselines, and tonight he was off all of them.

[Beat 93]
He went in.

[Beat 94]
Apartment 2E was three rooms and a fire escape: a main room with the workbench under the window, a galley kitchen, a small bedroom, and a bathroom that had been retrofitted by someone who had not entirely thought through the plumbing run. The main room was where Pixel worked. The kitchen was where Pixel fed people, including Kyle, on the rare occasions she could trick him into being fed. Tonight the kitchen was where she did her field-medic work, because the kitchen table was the only flat surface in the apartment large enough to lay a man across, and the kitchen had the best light.

[Beat 95]
She pointed at the chair. He sat. She put on gloves. She turned the music down, not off — Pixel did not work in silence, Pixel worked with the music doing the thinking she did not need to do consciously — and she came around behind him.

[Beat 96]
"Coat off, shirt up."

[Beat 97]
He took the coat off and laid it across the next chair. The coat was wet. The kitchen floor did not care. He took the shirt off entirely because he had learned, over four years of these visits, that *shirt up* and *shirt off* produced the same result when Pixel was the one doing the work and the shirt off was thirty seconds less negotiation.

[Beat 98]
She let out one long breath through her nose. Not commentary. Inventory. "Three?"

[Beat 99]
"Two for sure. The third might be a graze, might be in there."

[Beat 100]
"Lie down."

[Beat 101]
He lay down on the table. The table was warm because Pixel had been working at it before he arrived; her current project was scattered along its far edge, a half-disassembled neural reader pulled apart in the specific organized disorder of someone who intended to return to it. The wood was old. Pre-2200 oak, oiled to a depth that nothing the building's atmosphere did to it could ruin. Kyle had bled on this table before. The table had not held the stains.

[Beat 102]
She got out the forceps.

[Beat 103]
The forceps were the long pair. She had four pairs of forceps; the long pair was the pair she used for Kyle specifically, because Kyle's musculature ran lean enough that the entry track on most rounds was deeper than a normal-bodied retrieval, and the long forceps reached. The short pair was for the regular clientele Kyle did not ask about. Kyle had stopped asking about Pixel's regular clientele years ago, around the time he had stopped asking why she lived across the hall from him. He liked having her there. He left it alone.

[Beat 104]
She started talking. This was always the way. Pixel did not work in silence on a person; she narrated, low, the running commentary doing the work of telling him she was there and the work was happening and the next ten minutes had a person at both ends of it.

[Beat 105]
"Upstairs bird's been making sounds again. Three this morning. Same minor key, three different intervals, I think it's learning the building's HVAC."

[Beat 106]
"Yeah."

[Beat 107]
"There's a new noodle stand opened on Damen. Five blocks west of Chen's. I went past it on Saturday. They have a poster up, full corponation logo, and they are calling themselves *Damen Authentic* in three languages, and I do not need to tell you that an authentic anything that has a corponation logo printed on the poster is not authentic in any of the three languages."

[Beat 108]
"No."

[Beat 109]
"I think they are trying to do to Chen what they did to the old man on Ashland."

[Beat 110]
"They will fail."

[Beat 111]
"I think they will, but they will hurt her some first. You should know."

[Beat 112]
"I do now."

[Beat 113]
He felt the first fragment come out. She had it. She held it up where he would not see it directly but where his peripheral vision could register that something had been removed, because she had learned years ago that he hated the moment more if he did not have a visual confirmation that the procedure had progressed. The fragment hit the small metal dish at the edge of the table with the small bright tick of metal on metal he had heard a dozen times at this table.

[Beat 114]
"There's a song I like," she said, sliding the forceps in for the second one. "I'll play it for you. After. Not during. During is yours."

[Beat 115]
"Mm."

[Beat 116]
He let the array route the pain. Not off — the facility had been clear that fully partitioning pain was a long-term injury — but down, attenuated, the channel volume dropped to a setting where it was information rather than emergency. The trick was old. The trick was the trick the facility had built him to do. He used it the way other men used coffee.

[Beat 117]
She got the second one.

[Beat 118]
"Damen's bird theory," she said, transitioning without seam to a topic she had been waiting to get to all evening, "is that the building's HVAC plays it specific tones at specific times of day depending on which apartments are running their kettles. The bird memorizes the tones. The bird then sings the tones back when the tones are not playing, which is, if you really think about it, the bird trying to negotiate with the building. The bird is trying to bring the kettle back."

[Beat 119]
"That is a theory."

[Beat 120]
"I think the bird is smarter than the building. I think the building's HVAC is not actually responding to the kettles, but the bird thinks it is, and the bird is doing field experiments. There are worse epistemological projects." She moved the lamp. "Hold still."

[Beat 121]
He held still. She found the third fragment. The third was the graze — half-buried, the depth ambiguous until she had it under the light. She took it out clean. The fragment hit the dish. Three.

[Beat 122]
"Done."

[Beat 123]
She set the forceps down. She removed the gloves. She put a strip of biotape over each entry, slowly, with the patience she saved for things that mattered — the project on her bench, the bird, Kyle's back — and then she stepped back and washed her hands at the sink for longer than the procedure had required.

[Beat 124]
He sat up.

[Beat 125]
She was at the counter when he turned around. She had a kettle going. She had been heating it the entire time, he realized, timed, without announcement, to be ready exactly when the procedure ended. She poured two cups. She handed him one. The handle was on the side without the chip.

[Beat 126]
"Drink it slow."

[Beat 127]
He drank it slow.

[Beat 128]
He left her apartment at 23:47. He did not feel the time pass. The kitchen had been warm and the tea had been hot and Pixel had played the song she had mentioned — a thing he did not recognize, sung in a language he did not speak — and she had not said anything for the last six minutes of the song because the song had been doing what it needed to do without help.

[Beat 129]
He crossed the hall to 2F. He unlocked the door. He set Silence on the rack inside the entryway and Cacophony next to it, the holster open, the cylinder still loaded with all five rounds he had not fired at the dock. The bird's-head grip caught the light from his apartment's one functional ceiling fixture and threw a small red glint off the crosshatch texture, the only color in the room besides the matte black of everything else Kyle had brought home in his life.

[Beat 130]
He did not turn the light off. He did not undress. He went into the bedroom and lay down on the bed in the clothes he had been wearing since the apartment with the camphor smell, and he reached for Silence on the rack across the room without standing up, because the rack was where Silence lived but the bed was where he lived, and he did not leave that gap open on nights like this one.

[Beat 131]
He brought Silence to his chest. He held the saya across his sternum. The blade was steel. It had no opinion about whose chest it lay across or why. He held it there anyway — through the camphor smell, through the daughter's hand closing around the teeth, through Mrs. Chen's offer of forever — and the cold and the weight stayed put when nothing else did. That was enough.

[Beat 132]
He sat up. He felt the three biotape strips on his back move with his shoulder blades. He felt the cut on his left forearm where the boss's follow-through had grazed him — a thin line, scabbed clean, no infection signal from the subdermal mesh. He felt the array come up from its overnight low-power state and find him in the room and start cataloguing things that did not need to be catalogued. He let it.

[Beat 133]
He stood. He put Silence back on the rack. He put a kettle on. He looked out the bedroom window at the alley below, which was full of the same rain it had been full of when he had come up the night before, the alley itself unchanged in any of the ways that mattered. On the transit ledge across the gap a dozen pigeons sat hunched and grey against the wet, and among them one bird was lacquer orange and white and black, jewel-bright and alone in the row, koi-marked the way nothing in the row had any business being. The bike was where he had left it. The bird from upstairs was, as Pixel had reported, singing — a low minor-key thing Kyle had now heard with his own ears for the first time, three different intervals, three different tries at coaxing something out of the building's HVAC. The bird's epistemological project was ongoing. Kyle could respect that.

[Beat 134]
The kettle clicked. He poured. He held the cup in both hands and stood in his kitchen and did the thing he did most mornings, which was sit with the awareness that he was alive and uninjured beyond the catalogued, and that the day in front of him was going to be its own day, and that the work he did would not save the world and was not trying to, and it was still his to do.

[Beat 135]
He drank the tea.

[Beat 136]
The phone in his coat — left on the chair across the kitchen — chimed once. He set the cup down. He walked over and read the message. Sable's pipeline. A new contract had landed. The terms read clean. The pay read fair. On the surface it was a normal job, the kind the pipeline had been delivering for two months without giving him a reason to question it.

[Beat 137]
The bird upstairs sang again. The HVAC did not answer. The bird tried a different interval.

[Beat 138]
Kyle filed the contract for later. He finished the tea. He went to put on a clean shirt.

[Beat 139]
The day was going to be its own day. He had no idea, walking across his apartment to the closet, what the day was going to be. He did not know that he would be at Mrs. Chen's by 19:30 that evening for the second bowl of his usual two. He did not know that a hooded woman would be at the counter three stools down, watching him pretend not to watch her, until she tilted her head *come*. He did not know that the contract on his phone had been routed to him for reasons the contract's own metadata could not legibly describe, by a pipeline he trusted because the pipeline had not yet given him a reason not to. He did not know that *not yet* was about to become *eleven hours ago*, in a shielded room he had not yet agreed to walk into.

[Beat 140]
He did not know any of that. He buttoned the shirt. He put the cup in the sink. He locked Silence into its saya and Cacophony into its holster. He shrugged the coat on, the one that had been wet last night and was now dry from sitting against the radiator, and walked out of his apartment into the day the day had become. Down on the corner where the alley met the freight track a brown dog crossed the street, unhurried, at the same minute it crossed it every day, the same line it had walked for years, gone around the corner before the light at the crossing had decided anything about it.

[Beat 141]
Pixel's door was closed. The hallway smelled the way hallways smelled. The bird upstairs was still working on the kettle.

[Beat 142]
He took the long way.

[Beat 143]
The hinge on Kyle's closet door had been misaligned for four months. He knew it the way he knew the precise weight of Cacophony on his left hip and the specific sound the landing made three steps from the top. Outside, the streetlights along the block cycled down at the same hour they always did, each one letting go a thin capacitor sigh before it dimmed, the sound passing up the building and out again without anyone on the floor lifting their head. The door dragged at the bottom when you opened it past forty-five degrees and caught again when you closed it. He had adjusted his use of the closet accordingly.

Pixel was across the hall with her tool bag before he had finished his first coffee.

She didn't knock as much as announce herself — one knuckle, the door already opening. She had a flathead and a Phillips and a small plastic level she set on the floor beside the closet before she'd said anything past *morning.*

[Beat 144]
Kyle watched her from the kitchen. She wore the flannel she slept in and her hair was still compressed from the pillow. Her feet were bare on his concrete floor.

"Left three nights ago," he said.

She looked up from the level. "What?" she asked.

"The hinge. Started dragging three months ago, not four."

Pixel looked back at the door. "Three months is still too long," she said.

[Beat 145]
She had the screws out in under two minutes. The hinge plate had worked loose at one anchor, and she re-drilled at a sharper angle — the bit catching on old concrete, a dry smell of ground chalk — then pressed a plug of something gray into the cavity with her thumb before she drove the screw home. The anchor was an old Axiom collar, the company thirty years dead and its hardware still holding half the doors in the district. The whole operation took eleven minutes. She tested the door twice — full open, full close, no drag — and seemed satisfied in the way a calibration satisfies: precisely and without ceremony.

When she packed the tools back into the bag, she didn't move toward the door right away.

[Beat 146]
"You eat?" she asked.

"Coffee."

She made a short sound through her nose. She sat on the arm of the couch and held the tool bag in her lap. Kyle stayed in the kitchen doorway.

"The door," she said, "has been dragging since before the Depot job. The one that came in on a Friday and left the smell of burnt insulation in your jacket for three days after."

Kyle said nothing.

"I know because I heard you going in and out at night and the drag was there when you left and not when you came back. Different handling when your hands are full."

He looked at her. He became aware that he was holding his breath and let it go. She looked back. She didn't say what the reading was. She didn't have to.

[Beat 147]
She stood and went across the hall and closed her own door without any particular hurry.

Kyle stayed in the kitchen doorway a moment longer. He wasn't sure when it had shifted from her knowing him to her keeping track of him.

He went and opened the closet. Full open. Full close. No drag. He put his hand on the hinge plate for a moment, then let it go.

## A Restless Mind

[Beat 148]
"Because you are the one this concerns," she said. "The rest tomorrow. In the room. I cannot say more here."

[Beat 149]
He laughed. Quietly. One short exhalation through the nose, the kind that would not have been a laugh in any room but this hallway. The evening had been: agreeing to walk into a shielded room with a woman whose face he had not seen and whose name he did not know, then walking home to find a cup of tea waiting in his neighbor's doorway. The contrast was funny. Nobody else needed to know why.

[Beat 150]
The hood inclined. Not a nod. An acknowledgment. Then she stepped past him into the dark of the block without unlocking the building behind her. The building was where Kyle was going tomorrow, and Kyle was going to go.

[Beat 151]
The noodles were getting cold. Kyle was watching a woman pretend not to watch him.

[Beat 152]
Not loud. Not for Mrs. Chen. For him, across six feet of rain. *"Kyle."* Just the name. Level, unhurried, the tongue she had chosen to say it in the same tongue she used through the cracked terminal at the parts shop three blocks east — the modulated register, the older one, the voice he had been getting contracts in for two months and had not yet built a face for.

[Beat 153]
"You're back early," she said.

[Beat 154]
He set the chopsticks down across the bowl. He did not stand. He did not move his hands away from the counter. The left hand was already where Cacophony would be in eight-tenths of a second; the right hand was already where Silence's draw would begin in three-tenths. He let her see him not move and let the not-moving be the answer to the question she had not asked yet.

[Beat 155]
*Nobody stands still in the rain unless the rain doesn't bother them or the job requires it.*

[Beat 156]
She sipped her tea. He sipped his. The mist outside was settling on the alley wall the way mist settles when the air below is colder than the air above. Kyle had stopped checking the forecast around the same week he had stopped trying to keep up with Pixel's tea cabinet. *Hm.* He drank. Something in him eased at the first sip. Something else went watchful at the second.

[Beat 157]
He finished the tea. He handed her back the cup, handle-first, the way she handed him his when the convention ran the other direction. She took it. She bowed, a small bow, the bow of a friend acknowledging the closing of a transaction that had no exchange in it. He bowed back, smaller.

[Beat 158]
She tilted her head — a fraction, a direction, nothing more — and Kyle, who now knew exactly who she was and was therefore in a worse strategic position than if he had not known, did the calculation a freelancer does when his fixer of two months shows up at his stall in person and says only his name. *If she wanted me dead, the angle from the doorway would already be open. If she wanted to ambush me, she would not have spoken. She is here for something the cracked terminal cannot carry. She is here because the room she lives in is not safe.* Kyle's chair scraped back. He set the chip on the counter and said it the way he said it every night, low and level: *Standard rate.* Mrs. Chen's hand covered the chip without looking down, which was how she always took money from him when she thought he was about to do something she didn't approve of and had decided not to say so.

[Beat 159]
Pixel did not ask what was funny. Pixel never asked what was funny. The not-asking was the punchline.

[Beat 160]
She did not ask where he had been. He did not tell her about the woman in the hood, or the three blocks east-northeast, or the shielded room he had said yes to without yet knowing whose room it was. He did not tell her about the agreement — same time tomorrow, somewhere with a cage in it. He did not tell her about the way the agreement had landed in his chest at exactly the angle the agreement had been designed to land, and how he had said yes anyway, and how easily he had said yes.

[Beat 161]
"Bird's still out," she said. She meant the upstairs neighbor's bird, which was a recurring data point in Pixel's running commentary on the building and which Kyle had never actually heard but which he understood to be a presence in Pixel's life the way the rain was a presence in the city.

"Thanks," he said.

[Beat 162]
Kyle stood at the door for a count of ten. The brass-gasketed lock he could see through the window beside the threshold was the tell he had assumed it would be. He did not test the door. He turned. He walked back the way they had come — east-northeast in reverse, through the recycler vent's hot industrial breath and past the third-block security drone on its standard sweep and past the awnings that had not stopped dripping in the eleven minutes the walk had taken in the other direction and the further eleven it would take him in this one. Somewhere up in the ductwork between two buildings an elevator cable thrummed against the wall it ran behind, a long steel note rising and cutting off as the car reached its floor. He did not run the route past the array. He let it sit. He walked it.

[Beat 163]
He followed the woman into the rain. His left hand stayed where Cacophony would be. His right hand stayed where Silence's draw would begin. He did not stop tracking her three-quarter profile for the entire walk. She was already moving, deeper into the walkway's dark, and he fell in six feet behind her — far enough to read her, close enough that she knew he had chosen to come. The katana's weight shifted against his hip with each step, the way it always did on wet ground: a small correction, muscle memory now, the balance Seo had drilled into him until it stopped requiring effort. The blade itself he had no story for. He had stopped looking for one. Kyle did not know who she was. He knew the shape of her competence the way you know a locked door by the quality of the lock, and the lock was very good, and she had been standing in the rain for eleven minutes waiting for him specifically, and those two facts together were either a contract or a problem. In his experience they were usually both.

[Beat 164]
The bike was where he had left it, two blocks south of the noodle stall, the plate still the one he had ridden down on, the cover still beaded with rain. He started it. He rode the long way home through tier crossings he did not strictly need to make, because the long way home was where Kyle did most of his thinking, and tonight was a night he had something to think about. The crossing at the old Axiom substation read his plate without slowing him — the arch's hum dropped a half-step as he passed under it, the way it always did when it found him, and the air inside the scan went sharp with ozone and the leftover nervousness of everyone who had crossed before him — the substation was dead corp now, the company gone a decade, but the mounting collars still held somebody else's cameras, and the new flag over the gate was Arcturus blue, which meant the block had changed hands again since the last time he'd ridden it and nobody had bothered to tell the freelancers. Stash would know who paid the toll now. Stash always knew. He did not know who she was. He knew he had said yes. He knew tomorrow was a shielded room. He knew the discipline would show up because the discipline always showed up. The rest would have to be tomorrow's problem.

[Beat 165]
He kicked the stand down. He locked the wheel hub. He covered the plate. He walked the bike's stillness into the elevator and the elevator into the second floor and the second floor into the hallway. The stairwell wall carried the cable's thrum up beside him the whole climb, the building's pulse running parallel to his own, and the light switch on the landing was worn to bare brass and still warm from the last hand that had thumbed it. His step hit the landing across from 2F at the angle he had not announced and Pixel's door opened anyway.

[Beat 166]
He read the wording. *Shielded* did most of the work. The kind of shielding she meant was the kind that cost money she did not casually spend. The hum in his chest, the low electromagnetic signature that lived in the implant's background like a second heartbeat, was suddenly something he was aware of for the first time in years. He had not known it was there because there had been no reason to notice it. There was, now, a reason.

[Beat 167]
"Tomorrow's going to be its own day," he said, eventually.

[Beat 168]
The woman stepped under the awning.
