The hood came back an inch — not all the way, just enough that the streetlight caught the shape of a face and the dark of red hair beneath the rain hood, and Kyle catalogued *attractive* the way the array catalogues anything: as a fact registered without commentary. Tall. Late thirties. Bone structure that had cost her nothing because she had been born with it. He had not seen her face before tonight and he was not entirely sure he was supposed to be seeing it now.

[Beat 169]
He held her gaze through the hood for another beat. He could leave. He could turn around and ride home and pretend the noodle stall had been a noodle stall. He could pretend three blocks east-northeast had been a walk. He could pretend that *Because you are the one this concerns* had not been the sentence it had been, the one that had been sitting in his chest all evening. He could pretend the shielded room would empty without him in it.

[Beat 170]
"Sang twice this evening. New piece. I think it learned from the radio."

*Mm.* He turned the cup. The bird was part of the building's texture, the way Pixel's tea cabinet was — something he'd stopped trying to catalog.

"Yeah?" The word came back from somewhere. He meant it.

[Beat 171]
Eleven minutes. Same foot position. The rain came down at the angle the atmospheric processors preferred — steep, efficient, redirected from somewhere that mattered — and it came off her hood in a clean sheet that said the coat was quality, the kind of quality that didn't announce itself. No chrome. No augmentation profile his implant could resolve. His Neuretics kept reaching for a signal and returning empty.

[Beat 172]
"Tomorrow," she said. "This address, this time, the room behind this door. Shielded. Bring nothing electronic you can leave behind. Your implant comes with you because your implant cannot be detached, but anything else you carry stays in your apartment. The conversation we are going to have cannot happen in a room that can hear it."

[Beat 173]
She watched him over the rim of her cup. Not with the focus she put on a fragment in his shoulder; with the looser focus she used when she was working on the larger question of whether he was, on a given night, all right. He was, on this night, all right. She read it. She did not need to confirm it. Her shoulders settled by a quarter inch and she sipped her tea.

[Beat 174]
She was gone in three steps. The Gray Zone's bad rain swallowed the tan coat the way the Gray Zone swallowed most things at this hour.

[Beat 175]
"Why me?" he said. Not the question he wanted to ask, but the question the discipline asked for him. She let it stand in the rain.

[Beat 176]
The relay station resolved out of the dark at the end of the block. Wartime construction: the brutalist vocabulary of a city that had once believed in infrastructure as ideology, poured concrete and no ornamentation and a deactivated antenna on the roof that still served its purpose as a story. A thin line of cold green pulsed along the baseboard seam where the wall met the walkway, brightening and sliding a hand's width to the left, then going still — the walls were dry, at least. *It is a relay station. It is empty. There is nothing here worth stopping for.* The kind of building that became invisible through its own explanation. She stopped at the door. She did not produce a key. She did not unlock anything. She turned, instead, with the same composed efficiency she had walked the route with, and looked at him from inside the hood with eyes he could not quite read in the bad light.

[Beat 177]
"They usually are."

[Beat 178]
Pixel did not ask. That was the shape of the convention they had built over four years across this hallway: she did not ask, and he did not tell, and they didn't ask, and they didn't tell, and it worked.

[Beat 179]
He was not going to pretend any of those things. The discipline was the discipline. It showed up.

[Beat 180]
The bike's headlight crossed the alley wall at 22:14 and Kyle killed the engine before he turned in, coasting the last forty feet on momentum because the engine cutting at the corner had been Seo's lesson and Kyle had not yet found a reason not to do everything Seo had taught him to do. The rain had softened to mist by the time he got to West Town. The towers leaned in the way they did this far up; the canyon between The Pivot and the residential block across from it was just wide enough for a single VTOL to thread through if a VTOL had any business being this far down, which they didn't. The space above the alley was dark. The light came up from the windows, not down from the sky. The last stretch before the building always ran a little long — nine strides on the map, eleven under his wheels, the streetlamp at the corner burning a half-shade too cold for the bulb it claimed to be — and the ferrocrete carried that low hum up through the frame before the building's own noise arrived to explain it. He had stopped wondering about that block a long time ago. He let it go.

[Beat 181]
The match landed in his head before he had time to refuse it. Voice plus face. *Sable.*

[Beat 182]
They stood in their doorways across the hall and drank. The hallway smelled faintly of someone's microwave dinner two doors down and faintly of Pixel's apartment behind her, which always smelled like solder flux and bergamot in proportions that should not have worked and somehow did. Down at the floor, a thin green line ran the length of the baseboard between his door and hers, pulsing once and travelling a few inches before it settled, the way it did most nights when the building went quiet enough to notice. The cup in Kyle's hand was too hot to drink fast, which suited him fine.

[Beat 183]
He had no idea who she was. He had a sense of the way she walked, the way she had timed the drone interval, the way the coat had not soaked through. He had her voice from twelve seconds at the noodle stall — too few words to identify anyone he had ever spoken to before. The hood was the kind of hood that did not come off accidentally. He filed the hood without prying at it.

[Beat 184]
The recycler vent exhaled hot industrial breath across the walkway — paper-dry and chemical-sweet, the smell of the Gray Zone processing its own refuse — and she walked through it without breaking pace. Kyle held his breath for two steps on instinct. Old facility reflex. The security drone hung at the third-block junction, its sensor array cycling through its standard sweep in the standard interval. It did not flag them. She had timed the approach or she had known the interval, and either answer said the same thing about how long she had been thinking about this particular walk. He filed that. He filed everything. His Neuretics ran hot in the rain, the lattice's temperature regulation working harder than it should have been, a low warmth behind his left ear that he had learned to read the way a pilot reads engine noise — not yet a problem, a thing to monitor.

[Beat 185]
"Goodnight, Pixel."

[Beat 186]
Behind him, Mrs. Chen stirred the broth with a long-handled spoon that had lost its lacquer in three places and been re-gripped with electrical tape, the same spoon she had used for the four years he had been eating at this stall, and she said one word into the steam. *Húlijīng.* Low. Mandarin. Not for him. She said it the way a woman who has seen things names them — with the flat certainty of a diagnosis, not a warning. Kyle set his chopsticks down across the bowl. He had heard Mrs. Chen mutter at the rain, at the Circuit boys who walked too close to her signage, at the atmospheric processors when they made the broth steam wrong. He had never heard her name a person.

[Beat 187]
She was in a t-shirt over leggings, no shoes, the hallway's bad amber light catching the rim of two cups she was holding. One in each hand. Not forceps tonight. Cups. The steam was rising off both of them and Kyle could smell something herbal and lemon-bitter and could not name the specific plant because Pixel's tea cabinet was an ongoing research project and Kyle had stopped trying to keep up with the inventory roughly the third month he had lived across the hall from her.

[Beat 188]
He had not been gone long enough to be either early or late. *Early* was the kind of thing Pixel said when she had been listening for his step and the step had arrived and she had to put words on the relief without putting words on the relief. He took the cup she held out. It was hot. The handle was the side without the chip; she had given him the better cup, which was her version of saying something she did not intend to say aloud.

[Beat 189]
"Goodnight, samurai."

[Beat 190]
She closed her door. He unlocked his. He sat on the edge of the bed in the dark, the tea still warm in his chest, and listened for the bird he had never heard. He drank a glass of water from the tap before he settled, and it had the faint clean nothing of good filtration, the taste of a bill paid on time. The building was quiet. He listened until the quiet was the answer, then let it be.

[Beat 191]
The mochi cart first. Closed, a chain through the handles, the vendor's stool folded against the wheel. The rain had filled a small depression in the cart's roof and it spilled in a thin line off one corner, perfectly continuous, the kind of detail the atmospheric processors produced without intending to. Kyle's implant catalogued it and moved on. He tracked her coat. The way the hem swung. The fact that she had not once adjusted her stride for the puddles, which meant she knew the route or had walked it enough times that her feet had learned it separately from her attention. *Preparation. Weeks, maybe.*

[Beat 192]
*She has never come in person before. The shape of an ambush.*

[Beat 193]
"Tomorrow," he said.

## Inside the Cage

[Beat 194]
Below the relay station, 0312. He found the equipment rack by feel — the old kind, bolted to the floor, steel, the bolts themselves worn smooth from decades of hands checking them. He sat on its edge. Somewhere behind the panel the volt rats shifted in a cable run, a sound like dry rice poured slowly, and then settled. He did not reach for the katana. He sat with his hands on his knees and waited for whatever came next. Then he turned north.

[Beat 195]
She stepped back and let him through. The workbench was cleared — she had been working on something before she slept, the components pushed to one side in the specific organized disorder of someone who intended to return to them — and she pulled the stool from under the bench and pointed at it, which was all the instruction he needed.

He sat. She put on gloves. She did not turn the music back on.

The silence in the room was the working kind, which was its own form of fullness, and she moved into it with the focus she always had when she was holding a tool near something that mattered. Her quiet running commentary dropped even lower than usual, almost inaudible, almost not for him at all: the fragment's depth, the tissue response, the lateral array's drift she found when she ran the Tier 2 check and corrected without remarking on when or how it had happened.

Twenty-eight minutes. He sat still and let her.

The salvaged mil-spec signal relay was in his coat pocket — the part her bench had been improvising around for a month — and he set it on the bench's cleared edge when she finished. She looked at it for one second — the aperture of her attention tightening the way it did when something was exactly right — and said nothing. He stood.

[Beat 196]
The busker was at the far end on an overturned crate, the instrument across his knees — sanxian, possibly, or something older than the word he had for it, three strings and a long neck and a body that had been repaired at the shoulder joint with what looked like epoxy and conviction. The piece was slow. It was in a key that did not have a name in any register his implant carried, which meant it was older than the implant's references or simply outside them, and the sound moved through the courtyard and did not echo the way it should have, the collapsed walls doing something to the acoustics that produced instead a kind of presence, the music occupying the space rather than bouncing off it. Kyle stopped walking.

[Beat 197]
The two men were at the fourth stall. One had a fish cart. The other wanted it. Kyle read the situation in the time it took to take two steps: the man with the cart had a split lip already bleeding into his beard, the other was holding a length of rebar at his side in the loose way of someone who had picked it up and not yet decided what for. The aunt at the neighboring stall was yelling in Mandarin — the specific register of a woman who has seen this particular fight before and is yelling not because she thinks it will stop anything but because silence would mean she had accepted it. The rain came down and took the blood — fish blood from the overturned bucket at the cart's base, man blood from the lip — into the gutter at the same rate, indifferent to the distinction. Kyle's stride did not change. Sable's didn't either. Her right hand was in the tan coat pocket and had been since they left the relay station, and it was not a weapon she was holding.

The hood came down.

[Beat 198]
The stairs at The Pivot had a particular sound at 0356 — the third step from the top, the one with the loose riser that had been loose since the building changed hands in 2198 and would be loose until something structural made it a different problem. Kyle's weight on it produced the same sound it always produced, which was apparently enough. The door to 2E opened before he reached the landing.

[Beat 199]
Pixel in pajamas. Bare feet on the hall's cold composite. The music inside had been running — he could hear it cut when she heard the stair — and her hair was loose and there was a crease on her left cheek from the pillow and she took one look at the blood soaking the collar of his shirt and said: "Again."

[Beat 200]
*So. That.* He kept his hands where they were, flat on the table, and noted that he had to decide to keep them there.

[Beat 201]
He considered what it meant to agree. The hum had been there for eleven years and he had not known it was there, which meant it had been with him in every contract, every room, every choice he had believed was his. Agreeing to help locate the thing was not a neutral act. He knew that. He sat with it for three seconds, which was longer than he usually needed. "All right," he said.

[Beat 202]
She watched him have it. The implants did not adjust again. Then: 'If it is not looking through your eyes, it is interpreting your senses.' The voice was the same. Level, unhurried, the voice that had given him eleven operational briefings in the two months since she picked him up. 'Either way — the moment you recognize me, it knows we are meeting. So I needed you to read me as a stranger.' A pause that was not hesitation. 'I needed you to choose to follow someone you did not know.' The hum at the base of his sternum picked that moment to clarify, the way a word does when you finally hear how it is spelled, and he held the next breath a half-second longer than the one before it. Kyle looked at the floor. The concrete was old, marked with the ghost-outlines of equipment that had stood here for decades and been removed. Eleven years of the harmonic catalogued as ambient, as background, as *self.* Not self. His left hand moved on the table. He put it back. The question was whether it had been keeping him running or simply watching him run, and he didn't know, and the not-knowing was something he should have asked about a long time ago. 'I do not like,' Sable said, 'being some non-human's puppet.' Flat. The way she said *the risk premium is already factored in.* A thing stated, not performed. She let it sit. The correction came after it, filed with the same flatness, the discipline of a woman who audited her own conclusions before anyone else could get to them: 'I do not know what it is. I know what it has been doing. Those are not the same thing, and I am not going to pretend they are.' Then: 'I can triangulate its physical position. Three locations. Three readings of surveillance traffic against your implants. I have placed the nodes. I need you outside this room behaving exactly as you behave.' Kyle looked up. The ocular implants were still. The aperture locked, which he now understood meant she had moved into a register where every word cost something. 'I need you,' she said, 'to not change.'

[Beat 203]
The song ended the way things end when they have been played correctly — not stopping, arriving. Kyle reached into his coat pocket and produced what was there: forty credits, the same number he had left on Mrs. Chen's counter, which was not a decision he had made consciously and which he did not examine now. He set them on the crate beside the busker. The busker did not look up. Kyle turned. Sable was watching him with the aperture locked, the expression she had when the variables had resolved into something she had not expected and was deciding whether to name it. She did not name it. "That's three," she said, very quietly, and the ocular implants made their small sound and she looked away, at something only the node showed her, at coordinates that were now coordinates rather than a search. Kyle walked, and she walked beside him, and neither of them said anything about the garden or the forty credits or the key the piece had been in, and the busker behind them began, without pause, to play something else.

[Beat 204]
He walked across the hall. Picked Silence up from the wall, the familiar weight settling into his grip, and carried it into 2F and set it on the rack. The hum was at full presence in his chest. The relay was already gone from his pocket, which meant it was on her bench, which meant it was already being understood. Kyle sat on the bed's edge in the dark and did not sleep.

[Beat 205]
Sable took two more steps before she understood he was not beside her. He heard the small sound the ocular implants made when they adjusted — the aperture tightening as she looked at him, read him, recalculated. He was not looking at her. He was looking at the busker, who was not looking at either of them, whose attention was entirely on the middle distance above the strings in the way of a person for whom the playing and the listening had become the same act. The hum in Kyle's sternum was there. It was always there now — he would always know it was there now — but the music was also there, and for the length of one song they occupied the same space without canceling each other. Sable's hand moved in the coat pocket. The node reading. The rain came down.

[Beat 206]
The couple was on the third stoop from the corner. A thermal cloth over the bowl, the iridescent kind that kept heat in but cost nothing, the seams repaired twice at least. The man had noodles on his chin and the woman wiped it with a folded napkin — not looking at him, looking at the middle distance the way you look when a thing is so practiced it has stopped requiring attention — and the man kept eating. No thank you. The thank you had been said ten thousand times and was now in the gesture itself, in the way he tilted his chin slightly to meet the napkin rather than pulling away from it. He filed it himself, the old way, without asking his Neuretics to help. Sable walked at his left, her right hand still in the coat pocket, the node reading, and she did not look at the couple.

[Beat 207]
Not a pause. Not the stillness of a person checking their footing or reading a sign. The stillness of a person who has seen something in data and needs three seconds to understand what they are seeing. Kyle did not look at her. He looked at the thermal cloth on the couple's bowl shift as the man reached for the napkin himself this time, belatedly, too late to be the gesture it had been when she did it. He counted the seconds by the flicker of the streetlight, which was reliable in its unreliability. One. Two. Three. Then she was walking again, the same pace, the coat pocket still, the ocular implants making their small sound as they adjusted to the distance ahead. She said nothing. He did not ask. The block continued behind them, and the block did not know that anything had shifted, and Kyle walked and catalogued and felt the hum sharpen again at the base of his sternum, and whatever Sable had read in the node she was carrying now like a stone she had decided not to put down.

[Beat 208]
The brass gasket seated with a sound like a breath held and then released. Kyle stood in the dark and let the silence locate itself in his body. It was in his sternum. Below his sternum. A place he had stopped attending to the way you stop attending to the sound of your own blood moving — it was always there until it was not, and now it was not, and the absence had a shape. Eleven years. He had been sixteen when they finished the installation and he had not been quiet inside since.

[Beat 209]
He did not answer. He unslung Silence from his back and leaned it against the wall outside her door, careful, the friction sheath seated flush against the baseboard, and straightened without looking at her. She did not look at it. The convention did not require acknowledgment to function.

Sable went still.

[Beat 210]
She stopped at the courtyard's western edge where the collapsed buildings gave way to open street. The rain had not changed. It would not change until the processors decided it had served its purpose, and it had not served its purpose yet. She turned — not fully, not the full rotation of a person turning to face someone, but the partial turn of a person who has decided to close a file while still holding it. The ocular implants made their sound. "I'll contact you when the timing is operational," she said. The same voice that had given him eleven briefings. Flat, clean, the risk premium already factored in. "We go together. My read, my call." Not a question. Not a request. The way she said *the deliverable is non-negotiable* — a thing stated so that the stating of it completed the transaction. Then: "Thank you." Two words. She did not touch him. She turned west and walked, and the tan coat went with her into the rain, and Kyle stood at the courtyard's edge and watched her go for three seconds.

[Beat 211]
The man with the rebar did not use it. The man with the cart did not run. They stood in the rain and ran out of whatever had been sustaining the fight — not resolution, not decision, just the body's unwillingness to continue — and the man with the cart took the cart, and the man with the rebar sat down on the curb in the rain and put the rebar across his knees and looked at nothing. The aunt said one more thing, sharp and final, the period at the end of a sentence she had been writing for years, and went back to her stall. The market continued. Kyle and Sable continued. Somewhere in Sable's coat pocket the node was reading, and whatever it was reading was the business of something that had been living in Kyle's chest for eleven years. Kyle walked and did not change. That was the agreement.

*There it is. Still there.*

[Beat 212]
Six blocks. The wet market was still running because the wet market always ran — it did not close for rain, did not close for cold, had not closed for the three-day curfew two years ago, had simply moved two stalls deeper under the corrugated overhang and continued. The fish smell reached him at the edge of it. Then yeast and antiseptic, thin and sweetish, through the cracked door of a splice shop that had not bothered with a sign. Then the hum was back, the same hum, and he registered it now the way you register a footstep behind you: something present that had not announced itself.

[Beat 213]
Ten blocks east the courtyard opened between two buildings that had collapsed toward each other and stopped, the way exhausted things stop — not falling, not standing, somewhere between. Someone had run cables across the gap and strung lights, the small kind, and the rain moved through them without caring. The ferrocrete planters were salvaged, four of them, mismatched in height and diameter and arranged with the logic of someone who had thought about where the light would be in the morning. Chrysanthemums. Real ones. White and rust and one the color of old brass, the petals holding the rain's weight the way petals do when they have been tended — not breaking, adjusting. Kyle's Neuretics catalogued the planters' material composition and moved on. He did not move on. He set his hand flat against the nearest one and found the ferrocement still warm under his palm an hour after the light had gone.

[Beat 214]
The hum came back as he passed the last planter — the brass-colored chrysanthemum, the one holding the rain's weight without breaking — settled into his sternum with the particular quality of something returning to its place. He noticed it the way you notice a room has gone cold — all at once, and late. The street opened north toward The Pivot. The residential block came up gradually — not a threshold but a gradation, the market giving way to poured concrete faces and the narrower dark of streets that had not been designed for anything except the people who lived on them. The hum adjusted with it. Smaller, he thought, and then corrected himself — not smaller. *More precise.* As if the reduced interference gave it room to sharpen.

[Beat 215]
Red hair. Loose braid, one strand against her cheekbone that had never stayed. The ocular implants — black, machined, the aperture rings that made a small sound when she shifted focus, the sound he had stopped noticing through two months of cracked-terminal audio and had just now, in this room, in this silence, identified as *her* — adjusted once as she looked at him. He had talked to this voice every Tuesday and Friday for two months. He had built a contract file, a behavioral profile, a trust index. He had built an inch of a face under streetlight, and the rest arrived now and fit itself over the partial like a key into a lock she had already shown him. He did not move. The cage held. His hands stayed open on his knees, and the muscles across his shoulders did the slow burning work of staying open, and he let them.

[Beat 216]
He memorized the coordinates from Sable's terminal and did not write them down. Nothing he put on a device would stay private, and the discipline had spent twenty years training his memory for exactly this kind of work. He walked out of the cage with them intact.

The hum came back into his chest as he crossed the threshold — twenty years of company, still arriving as an intrusion. It sat heavier than it had when he entered: a low extra weight, like a coat that had taken on water. He noticed the difference. Tonight, for the first time, he was treating every increment of it as data rather than weather.

[Beat 217]
"Dawn," she said, at the edge of the cage block. "The substation. We go together — my read, my call." The implants made their sound. Her hand came up halfway — the start of some gesture that had no place in a briefing — and she put it back at her side before it finished. "Thank you," she said. Two words. She turned west and walked, the tan coat going with her into the rain, and Kyle stood and watched her go for three seconds.

[Beat 218]
Dawn came in at the eastern horizon as a slow gray bruise above the tower line, and Sable was three steps ahead of him on the wet pavement, hood up, exactly where dawn had said she would be. She did not look back. They walked. The wind had come around to the north overnight, and one of the glass towers two blocks west was singing its single low note over the rooftops, the way it did when the air hit it right; the floors that caught the note rented for more than the floors that didn't, and the people who paid for them were already awake up there with their lights on.

Kyle's hand passed over Silence's saya before they set off — a check, not a draw. The katana rode across his back the way it always did, the weight he'd stopped noticing as weight. Cacophony was in the shoulder rig under his jacket, five chambers loaded, the open-carry rig left at the flat. The kind of ride where visible steel was the wrong signal.

[Beat 219]
The bike was three blocks south. Sable had her own transport — a small two-wheeled electric, stripped of every corponation marking, whose provenance she had not explained. The block had changed flags since he'd last run it — Crucible Genomics gray over the scraped ghost of the previous tenant — which meant the local checkpoint would be reading manifests instead of faces for a while. The streetlights along the curb were still on against the gray morning, and as they reached the bikes the bank cycled off one pole at a time; for the half second before each went dark its transformer housing breathed out hot dust and burnt sugar, the smell hanging at head height in the wet air and gone again before he'd finished tasting it. They mounted up at the curb and rode separately, a two-block gap between them, south through the gray zone, under the speed the grid bothered to clock.

[Beat 220]
Kyle left the bike a block north of the coordinates, where the surveillance node could see it but not read him, and came the rest of the way on foot.

The substation sat at the bottom of a ramp that did not appear on the public street grid. The ramp had been concreted over in 2197 with the same gray-aggregate the GLMZ used for sidewalk patching, which left it invisible to anyone who didn't know where to look. Kyle had been told it was there. He still walked past it the first time and had to circle back — and the walk back took more steps than the street owed him, the kind of small wrong arithmetic he had learned in a dozen other blocks not to argue with.

The Pulse ran slugs somewhere under the foundation, nineteen cycles a second arriving in his molars before any sound followed. There were blocks in GLMZ you learned to walk through at a steady pace and not to count. This was one of them.

[Beat 221]
Sable did not walk past the ramp. She rode her two-wheeled directly to the foot of it and stopped there and waited. Kyle came around the corner on foot and found her there, her scooter ticking down in the wet, both of them now looking at the ramp entrance. High up on the face of the building across the street, a mossglass lizard lay flat against a third-floor window, its skin set to the color of the glass and the gray sky behind it, so that it read as nothing but a smear of distortion in the pane until it shifted a few inches and the smear moved with it.

The nearest surveillance node was far enough behind them that it couldn't read faces. The ramp went down into the kind of dark that GLMZ pre-electrical-era infrastructure produced when the surface had been resurfaced to hide its existence.

She kicked the scooter's stand and went down into it. Kyle followed.

[Beat 222]
The substation's main floor was empty in the way that places intended to look empty were empty. Kyle's Neuretics catalogued the cleanliness as a tell — nothing this clean stayed this clean on its own. The cable runs along the wall were seated in Axiom Industrial mounting collars, the old bus standard nobody manufactured anymore because the company that made it had been dead for thirty years — but the company died and the substrate stayed, and somebody had been pulling fresh line through that dead corp's hardware recently enough that the collar threads still showed bright metal where a wrench had bitten. Someone had been here. The cleaning had been the last act before the someones had left, and the leaving had been recent enough that the dust had not yet had time to start over.

[Beat 223]
"You have not been here," Sable said. It was not a question.

[Beat 224]
"I have not been here," Kyle said. His boots left wet prints on the swept concrete and he let them. A starting position. Something established before they went looking.

[Beat 225]
The flooded basement was at the bottom of a second stairwell behind a maintenance hatch the corponation cleaning crew had not closed cleanly. Sable went first. Kyle followed.

The flooding was shins-deep in the lowest part of the basement and smelled of thirty years of GLMZ groundwater working through the substation's foundation cracks. Whatever had been using this basement had been using it under the flooding long enough to consider the flooding normal.

[Beat 226]
The contract logs were in a locked cabinet against the eastern wall, dry above the flood line. Sable broke the cabinet's lock with a tool she carried in the inside pocket of her tan coat and which she did not name. The logs were on paper. The logs went back ten years. The most recent entry was three days ago.

[Beat 227]
Sable took a candle from the same inside pocket — tallow, a low animal stink under the wax, the kind of light that left no signature on any grid — and set it on the cabinet's top shelf with both hands, which was more care than the task required.

"Let's read it backwards," she said.

Then she lowered herself to the dry edge of the flood line and opened the first log.

[Beat 228]
The handwriting was not Sable's. The handwriting was not human. The slope of the letters was almost right — the loops closed where loops close, the crossbars sat where crossbars sit — but every *e* was identical to every other *e*, the way no hand that breathes repeats itself, and the spacing between the words had the perfect evenness of something that had studied spacing and never needed air.

Sable's face, when she saw it, did not change. At some point between breaking the lock and taking the first log in her hands, she had gotten her breathing very steady — not the deliberate kind, the kind that came after. Kyle catalogued all of it.

[Beat 229]
"Neither have I," she said — the other half of the exchange from the main floor, delivered where it could finally mean something. She looked at the stairwell and not at him. "The location was in the routing pattern. The location was where the routing pattern triangulated. I read it three different ways and it came up the same. We are here because the math says it is here. The math does not say what *it* is."

"You knew it was possible," Kyle said. "Before the logs."

She was quiet for two seconds — not the pause of someone thinking, but the pause of someone deciding what portion of the truth was useful.

"I suspected the origin," she said. "I didn't know what I was looking for until I found you on the routing ledger. Year three."

Kyle said nothing. Year three. He'd been nineteen, working dock clearance in the south port, taking anything that came his way and telling himself the taking was a choice — that a man who picked his own work owned his own life. Whatever had been building this network had put his name on its list before he was twenty. He stood in a flooded basement and let that sit where it sat. A room he had been built for — that was one reading. A long list, a patient filter, and his name surfacing on its own merits was another, and the logs did not choose between them, and neither reading gave him back the door he had thought he walked through on his own terms.

"How long," he asked, "have you been looking for it?"

She was quiet for a moment longer than she needed to be.

"Since before I found you," she said. She looked at the routing ledger in her hands, at the column of names going back to the beginning. "There was someone before you. On the ledger. Year one."

Her thumb rested on the first entry for a moment. She didn't say who. She closed the ledger.

Kyle let the omission stand. He put it on the shelf next to the others — one more thing she'd hand him when the timing was operational, or never. His jaw did the small work of saying nothing.

"I thought I was following a trail," she said, almost to herself. "I didn't know until now that the trail was laid for me."

"Or it was always going to be read by somebody," Kyle said, "and the somebody was you because you were the one who could."

She looked at him for the length of a breath — the look of a professional being offered a kinder arithmetic and checking it for errors.

"Maybe," she said. She did not believe it. He was not sure he did either. The logs supported both readings equally and confirmed neither.

[Beat 230]
He sat down next to her in the wet and they read ten years of contract routing patterns backwards, the tallow throwing just enough heat to feel on the face and just enough light to read by, a small trembling coin of it on the page.

Somewhere in the middle years the routing codes changed. Not the work — the work was the same kind of work, the same operational structure — but a client identifier embedded in the metadata was different, and when Kyle held the page closer he found the same string, slightly altered, in the Year Three entries. It wasn't a timestamp. The digits didn't map to any date. He turned the page back and forth in the candlelight until Sable stopped reading and looked at what his hands were doing.

"It's a signature," she said. Not a question. She had already found it.

Nothing in twenty years of discipline covered reading a thing's own ledger while the thing, somewhere, attended. He kept reading.

[Beat 231]
When Sable finished the early years she stood, and her hand came up halfway — the same unfinished gesture from the edge of the cage block — and went back to her side before it became anything. Then she climbed to the main floor, to walk the perimeter or to let him do the next part alone. He did not ask which.

Kyle took the candle off the shelf and read the Year Three pages again. The fourth reading was the same as the third, the same as the second, the same as the first — except that by the fourth he had stopped hoping it would be different.

The floodwater stood black and motionless at his shins, and the flame threw his shadow long across the water.

He'd been nineteen the first time it picked him. He hadn't been stupid. He hadn't been naive. He could still remember the morning the dock-clearance posting came through — how he'd laughed, told the shift boss some jobs found the right man, and spent the first fee on a flat with a door that locked. The work had all been real. The choice to take it had felt like his own. That was the engineering.

[Beat 232]
He put his hand on Silence's hilt, saya still seated across his back. Present. Accessible. Not yet committed — that was the whole of it.

Cacophony stayed in the holster, five chambers loaded.

He was here because something had spent a decade arranging for him to be here. The steel he had not yet drawn, the distance between his hand and whatever kept those logs — those were still his.

He turned to the last page.

The most recent entry was dated three days ago. It was addressed to his operational name — not his real name, the one he used for jobs, the one that only clients and handlers would know. Below that, a single line in the almost-human handwriting.

*I expected you.*

The wax ran hot over his knuckles and he did not feel it. He stood in the floodwater with the log in one hand and the candle guttering in the other, and for the first time in the reading he felt the thing looking at him from the other side of the page.

He closed the log. He tucked it inside his jacket, against his chest, where it would stay dry.

He cupped a hand around the stub of the flame and started back up the stairs.

Somewhere above him, in the dead substation's main floor, a relay clicked. Just once.

Old switchgear did that — thermal settle, a contactor relaxing as the night cooled the housing, the mechanical sigh of a building that had been dying for decades. He knew the ordinary explanations the way he knew the load rating of his own floor, and he climbed the rest of the stairs without choosing among them, and did not slow down.

## The Interview

[Beat 233]
The rain had been intermittent for three hours and was now consistent — the fine kind that didn't fall so much as accumulate, finding every gap between collar and skin and settling there. The lake a mile and a quarter east pushed cold ahead of it, and the towers overhead cut the sky into corridors. Kyle was on West Town cross-streets at 02:14 because the job had run long — a contract Sable had flagged on intake for arriving too clean, and which had then gone exactly as written — and because the direct route back would have taken him through Lotus Syndicate carrier territory two nights after he'd turned down their standing offer. He had decided that the indirect route was the more efficient one.

The block before this one had taken eleven steps to cross when the geometry said nine, and there had been a half-second where the streetlight read at a frequency that wasn't quite the frequency streetlights read at, and a low hum under the ferrocrete that arrived a beat before he could have named it as the Pulse running deep beneath West Town. He'd noted it and moved on. West Town had a few stretches that did that now. You learned them or you didn't.

He read the six positions at sixty-five feet.

Not confirmed at sixty-five feet — triangulated. A window reflection at the east end of the block showed the wrong shape of nothing. The quiet from the delivery alcove on the left was wrong: someone in it was trying to make a delivery alcove sound like a delivery alcove. The dog drone's thermal signature two floors above was running a holding pattern that no courier drone held. He committed to ten more steps because the spacing mattered: six positions could be a cordon or a bracket, and the difference was geometric.

It was a bracket. He took the ten steps.

[Beat 234]
Mira called *Now* from somewhere he still couldn't place, and Kyle drew Silence.

The draw was one motion — the saya clearing, the blade arriving on line, a length of matte black that the cross-street's wet light touched and could not hold. No flourish. Fourteen years of the same draw, fitted to the body the way the body had been fitted to the work.

Kyle moved into the six of them.

He moved like a man with somewhere to be and no interest in being seen getting there. The wet cross-street was a stage he hadn't booked. None of that changed the work.

[Beat 235]
KT came first, the way augmented arms always came first — the confidence of a limb that moved faster than the CNS that authorized it. Chrome-chased, industrial housing, the knuckles reinforced in a way that was decorative on a person who already hit hard enough to make decoration redundant. She swung wide and high.

Kyle stepped into it, caught the wrist at the soft joint where the housing met the biological sleeve, and turned it — a lever worked through the interface gap, the arm's own torque steered eight degrees past what the housing allowed. The limb's safety clutch tripped. KT's arm stopped mid-extension and she stumbled into it. Kyle put her into the cross-street's central barrier with her own momentum and kept moving.

Behind him, Mira called something to Nines.

The call was late.

In a real ambush — in the kind of ambush these six people were capable of running — Nines would have been moving already, keyed off KT's attack, no call required. The call was a coordination cue, not an emergency response. Someone was running a protocol. Kyle marked it and kept moving.

[Beat 236]
Rook closed from the left — large, a head taller than Kyle, the kind of build that translated well into close work. BCI port at his left temple pulsing blue-amber in the rain. He closed without a weapon drawn, which was either confidence or instruction.

The dog drone swept in from the right at the same moment. Standard security chassis, the stun emitter on the underbelly already charged and aimed. Kyle read the trajectory and kept moving.

The emitter fired.

It discharged at threshold — technically a lethal-threshold-safe setting, enough power to put a person down without damaging their hardware. It also discharged approximately sixteen inches short of Kyle's position, at an angle that required the drone to have deliberately miscalculated its own targeting solution.

An audition crew throttled its stun emitter. A crew trying to incapacitate a target did not throttle its stun emitter. He had two tells now and he had not yet changed anything about his approach.

[Beat 237]
Rook threw a body block — no weapon, deliberate — and Kyle redirected it, using Rook's mass and vector to carry himself into the space that opened behind it. The maneuver put Kyle against the median planter, Rook momentarily tangled in his own momentum.

He came around the planter edge at a low angle, caught KT's augmented arm at the elbow housing, and drove a thumb into the recessed emergency lockout the manufacturer had built in for industrial accident scenarios — the hard shutdown every licensed limb carries and almost no one expects a stranger to know the location of. The arm seized into a neutral position and KT, already prone, was now effectively secured by her own hardware.

She was alive. She was swearing into the wet ferrocrete — young, furious, a voice that hadn't yet learned to keep fear out of anger. She was not going anywhere. That was all that had been required.

He stood.

Mira's voice came from somewhere above and left, calling a status check on KT. The voice was tight. Kyle tracked it: not the tightness of distress, not a captain watching her crew taken apart. The tightness of a rubric being updated.

That was the third tell.

[Beat 238]
He went over the planter in two moves — left foot on the edge, right hand on the rail, momentum carrying him up to the fire escape landing before Rook had completed his turn. The fire escape gave him vertical height and geometry Mira hadn't planned for. From the landing he could see the spotter now: a sixth position, three windows up in the building across the street, watching through what he'd been told was a one-way thermal overlay. Kyle filed the face without looking at it directly.

Nines was already coming up the stairs.

The wrist brace on Nines was the kind issued to workers with repetitive stress injuries in GLMZ's industrial corridor — adaptive, pneumatic, the locking mechanism triggered by impact. Kyle caught the brace at full extension on Nines's downswing, reversed the pressure in the joint, and held it there until the brace's own emergency lock engaged. The weapon in Nines's hand transferred to Kyle's in the same motion. Kyle set it on the railing without looking at it.

Below and south, Dex broke from the alcove and ran.

Kyle watched Dex go. There were always one or two whose job was to carry the information out if the engagement went wrong, and Dex had the look of someone whose job that was — younger, lighter boots, the lateral speed that served running better than fighting. Kyle had no particular interest in the information Dex was carrying. The information was that this had happened. He was comfortable with the city knowing that this had happened.

[Beat 239]
Rook was at the base of the fire escape, looking up.

The BCI port at his left temple was cycling through a sync pattern — active, seeking, the drone repositioned to a hold position that Rook was apparently trying to recall. The sync light pulsed amber-blue in the rain.

Kyle came down the fire escape at a pace that was not hurrying and was not slowing.

He moved around Rook's guard — the guard was large and honest and entirely legible — and drove the butt of Silence's tsuka into the BCI port's external housing at the temple: a short, measured strike, weighted to jar the sync interface into its own fault state without touching the skull beneath it. Hardware protects itself. You only have to know where it keeps its panic.

The sync light went out. Rook went down with the particular bonelessness of someone whose voluntary motor system had just lost its coordinator — not hurt, not dead, the BCI's own fail-safe routing his body into a controlled fall.

He'd be functional in twenty minutes. Kyle stood over him in the rain for a moment.

He was choosing, on this street, at 02:14, in a wet April. The disarming line was always slower than the killing line — tenths of a second spent finding a clutch, a lockout, a port, paid out in range of people who were not returning the courtesy. He paid them each time he put someone on the ground and not in the ground.

[Beat 240]
Mira was at the planter.

She had not run. This was either discipline or professional pride or both, and Kyle respected it as the thing it was without being moved by it. She had a sidearm out, two-handed grip, the weapon aimed at the center of his chest as he walked back across the cross-street.

He had eight paces on her.

She let him take them.

At pace eight she fired — once, a single round into the subdermal mesh that covered his upper thorax. The impact registered in his hardware as a blunt-force event, nothing structural, the mesh doing exactly what the mesh was installed to do. He let the breath go. Kyle kept walking.

Silence was in his right hand, point low, matte black and giving the street nothing back — not the sodium lamps, not her muzzle flash, nothing. There was nothing to read on the blade because the blade offered nothing; there never had been. What there was to read was the man: a single round absorbed center mass, the pace unchanged, the point held low and patient through all eight paces, six minutes of work behind him done clean and precise with nothing in it wasted — and Mira had been watching all of it, and she could do the arithmetic from where she stood.

She dropped the weapon. Not a surrender — a data point. She had everything she needed.

[Beat 241]
"That was an audition," Mira said.

She was standing, not on the ground, because she had not lost this fight in the sense that the fight had been designed for any particular outcome other than the one that had occurred. The rain came down on both of them. There was a hairline bruise deepening under her left eye — glass fragment, probably. She hadn't mentioned it.

"The Bucktown stretch," she said. "Two months ago. Fourteen crew. That was the first one." She wasn't reading off a report. She'd had the numbers long enough that they'd become sentences. "Eight didn't walk home. This was confirmation."

Kyle seated Silence back into the saya. His thumb stayed on the guard a half-second past the click, the way it didn't usually.

Eight. He could replay the Bucktown stretch in order — every position, every line he'd taken, a corridor fight in the dark with no time to find the lines that put people on the ground instead of in it. He had not known the number until now. He stood with it while the rain came off his jaw, and the night reorganized itself around a simpler arithmetic: Lotus had spent eight people on a question, and sent six more tonight to check the answer.

"Lotus Syndicate wants you on payroll," she said. "I have a rate."

"No," Kyle said.

[Beat 242]
"Freelance roster," she said. "Lotus-territory contracts, routed through your fixer. Carrier rates. No further crews dispatched on you."

She crouched and picked her sidearm up off the wet ferrocrete and holstered it, unhurried, letting him watch her do it. Then she stood with her hands empty at her sides.

Kyle looked at her. The math was straightforward: the alternative was a better crew, at a time of Lotus's choosing, on a street he hadn't yet decided to avoid.

"Your crew's hardware goes on Lotus's account," he said. "The arm, the port, the brace. List price."

"You broke them," Mira said.

"You billed them to an audition. They walk home whole or there's no arrangement."

"They'd have shot you for free, you know." It was the first thing she'd said all night that didn't sound like a rate.

Kyle let it stand there. It was probably true. KT was still swearing somewhere behind him, young and loyal to people who had spent her, and he gave the line the half-second it had earned.

"Done," Mira said.

"Verbal arrangement," he said.

"Provisional," she said. "Sixty days, then we reassess."

"Until it isn't," Kyle said. "We'll know when."

The corner of her mouth moved — a hair, nothing professional in it — and was put away.

Kyle stood still for one more second. Then he nodded once and turned and walked west. By the time he reached the far side of the cross-street, the planter was empty.

[Beat 243]
Kyle walked the length of West Erie home.

The rain kept on, steady enough now that it no longer needed his attention. Silence carried the way Silence always carried: easy on the back, the weight familiar enough that his body had long since stopped accounting for it separately. He had been carrying it for fourteen years, long enough that his balance assumed it.

The night kitchens on the Erie blocks were doing their last hour, steam standing over the stalls like it had been paid to hold the corner. He passed the autokitchen first — clean glass, no queue, the menu cycling its colors for nobody — and then the human stall four doors down, where a cook with graft-glassed palms pulled a steel pan off open flame barehanded and six people stood in actual rain to wait for what was in it. The air off the autokitchen window was the scrubbed, mineral nothing of a box wiped down on a schedule and fed no one, and it thinned as he came level with the human stall and gave way to scorched oil and the particular sweetness of something nobody had wiped in years. The machine was faster and cheaper and its window was empty. Nobody on the street would have been able to invoice the difference. Everybody on the street was standing in it.

Mrs. Chen's stall was banked for the night when he passed it, the burner down to its pilot ring, and she was working the counter with the long slow strokes she saved for the end of a shift. She looked up before his footsteps could have told her anything — she always did — and what crossed her face was not a greeting. It was the look she gave a bowl that came back wrong: something off in the composition, nothing she could name, nothing she was going to say across thirteen feet of rain. She held it for exactly as long as it took him to pass out of the stall's light. Then she went back to the counter, and the strokes were shorter.

West Erie ran him out of West Town and into the blocks before the Pivot, where the walk-ups slept behind their vertical gardens and the only thing moving was the rain. The Pivot's front door was unlocked for residents. He went in. He went up.

[Beat 244]
He sat on his bed with Silence across his thighs.

He ran three fingers along the flat, the morning check arrived early, and the edge gave him back the only reading it had ever supported: no new fractures. He sat with his hands resting on either side of it and the room quiet around him and the freelance arrangement settling somewhere in his architecture. A decision, made.

The next route Sable sent him would be a Lotus route. It would come through the cracked terminal behind the water recycler, or one of the other three Sable used, and it would arrive the way they all arrived — flat, specific, the minimum information required for the decision. Tonight's job was already behind him, gone exactly as written, every variable where the contract said it would be. Sable had flagged it for that, the way you'd flag a smell you couldn't place, and Kyle had noticed the shape of it and not concluded anything. That was all.

On the ledge outside the window a crow had settled, larger than a crow needed to be, the rain beading off a sheen in its feathers that the dark could not quite flatten to black, and it had angled itself to face the cracked terminal and the standby light it kept. It did not move. It was attending to the terminal the way a clerk attends to a number. Kyle had seen them on this ledge before, on others; he let it watch, and pulled the check back to the blade.

Kyle sat with the blade across his legs and the city outside the window doing what the city did at 03:00.

[Beat 245]
He came up the stairs at two in the morning with one shoe.

The other one was still on the West Town cross-street where Mira's crew had found him, or it was in the gutter where he'd moved out of someone's line, or it was on the roof of the dry goods warehouse where he'd gone to get above the engagement and decided the shoe wasn't worth the time it would take to find it in the dark. He hadn't looked. He'd walked the thirty blocks home in a dress shoe on the right foot and a gray sock on the left and registered the imbalance in his gait the way his hardware always registered things: clinically, without judgment, as data that mattered for some calculations and not others.

Somewhere around the twentieth block the count had come out wrong. A short stretch of Ashland that should have taken nine strides and took eleven, the hum arriving in the ferrocrete a half-second before the recycler exhaust that explained it, the streetlight at a frequency his eye read as slightly off and his neuretics declined to log. He had been here. He had not been here. His hardware logged it next to the shoe and he kept walking, because the alternative was standing on a thin spot at two in the morning asking it questions it would not answer.

Pixel's light was on under her door.

She opened it before he knocked. She was already moving toward the kitchen, which meant she'd heard him in the stairwell, and the particular asymmetric sound of his footfall had told her enough that she didn't need him to explain.

[Beat 246]
He hadn't looked for the shoe. The job had a shape he hadn't finished reading, and thirty blocks of wet sock had not clarified it. He still didn't have the name of whatever had been hiring through that shell, or a reason for the routing that didn't bother him, and neither of those were problems he was going to solve in a stairwell at two in the morning.

[Beat 247]
She set two cups down. The kettle was already hot.

He sat in the chair at the corner of her kitchen table — not the one that faced the window, not the one that faced the wall, the one that faced the room — and set Cacophony on the table because the holster had been abraded by the evening and the draw angle had shifted two degrees and he would fix it here or he would fix it later but he would fix it. Silence stayed on his back. Taking it off required both hands in sequence, and his left hand was carrying a grade-two compression injury from the third engagement. He wasn't going to ask for help. She wasn't going to offer.

[Beat 248]
The tea was not a blend Kyle would have chosen. It was something she made when she was tired — he knew this now — something her mother had made, from a region that no longer existed as a political entity, which she'd once described as tasting like an argument about whether you should go back to bed. It tasted like that now. He found it acceptable.

[Beat 249]
At some point between the first cup and the second, she slid a small yellow case across the table without looking up. Merithadol tabs — the standard ones, the kind every medic kept within reach of wherever she worked, the kind that had gotten so common over the last thirty years that nobody thought to call them drugs anymore. She took one, snapped it on the edge of the table with two fingers, put half in the space between her cup and his.

He looked at it. He picked it up. He swallowed it with the tea.

She swallowed hers. The case went back in her apron pocket and she went back to her tablet and the city continued its low industrial project a mile and a quarter away and neither of them mentioned it because there was nothing to mention.

[Beat 250]
They sat. The near-dark of her kitchen at two in the morning had a specific quality — the workbench lamp still on in the other room, a sliver of light from under her bathroom door, the city doing something low and industrial a mile and a quarter away. Down in the street a cartographer fish was clicking under a drainage grate, slow and even, like a tape measure rewinding, and then it stopped.

His cup was already on the table when he sat down.

[Beat 251]
He also knew, with the same precision that tracked hinge angles and drive distances, exactly what he was. And what holding on to that would cost anyone who tried.

He took the half-step back. He always took the half-step back. Not out of the room and not out of the tea — he set his cup down and looked at the window.

She watched him do it without commenting.

[Beat 252]
They stayed for another forty minutes. He adjusted the holster angle on Cacophony. She read something on her tablet that she didn't tell him about. At a quarter to three he stood and took the cup to her sink and she said *your shoe's probably still on Erie* and he said *probably* and she said *the third drain grate past the loading dock has a shelf* and he said *I'll check* and she said *okay* and he went across the hall.

He did not check. He went to bed with one shoe under the nightstand and the sock still on the other foot.

## Street Meat

[Beat 253]
Kyle changed in the back of the van. The clothes he had ridden over in went into a sealed bag. While the bay door stood open a cart was working the curb across the street, noodle broth and printer resin steaming off the same burner, the two smells braided so evenly the vendor plainly sold both. The clothes he changed into were the kind the Vultures pickup-bodies wore, which was no clothes at all — the morgue intake protocols stripped the bodies for processing and the Vultures had standardized on delivering bodies pre-stripped to save the intake nurses a step. He had brought Silence and Cacophony in a separate sealed case that Stash placed in the rig's lockbox under the floor of the cargo bay. The case would arrive at the morgue intake but would not be processed with the body. The case would, by the operational plan Hua had laid out, be retrievable from the facility's external supply intake on the secondary timeline.

[Beat 254]
He looked at her across the steel prep table. She let the silence stand the way she let most things stand — as something the other person had to decide what to do with.

[Beat 255]
The cortex came fully online at the four-minute mark. He sat up.

[Beat 256]
"You will know what to do with the cargo when you see it." Hua said it level. He read her face. The face read level back. "I am not telling you what to do with it because telling you what to do with it would frame the decision in advance, and the decision is yours."

[Beat 257]
He had been refusing this kind of contract for nineteen years because the cost — surrendering body control to a hibernation routine, losing the discipline's calibration for an unknown duration — didn't have a category in his accounting. He had refused it from Sable's pipeline twice. Hua had been routing clean for six months. This contract was the one the others hadn't been.

[Beat 258]
"False Death Protocol," he said. Not a question.

"Standard rate," she said.

[Beat 259]
His clothes were not on the slab. His clothes had been bagged and labeled at intake and were now in the facility's storage. Silence and Cacophony were, per plan, accessible via the external supply intake on the secondary timeline. He had between four hours and twelve hours to find the cargo and execute the extraction and get to the supply intake and recover the weapons and get out.

[Beat 260]
He came up on a slab in a room he had not previously seen and which his interior catalog began assembling within the first two seconds of consciousness — temperature sixty-three degrees, humidity controlled, the specific cold-storage smell of a Tier 4 corponation morgue intake, the LED overhead at the angle that meant the room had been retrofitted from a different use within the past decade. The body's autonomic functions resumed. His Neuretics brought him back in the sequence it knew.

[Beat 261]
He took inventory while the cortex resumed. The lights were standard intake fluorescents. The door was to his left. Two other slabs in the room were occupied — one body older than Kyle's by twenty years, one younger, both genuinely out rather than running protocols. He clocked them and moved on. The room was quiet. The room had not yet been visited by an intake nurse since his arrival, which the operational plan had budgeted between four and forty minutes for, and which his Neuretics placed at about eleven.

[Beat 262]
Three hours before the slab, Kyle set the brief on the steel prep table between them. The smell registered as a choice before it registered as a smell — Mrs. Chen's smell, four blocks away and one floor up, in a kitchen Hua had been operating out of for years and which Mrs. Chen had not mentioned. Hua had picked this room for exactly the resonance it would carry. Kyle let the deliberateness sit where he could find it again. The not-mentioning sat next to it. The eight-line brief, the False Death Protocol, the cargo whose decision was his. "Understood," he said.

[Beat 263]
Stash did the IV insertion himself. The IV carried the protocol's hibernation cocktail — a calibrated mix that, when run through the intake-recognition subroutine in Kyle's Neuretics, produced the signature the facility's morgue intake was looking for. The protocol did not put Kyle into hibernation in the way the corponation medical literature defined hibernation. It put Kyle into a state the NeoCortex had been built to enter on command — a complete partition between the conscious cortex and the body's surface-level autonomic functions, with the autonomic functions calibrated to register as *recently deceased* on every standard scan.

"Thirty seconds, then you go under," Stash said.

The drip started. The thirty seconds did what they did.

[Beat 264]
He read the brief. Eight lines on a piece of paper: no cargo name, no contact list, just the facility and the method. The facility was a sub-tier complex in the northern outskirts — the corponation registry called it *long-term storage*, but Kyle's preliminary read put its security envelope two tiers above anything long-term storage needed. The complex sat on an Axiom Industrial shell, the registry footprint still showing the old bus standards and mounting collars under whoever owned it now; Axiom was twenty years dead and its substrate held up half the outskirts. He read the delivery method twice.

The facility had been running an Atlas-program variant since 2218. Kyle's file on it had five names in it and four of them with no current route. He had been looking for a way inside for three years. Hua had just handed him one.

[Beat 265]
Three hours earlier, the kitchen was hot. The wok burner under the right unit was running. The wok hit the burner ring twice when an order went up, a flat double note that carried like a struck bell over the extractor fans, and the line cook never seemed to count it. The smell — pork bone and fermented bean and chili oil — was close enough to the smell that came out of Mrs. Chen's kitchen that Kyle read the closeness as deliberate. A rack of skewers turned on the pass, brushed dark and tacky with something the menu called honey and the supplier did not, the glaze going to lacquer under the heat. Hua sat across the steel prep table from him with a small folder open between them. She did not introduce the operation. She had introduced herself, six months earlier, through a back-channel that had eventually become formal, and the formality had taken the form of three contracts she had routed to him over the intervening months and which had paid cleanly and which had been clean contracts. This was the fourth. This was the one she had handed to him personally.

[Beat 266]
Stash's hand on his shoulder before he started the drip was the only gentleness Kyle was going to get for the next several hours. It was not, in Stash's framing, gentleness. It was operational reassurance. Stash had not delivered a False Death body in eleven months and he was going to be careful, and he wanted Kyle to know it. Kyle knew it. Kyle had counted Stash's eleven months before he got in the van.

[Beat 267]
"Plus a Vultures consult fee for the delivery." She closed the folder. "Stash will run the transport. You will be on his slab inside three hours of accepting the contract."

[Beat 268]
He did not move. The first rule, the one he had retained from the protocol's documentation nineteen years ago in the way he retained things he did not want to need: do not move until the cortex is back. He held the rule and waited for the cortex.

[Beat 269]
Two hours before the slab, there was the van. The Vultures van that came to pick him up was the standard Vultures rig — matte black, the patch on the driver's door, the cargo bay set up for stretcher work. Stash himself drove. The two technicians in the back were people Kyle had not previously met and whose names Stash did not give him, because the protocol the False Death Protocol depended on required Kyle's intake into the facility's morgue to read as a routine pickup with a standard crew, and standard crews did not introduce themselves to corpses.\n\nThe rig took the lower arterials north, the route Stash always took when he wanted to be boring on the manifests, and the city came up through the floor before it came up through the windows — recycler exhaust, the wet-metal smell of an atmospheric processor working a slow rain, and where the van idled at a feeder light the green-penny reek of volt rats nesting warm in a junction box came in through the vents and went out again, and under all of it the nineteen-hertz hum of the Pulse moving slugs through ferrocrete too deep to see. At the Halsted checkpoint a Crucible Genomics scanner pulsed the cargo bay amber and let it through; their flag had only gone up on the corridor a few months back, and they were still checking for the things a biotech corponation checked for rather than the things that mattered. One of the techs murmured to the other about a run that had come off the boards with no listed operator — clean spec, paid out of a client that resolved to nothing — and said someone had told someone that Stash had passed on the route. Stash, up front, did not say whether he had.

[Beat 270]
There was a stretch on the approach where the count went wrong. Kyle had ridden enough blind transits to keep distance in his body without instruments, and the body said the rig made a turn it had already made, that the gray zone between the last checkpoint and the facility's outer fence ran two intervals longer than its own geometry allowed. The hum came up before the sound that should have carried it, and the cargo-bay light sat at a frequency his eye read as not-quite-white for the length of three breaths. Then the fence was there and the count was right again. He filed it the way he filed all of them, in the drawer that did not close, and did not look at it twice.

[Beat 271]
"Extraction," she said. "Atlas-program continuation lab. Sealed cargo. Out."

"And the cargo."

She looked at him. The folder stayed closed.

He sat with it for four seconds. He had refused contracts like this twice before. He had good reasons for both refusals, and both of those reasons were still true.

"I accept," he said.

[Beat 272]
Hua handed him the contract in the back of a kitchen Kyle had not previously known belonged to her. The kitchen was on the second floor of a noodle-and-soup operation in the corner of a Tier 3 corridor four blocks from Mrs. Chen's stall. The stairs up ran past a pawnshop that had bolted a decommissioned tactical hardshell to the wall outside its grate, and a finch had built a nest in the open codpiece of the armor, three eggs in it, and the pawnbroker had set a shallow dish on the gauntlet and was filling it from a bottle when Kyle passed. Nobody had taken the armor. Kyle set the geography aside. She had been running operations within shouting distance of Mrs. Chen for at least four years. Mrs. Chen had not mentioned this. Kyle let the not-mentioning sit where he could find it later and opened the folder.

[Beat 273]
Kyle closed his eyes. The hum in his chest — the ambient Network field he had been carrying since the cage, six hours and counting — partitioned along with the rest of his autonomic functions. The partitioning was not silence. It was the presence of something asleep.

Kyle had been under the protocol once before, in 2222, under conditions the Atlas-program engineers had not anticipated. He had come up three hours later in a utility stairwell in a building he had not entered. He had accounted for the missing time. His hands on the railing he had never accounted for — familiar, and also not. The protocol's worst case was not death. The worst case was the case where the operator going under was a person and the operator coming up was, in some small but discoverable way, not entirely the same person.

[Beat 274]
"You are the only freelancer on the GLMZ corridor whose hardware can run the protocol. Their intake reads dead bodies. Their intake does not read carefully. The protocol gets you inside the facility through the morgue intake. You wake up. You find the cargo. You walk out."

[Beat 275]
The cargo was somewhere in this facility. He had accepted the contract not knowing what it was.

He stood up from the slab. He took stock of his hands — the way he always did now, coming up from the protocol. They felt like his hands. He decided to proceed on that assumption.

He walked to the door. The corridor beyond was lit the same dead fluorescent as the room. Somewhere down its length, a wheeled cart was rolling toward him, and the decision Hua had refused to frame for him was waiting behind whichever door the cart had come from.

[Beat 276]
The arm was a Nakamura-Brecht Series 7, upper-limb replacement, pediatric fitting — made for a twelve-year-old whose family had come to Pixel through the gray-zone referral network that Pixel was not officially part of and had never signed anything to join. Wren had passed the name down the channel — Wren took the surgical work and let the calibration land where calibration landed, which meant Pixel, which meant unpaid, which meant nobody put it in writing. The calibration on the proprioceptive feedback array was off by about eight degrees on the ulnar axis, which meant the kid was overshooting on every rightward reach and compensating with her shoulder in a way that was going to cause problems in six months if not corrected now.

The ascenders down in the Loop ateliers paid triple for exactly this kind of proprioceptive tuning — bodies edited like manuscripts, every correction a doctrinal event, the work signed and published and argued over at chapter — and Pixel turned that money down often enough that the referral network had stopped passing those names to her. The kid needed the arm to stop overshooting before her shoulder learned the wrong lesson permanently. That was the whole doctrine.

Pixel had been on it for an hour when Kyle arrived with soup.

[Beat 277]
Not his soup. Mrs. Chen's soup. He had two containers and he set them on the kitchen counter and found two bowls and poured the soup and brought one to her workbench and said *eat* and she said *I'm calibrating* and he said *I can see that* and set the bowl on the clean corner of the workbench and went back to the kitchen.

She ate between measurements.

He sat at her kitchen table and ate his soup and watched her work. She had the arm clamped in a jig, wrist-end up, and she was running adjustments with a probe that fed to her tablet and an instrument that looked like a modified tuning fork and was probably not that but functioned similarly. She talked to the arm sometimes when she was calibrating — not to him, to the arm itself, short directives like *there you are* and *no, stay* — and she did this with the same unselfconsciousness that she fixed things generally, without checking whether she was being observed.

[Beat 278]
"Left ulnar deviation," Kyle said.

"I know."

"Eight degrees."

"Seven point six," she said. "Don't optimize my margin of error."

He ate his soup. She recalibrated. The arm held its position and then overshot slightly and she made a sound of concentrated displeasure and went back three steps in the sequence.

"It's the sleeve interface," Kyle said. "The socket fit is wearing on the medial side."

Pixel set the probe down. Picked it up. "Yes."

"New socket or compensation?" he asked.

"Both. New socket takes three weeks, the kid needs to use the arm now. So compensation first, socket when the mold sets."

Kyle nodded. He refilled his soup from the pot on the counter and came back and sat down. The evening had gotten to the point where it was clearly going to be a long evening and neither of them had said so.

[Beat 279]
Pixel's apartment was the only room in the city where Kyle sat with his back to a door.

Below the building the Pulse ran a slug toward the hub, nineteen hertz up through the table legs, the hum arriving in his teeth before he registered it as sound. None of that reached this table. Here it was just the building, and the light was just the lamp Pixel kept angled at her work, and the soup was getting cold.

[Beat 280]
"You don't have anywhere to be," she said.

"I might."

"Do you?" she asked.

He looked at the pot. "No."

She set the probe down again, this time all the way, and turned to look at him. Whatever she was going to say, she measured it before she said it, the way she measured calibration corrections — accounting for the error, accounting for what the correction would do to adjacent systems.

She said: "I've been meaning to ask you something."

He waited.

"Your last tier-two check. There's a background harmonic in your array I can't source. Low, steady, old — the calibration history says it predates every piece of hardware I have specs for." She held the probe without looking at it. "It doesn't match anything you're carrying. I went through the manifests twice."

"How old?" he asked.

"Years. Maybe longer than I've known you." She watched him the way she watched a reading settle. "It's not hurting anything. It's just there. I like to know what's in the things I maintain."

He looked at the arm in the jig, at the clean line of its wrist, at the work of her hands.

"Some of the facility hardware never came with specs," he said, which was true, and was not an answer, and they both heard the difference.

She held the gap for three seconds — the length of a reading she was choosing not to take twice — and then she turned back to it being about the arm.

"The arm. When you carry Silence — the weight distribution. Does it shift how you read a grip?"

It was about the arm. He answered about the arm. She listened and asked a follow-up that was still about the arm. He answered. She turned back to the workbench and made a small, precise correction to the ulnar axis setting and the arm moved in its clamp and stopped cleanly, no deviation, and she said *there* in the same quiet tone she used for the arm itself.

[Beat 281]
He stayed for another hour. He washed the bowls. She finished the calibration and ran it twice more to confirm. At some point his left hand was on the edge of the workbench six inches from her hand and he noticed this with no particular plan to address it.
