She noticed it at the same time. He could tell she noticed it by the quality of the pause in her work — three seconds, no more, before she picked the probe back up.

Nothing was said.

He left at eleven. She said *tell Mrs. Chen the soup was good* and he said *she knows* and closed the door, and the sound of the latch catching was different from every other latch in the building and he'd known that for months and now he'd said the word *months* to himself in the stairwell and made himself not stop on the landing to look back at her door. The stairwell smelled of wet ferrocement.

## A Borrowed Hand

[Beat 282]
Twelve hours to find the doctor. His hands at his sternum, palms outward, the nylon knot holding. He does not stop walking.

*I will visit you again.* His own voice, his own registers, the cadence slightly long — reading a sentence it has not spoken before. He does not stop walking. Ninety-four steps per minute, exact, the pace stays exact because the pace is the only variable he controls right now and he is going to control it. He counts two steps. Four. Then he asks it flat, his own voice, the question he has been holding since the maintenance lead seated behind his ear eighteen hours ago by the count he is keeping: *What are you called.* The rain comes down. A pause — not hesitation, something more like courtesy, the beat of something that has learned to wait. Then his mouth answers itself: *Manifest.* And then the mouth is his again and he files the name in the same place he filed the salvage glyph and the EMT-spec band model and the specific sound of the cleaver going down twice, and he keeps walking, and the arithmetic resolves in the dry register his Neuretics reserves for catalog work: the cargo arm is empty hardware. Has been since the junction box on the maintenance landing. The E.L.F. is resident in him — has been since the contact seated, will leave when Manifest chooses to leave, will return on Manifest's schedule. He has no leverage. He does not stop walking. The autocab is three minutes out. Sable said three minutes. The destination is in the car's routing system, not his, and the address belongs to Sable. He has twelve hours and three minutes, and he keeps walking.

[Beat 283]
The arm comes free of the shoulder coupling with a sound like a wet bolt shearing — not clean, not catastrophic, just the specific protest of corroded metal that has decided to cooperate — and Kyle catches the weight with his left stump and his chest simultaneously because catching is the only option available and the arm is ten feet of Axiom-era industrial hardware and approximately ninety pounds of it comes across his lap in the dark and the current runs cold over his boots and he sits with it and breathes.

[Beat 284]
"The Vásquez shipment," Hua says. Not a question. She does not raise her voice. "The Syndicate's contracted route. The Syndicate's contracted fee." She turns from the tank and looks at him with the expression of someone who has already finished a meal and is now simply clearing the table. "You took both." She is quiet for a moment. "I would have liked to know what arithmetic got you here." The question is not rhetorical. She is genuinely curious, in the mild, professional way of someone who has done this long enough to stop being surprised by the crimes and start being interested in the math that made them seem possible.

[Beat 285]
Kyle has not been touched by another person without intent to harm or repair in four months. Longer, for tenderness. The hardware does not have a category for the temperature of a tear on a fresh surgical seam. The tear runs down to the pink bracelet at his wrist and sits there, warm, in the groove where the seal is still finding its hold. He does not move. He does not pull back.

[Beat 286]
The silence that follows is not empty. Hua looks at him for a long moment, her real hands holding Silence with a care that is worse than contempt — the care of someone handling an object they have already decided to keep. Then she nods, once, and behind him he hears the first snap of the nylon roll come open.

[Beat 287]
The door to 2E opens before he reaches the landing. It has been doing this for four years — the music cut, the door open, the question not asked until he chooses to answer it.

[Beat 288]
The convention is the convention. He almost does. The hand twitches in the direction of withdrawal — his right hand, the dominant one, the one that has been making this kind of decision for him since he was fifteen — and then the twitch resolves into stillness because he is too tired to complete it. He looks at her face. Her eyes are wet. Then they are not wet — they are leaking, openly, crying she would have done exactly the same with nobody in the room. The tears go down her cheeks and onto her jaw and from her jaw they fall.

[Beat 289]
*The distinction matters.* He follows.

[Beat 290]
She holds the door for exactly as long as it takes him to walk through it. Ashland and Whiting at 0347 is empty in the way that intersection gets at this hour — the recycler exhaust steaming off the corner stack, the streetlight on the west side flickering at the rate it has been flickering for two years, a single Vultures truck idling at the far curb, lights off, the driver behind the wheel reading something on a handset and not looking up as Kyle passes. A breath before the streetlight cycles, the whole corner smells of hot dust and burnt sugar off the transformer housing, and then the light comes up amber and the smell is gone and no one on the block has stopped to mark it. The corner used to fly Arcturus colors; sometime in the last month the substation badge got swapped for a Cinderfall mark, which means the block changed hands again and the checkpoint two streets south will be checking for something new now. Kyle files it the way he files which freelancers are working which seams — Stash had this run last month, before the flags moved — and does not slow. He does not look at the truck either. He keeps walking.

[Beat 291]
The transfer is instantaneous. The status strip goes dark. Then his mouth opens.

[Beat 292]
The bootstrap problem is this: Manifest's first act was to use Kyle's voice to answer a question Kyle had not yet decided to ask aloud. The sequence requires that Manifest had already mapped the speech pathway before Kyle chose to open it — which means the contact at the junction box did not initiate the transfer, only confirmed it. The E.L.F. had already assessed the architecture and decided to proceed, and the maintenance lead seating itself behind his ear was not introduction but formality. Kyle processes this in the space of four steps. He does not know yet whether this is better or worse than what he imagined. He suspects the question will matter less than what he does with the answer.

[Beat 293]
Kyle stops at the top of the stairs. He does not know how Pixel knows about Sable taking the doctor. Then he does know — Sable would have routed a message, because Sable routes messages, because Pixel was in the middle of the operational loop in the kitchen scene at the half-hour mark and Sable does not leave loose ends with operators she has decided to trust. He files the implication. Sable has decided to trust Pixel. What that means can wait for tomorrow.

[Beat 294]
*"No,"* he says.

[Beat 295]
Not the arm — the arm follows, the terminal fingers adjusting their grip on the pipe with the small corrections of something that has learned the tool's balance. What moves first is the routing: a warmth behind his left eye that is not his, a flicker in his Neuretics' spatial model as the E.L.F. overlays a path through the thermal trace that Kyle was not building. The path goes north and then east and then up through a service corridor his survey of the Depot's lower levels had flagged as sealed. It is not sealed. The E.L.F. knows this because the E.L.F. has been in the pressure-plate firmware for the last hour, possibly longer, and knows which plates have been walked in the last six months and which ones have not, and the sealed corridor has not been walked, which is different from sealed.

[Beat 296]
The shape resolves as he gets closer. The torso shell has collapsed inward and the stance legs are fused at the hip joint — the corrosion is so complete the left leg is part of the silt now, indistinguishable from it, the ferrous content of the metal bleeding into the sediment and staining it rust-brown in a radius of maybe sixteen inches. The head sensor pod is gone. Not damaged — removed, the mounting collar still intact but empty, which means scavengers, which means people have been in this section of the channel and left this unit in place because the rest of it was not worth the extraction cost. The arm is the only thing they could not reach easily, which is the point of the arm: it is ten feet at full extension, built to reach into a thirty-three-foot freight container from a fixed pedestal mount, and it is still attached at the shoulder and the shoulder still has the amber LED and the amber LED means trickle current, which means the actuator firmware is still idling, which means the shoulder joint still moves.

[Beat 297]
Pixel takes one step into the hall. She does not take a second. She has understood — quickly, the way she understands most things — that Kyle is not asking for help. She waits at the door anyway.

[Beat 298]
Ten feet of Axiom industrial hardware moving at the radius it was built for — the reach of a freight bay, the arc of a loading dock where the clearances are measured in container widths — and the conduit pipe goes through the first man's sternum at a swing radius nothing in the room was built to defend against, the sound wet and structural simultaneously, and the man goes down and does not come back up. The second man has his hands up and his mouth open and Kyle is already inside his guard, the arm's elbow joint driving him backward by the throat into the wall, pinning him there at the junction of the arm's extension and the wall's resistance, and Kyle holds the pressure long enough to feel the man's legs stop working and then moves. The third man fires from somewhere to Kyle's left — the round punches into the cargo arm's shoulder housing, a dense metallic impact that travels up the coupling into his right stump as pressure without pain, and the arm stutters, the E.L.F. routing power around the failure with the small corrections of something that has learned to work with what it has, and the arm holds. Kyle takes three steps toward the third man and drives the glass shard up under his jaw — end-on, fast, a method he did not have an hour ago and will not use again after tonight if the math works out — and the man sits down with a specific finality. The fourth man tries to come inside the arm's reach, which is a reasonable tactic and a fatal one, because inside the arm's reach is where Kyle is, and Kyle puts him through the glass tank with his shoulder and his weight and the momentum of a man who has already decided what this room costs. The glass comes apart in a specific way — not shattering but yielding, a pressure-differential failure, the water driving outward in a sheet — and the crocodile, suddenly free, suddenly awake, attends to the fifth man without further input from Kyle.

[Beat 299]
Kyle nods. The nod is the only thing he can produce that the convention will accept. Then he walks past her — careful, slow, the shoulder catching — and crosses the hall to 2F. The lock reads his biometric. The door admits him. He turns on no light. The room organizes itself out of the dark the way it always does: medkit by the door, notebook on the counter, the photograph of his mother on the windowsill facing the intersection because she would have wanted to see the street. Inside the wall behind the counter something shifts in the conduit, a sound like dry rice poured slowly, and then holds still while he stands there.

[Beat 300]
Across the hall, the music does not come back on.

[Beat 301]
Silence comes off the right hip. He lays the friction sheath across the pillow first, then his torso, then settles the blade across his sternum in the saya the way Seo taught him to settle a sword for sleep when the night had gone wrong and the watch was still required. His left hand rests on the tsuka. His right hand rests on the saya. The pink seam at both wrists is visible against the dark of the leather, and Pixel's tears are still on the right cuff in two small dark spots that will be dry by morning and will not be visible to anyone who does not know to look.

[Beat 302]
"Why are you helping me."

[Beat 303]
*The body is going to shake in approximately seven minutes. Be inside before then.*

[Beat 304]
*Sleep. The arithmetic resolves in the morning. Sleep.*

[Beat 305]
*"Thank god you're all right."* The words come out small and wet, pressed through her fingers. Then: *"Sable said she'd take her somewhere safe."*

[Beat 306]
The two men take him by the shoulders and the chair-arm leather is slick under their grip where his blood ran, and they drag him south toward the freight grate, and the grate opens onto twenty feet of fall into the storm sluice, and Kyle can hear the water below — the resonance of a confined channel moving fast — the drop had a current, and the current was going somewhere. The smell is still in him. The fourteen hours have already started. The sword is still upstairs on the table with her hand on it, and he is going down the hole, and those two facts are the only things in the room that matter, and he holds both of them in the same place where the facility taught him to hold operational data, and the grate opens, and the men let go.

[Beat 307]
The music in 2E does not come back on. He has no memory of when it cut.

[Beat 308]
The hum starts in his teeth.

[Beat 309]
The cleaver comes down once.

[Beat 310]
"What was the debt."

[Beat 311]
*Take what is yours. The borrowed limb is not yours. It was a tool. The tool is finished.*

[Beat 312]
The first one lands on the inside of his right wrist, on the pink seam.

[Beat 313]
He rolls onto his side. Gets his knees under him. His shoulder goes to the wall and the slope is approximately twenty degrees and the ferrocrete is wet and poured without aggregate which means it is smooth enough to walk against without tearing cloth. He walks himself upright using the wall as a spine. When he is standing the current runs at his ankles and the dark is absolute and his Neuretics routes a low-draw thermal trace through the optic thread — the left pupil, the one with the gold ring, the one that dilates slower — and the tunnel resolves in false-color heat: the water running white-green, the walls running grey-black, and far south, past a bend he cannot see around, something warm. Multiple signatures. The movement is wet and multi-jointed, the sound arriving in the water before it arrives in the air, and his Neuretics identifies it as *biological, plural, mobile, approaching* and flags the heat signatures as mammalian-adjacent but not human, which is the precise category of information he was hoping not to have tonight.

[Beat 314]
*The convention between you is no longer purely tools-and-quiet. Note that. The catalogue has updated.*

[Beat 315]
He hits the water back-first and the cold is immediate and total — not a temperature, a system shock, the kind that locks the diaphragm and forces the hardware to route oxygen priority before the lungs remember what they are for. The current takes him east. He counts: one, two, three, four — and on four his right shoulder finds the slope and he lets the current carry him into it, uses the momentum instead of fighting it, and his back scrapes ferrocrete and he stops moving. Six seconds. His Neuretics timestamps it. He is alive.

[Beat 316]
*I am going up.*

[Beat 317]
He steps inside. The arm swings. Too high, too late — the E.L.F. routing around the intent instead of through it, compensating for a body that no longer has the geometry the firmware expects — and the pipe catches the chitin sheath at the shoulder joint and rings off, the vibration traveling back through the coupling into his right stump and he feels it as pressure without pain, which is worse somehow than pain would be. The mutant's shoulder hits his chest. The wall arrives at his back. He takes it on the shoulder blades and his knees bend and his feet find the shelf edge and he does not go down, because down is where this ends, and he has fourteen hours left and intends to use them. The beak drives at his throat. He turns his head. The beak plates rake his jaw at the chrome bracket and the ache he has been using as a handhold all night flares white. He keeps moving and his left stump comes up.

[Beat 318]
The Pivot's lobby door reads his biometric and admits him without requiring the second factor; the lock has not asked for the second factor in three years and Kyle has not pressed the building's manager on the slack because the slack works in his favor. The reader is still bolted into an Axiom mounting collar in the door frame — the company nineteen years dead, the collar still carrying current, the way half the infrastructure in the Gray Zone is a ghost corp's bones with someone else's hardware screwed into them. A loop of prayer beads hangs over the junction-box handle beside the reader, worn flat on one side from a thumb that is not his and that he has never seen. The stairwell is the stairwell. He climbs. The third step from the top has the loose riser that has been loose since 2198 and produces the same small sound it always produces under his weight, and Kyle's weight on it produces it correctly, and that is apparently enough.

[Beat 319]
He can see it in his Neuretics' triage logic, the same way you can see a closed door from both sides simultaneously: the needle cluster at the arm's terminal harness is there, the bus coupling is there, the dorsal port row beneath his right-forearm skin sleeve is there — undocumented, unmentioned, the geometry of it too convenient, and he does not look at it long — and none of those three things can reach the others without a hand to manage the sequence, and the only hand in the trench is attached to a machine drawing trickle current off a degraded cell, and the machine does not know what a body is. He sits with it for sixty seconds. Somewhere in the cable run above his head something shifts its weight, a sound like dry rice poured slowly, and settles again. His Neuretics offers nothing. The hum at 19Hz runs through the ferrocrete into his spine and he sits with it the way he has sat with everything tonight and does not move. Then the hum changes register.

[Beat 320]
The freight grate opens onto rain.

[Beat 321]
Kyle finds his voice. He gives her the answer the convention requires: *I understand what I owe.* There is a correct order to this, and people die when it is done out of order.

[Beat 322]
Then he turns toward the chum barrel.

[Beat 323]
The man closest to the door looks at the silhouette — the arm hanging nearly to the floor, the fingertips dragging across the flagstones with a sound like someone slowly drawing a finger along a table edge, the conduit pipe loose in the terminal grip, the shape of a man who came back from somewhere he should not have survived — and says one word. Kyle does not catalogue it. It is not information. The arm swings.

[Beat 324]
The cargo arm drags. The conduit pipe is gone — left in the last man, the geometry of extraction not worth the cost — and the terminal fingers trail across the flagstones in the blood and the salt water with the sound of someone drawing a slow line. His boots find the track he left coming in and follow it in reverse. His Neuretics timestamps the crossing at eleven seconds. The bio-battery reads fourteen percent. He does not hurry. Hurrying is information he is not prepared to give her.

[Beat 325]
Pixel in pajamas, the same as last time — bare feet on the hall's cold composite, the music inside the unit cut the moment she heard the stair, her hair loose, the crease on her left cheek from the pillow she has just left, the oversized sweater traded for a long-sleeve thermal that covers the same arms with the same compulsive thoroughness. She takes one look at him — the blade at his hip instead of his back, the pink bracelet visible at his cuffs where the leather rides up, the way he is standing — and her hand goes to her mouth and stays there.

[Beat 326]
"The punishment," Hua says, and she picks up Silence by the saya — not the handle, the saya, careful, informed — "for theft from the Syndicate's contracted work is the hand that took it."

[Beat 327]
"I did not steal it," Kyle says. His voice comes out level. The hardware is good for that much. "I completed the delivery. The client redirected the fee before your collection window. That is a scheduling dispute. Not a theft."

[Beat 328]
Two blocks from the Ashland junction his mouth opens.

[Beat 329]
She stops pacing in front of the tank. The two men at his shoulders have not moved in ninety seconds. The toolkit on the floor between them is a black nylon roll, the kind that fastens with two snaps and opens flat, and Kyle has not looked directly at it because looking at it is information he is not yet prepared to have. Silence is on the table. The angle Hua arranged it — parallel to the table edge, saya toward her, handle toward the room's center — is the arrangement of something already appraised. She has not touched the blade. She has touched the saya twice, with two fingers, the way you confirm a purchase without appearing to need it. Across the table, the chum barrel stands beside the tank, its lid set aside with the same ritual neatness as the friction sheath. The room smells like an argument that was settled before Kyle arrived.

[Beat 330]
Hua carries his hands to the chum barrel one at a time with the unhurried care of someone handling property she already considers hers. She places them palms up at the rim, parallel, the stump-ends pointing inward, and the arrangement is deliberate — a bracket, closed. The crocodile does not stir. Then she walks back to the table and puts one of her real hands on Silence's saya, and she does not look at Kyle when she does it, and that is the worst thing she has done tonight, because it means the gesture is not for him. It is for herself. It is the gesture of a woman filing something under *kept*.

[Beat 331]
The pink seam aches under her fingers in the specific way a fresh reattachment aches — the nerves still negotiating, the subdermal mesh still re-pairing, the deep tissue lodging a small steady complaint about the cleaver work that the analgesic cannot fully suppress. It hurts. It hurts in the way that promises days of it, maybe weeks. Pixel could draw him the curve.

[Beat 332]
It does. Pixel nods, once, and the nod is acceptance. She will accept it. The discipline is what is letting him stand here at all. Neither of them says so.

[Beat 333]
He sits on the edge of the bed. He does not remove the jacket. The shoulder is too sharp and the right hand at sixty percent grip is not capable of the small precise motions a jacket requires. The boots come off because the boots come off — that motion is automatic, has been automatic for nineteen years, will be automatic until the body stops. He does not remove anything else.

[Beat 334]
*Cooperate,* his own voice says, the syntax slightly long, the cadence of someone reading a sentence they have not spoken before. *Power first. Then sequence.* He hears himself say it. The arm is empty hardware now — the amber LED has gone out, the maintenance cycle has stopped, the trickle current is still running but nothing is running it anymore — and the thing that was in it is behind his eyes now, and there is nothing he can do with it tonight and he waits. The arm's fingers move. One at a time, slowly, each motion costing the residual cell a fraction of what it has left, each motion buying the next motion, and the arm reaches up from his lap with the deliberate patience of something that has just acquired, for the first time in eighteen years, a reason to be careful.

[Beat 335]
The arm sets the pipe down. Kyle watches it find a fragment of the cargo housing along the channel floor — stress-fracture edge, not built to cut but capable of it — and bring it to his left stump. The terminal fingers fit the shard against the cauterizer band, pull a length of cable sheathing from the debris at his feet, and bind it in three passes: functional, not clean, the way the E.L.F. does everything. When it is finished, it confirms the angle with a ghost-motion at his left shoulder. He does not ask what it was doing. He can see what it was doing. The arm picks the pipe back up.

[Beat 336]
He says nothing. His Neuretics is at thirty-one percent and still burning cycles on the exits — service hatch behind the tank, single door behind him, ventilation width insufficient — and on Hua's hands, and on the crocodile, which has still not moved. His jaw aches where the left molar meets the chrome bracket they installed at the facility, the one that never sat right, and the ache rings high and hard — whatever hit him was harder than a fist.

[Beat 337]
She does not say: I'm glad you're alive. She does not say: I told you this would happen. She does not say anything — she just reads the state of him: the hands at his sternum, the EMT-spec bands at the stump edges, the cargo arm riding dark and dead at his shoulder coupling, the tissue window still holding by whatever margin is left — and she takes the necklace first, the twine looped over his head and laid on the instrument tray, his hands following onto the steel, palms up, the way Hua arranged them. She takes the cargo arm next — the coupling releases at the shoulder, the arm goes to the corner and does not move, shell only — and she takes Silence last, two hands, level, the way he carries it, and she lays it on the side tray and it is there, it is on the tray, it is four feet from where his left hand is about to be again. The analgesic she mixes herself, the dispensing unit barely larger than his fist, and it goes in through a port at his neck, and the night begins to mute the way it mutes when someone who knows what they are doing decides it should. As it mutes, the Neuretics feed produces a glyph in the not-factory pattern, three syllables in old Axiom signage, the same signature Manifest has been signing in since the junction box: *we are not done.* The sentence sits somewhere he cannot easily delete, patient and old, the same frequency as something that has been in the ferrocrete longer than the facility's survey teams were willing to go. He does not know where this room is. The doctor does not tell him. He does not ask.

[Beat 338]
The arm picks up the pipe.

[Beat 339]
Both bands hold. The left seam weeps more than the right — the second cut was ragged, the band compensated with more heat, and the residue at the edge smells like burned hair and the disinfectant the EMT-spec model uses to sterilize the contact surface. The smell is information. The smell means the seal is there. The hands are twenty feet up in a chum barrel, palms up, the way she placed them, and the EMT-spec band is the same model the Depot's first responders carry because the Syndicate bulk-buys from the same municipal supplier — fourteen-hour nerve preservation, civilian-rated, the kind of equipment that assumes the patient is going to a hospital afterward. The Lotus Syndicate did not buy these bands to be kind. They bought them because dead hands do not reattach and reattachment is the point of the punishment. The punishment is the night. The cauterizer band is the frame around the night, the thing that says: *you will feel this for fourteen hours and then you will have to live with what it cost you.* He has eleven hours and fifty-three minutes left. A civilian AutoDoc charges under Φ800 for reattachment if the tissue window holds. He has Φ1,400 in a dead drop three blocks from the Depot's north entrance. The math is simple.

[Beat 340]
The cargo arm closes around the saya and the grip is wrong — the E.L.F. compensating, the terminal fingers redistributing pressure in real time, the lag visible, a half-second where the blade tilts before the firmware corrects and the weight finds level — and then it is right, the saya seated in the borrowed grip, the handle toward him, and the saya is wet from the trench and the weight is the weight he has carried since he was nineteen years old and the weight is correct. He does not raise it. The cargo arm holds Silence at low carry, formal, present, the threat already receipted by every man on the floor between here and the service hatch. He looks at her. She is breathing through her nose, controlled, the breath of a woman who has run this room for a long time and knows that the room is no longer running the way she runs it.

[Beat 341]
The hands knock against his sternum with each step. Palms outward, dripping now with rainwater mixed with chum barrel residue, the twine doing its functional work, the knot holding. His Neuretics timestamps his pace at ninety-four steps per minute, which is the pace the facility calibrated as sustainable-while-injured, the pace that does not read as flight and does not read as incapacity, the pace that gets you four blocks without anyone calculating what you are. He passes the first block. The rain is the same rain it was before the Depot, before the sluice, before the table. The second block. The cargo arm drags at the coupling. Silence is still in the borrowed grip, the saya wet and dark in the rain. The blade was always the point. He is still holding the blade. He keeps walking.

[Beat 342]
Not weather — redistribution. The atmospheric processors are running their usual math two miles east and the Gray Zone gets what the Core sheds, and tonight what the Core sheds is a cold November downpour that hits the flagstone around the grate in flat percussion and does not stop. Kyle pulls himself through on his right forearm and the cargo arm's weight swings him sideways on the lip and he catches himself with his knee and stands. The bio-battery reads nine percent. The cargo arm's status strip has been dark for six minutes — brownout, the trickle cell finally gone, the E.L.F. drawing on his own reserves to keep the terminal fingers closed around Silence, which is a cost he did not negotiate and cannot renegotiate at nine percent. He does not ask it to stop. He does not ask anything. He starts walking north.

[Beat 343]
He calls Sable from the corner of the gas exchange station on the north side of the block, in the rain, with his hands at his sternum and the cargo arm dead at his shoulder and the window at eleven hours forty-seven and falling. Nine words: *I need to go to the doctor. The real one.*

A pause. The kind she uses when she is doing arithmetic.

*'The location isn't safe for you to have,'* she says. Flat, the same voice she has on every channel, the voice of a woman who has run this calculation before. *'You give it up. You don't mean to, but you do. It's safer if you don't know.'*

The laugh that comes out of him is not one he catalogued. It arrives from somewhere below his Neuretics' management layer, the kind of laugh that happens when the gap between two facts becomes suddenly visible — and the gap is that he has already been tortured tonight, has already paid with the most information his body had available to give, and no address is safer than where he has just been. He laughs in the rain at the corner of the gas exchange station with his hands at his chest, the cauterizer seals weeping slowly, the sound coming up out of him long and uneven and nothing like a man who is all right: *I guarantee you it fucking isn't.*

The line is quiet for four seconds.

*'Three minutes,'* Sable says.

[Beat 344]
The second snap of the nylon roll comes open behind him and Kyle counts what that means: two snaps, tool roll, multiple instruments. He counts the men by their breathing — four, including the one who has been standing to his left so still that Kyle almost miscounted him as furniture. The wood block appears from somewhere below his sightline and slides under his right forearm with the practiced ease of something that has a designated shelf. The grain is dark at exactly the wrist-width. He does not look at it directly. He is looking at the crocodile.

[Beat 345]
Not a sound — a frequency, arriving through the ferrocrete and into his boots and up through his skeleton the way certain bass frequencies will, and his Neuretics timestamps its arrival at 19Hz and flags it *environmental, persistent, source unknown* and does not know what to do with it, which is information. The hardware always knows what to do with sound. When it does not, it means the sound is something the facility never catalogued, which means the facility never found it, which means it has been here longer than the facility's survey teams bothered to go. He walks toward it. The current is lower here, ankle-deep, the channel widening where two drainage routes converge, and the trench silt begins at the east wall and deepens as he moves — the thermal trace showing the silt as slightly warmer than the water, metabolic processes in the sediment layer, the slow heat of eighteen years of freight runoff settling into whatever the channel floor used to be. A grate low in the west wall breathes up at him in long slow pulls — lake water and rust and machine oil off the flooded sub-basements below — and he passes through it and does not slow. He sees the amber before he sees the shape. One small LED cycling at the shoulder, maybe six feet from the channel wall, and the cycle is slow enough that the firmware running it is in maintenance mode, which means it is not running anything else, which means whatever is still running has been running it for a very long time on whatever charge is left.

[Beat 346]
The distance from Ashland-and-Whiting to The Pivot is three-quarters of a mile. Kyle walks it in twenty-three minutes, which is twice baseline. The shoulder catches at every step. The reattached fingers send small probing signals up both wrists that his Neuretics catalogues and does not act on. The blade rides at his right hip in the friction sheath because the back-carry is not available tonight. Somewhere in the middle of it there is a block that runs long — the stretch past the dead Cinderfall substation, where the streetlight comes at the wrong frequency and the hum arrives in the ferrocrete a half-beat before any sound he can name, and he is certain he has already passed the boarded transformer kiosk before he passes it. The junction box on the kiosk's flank leans the air toward it, and the hair on his forearm stands a half-second before his sleeve brushes the metal; inside the housing something small and many-bodied shifts and goes quiet. He files the wrongness the way he files everything tonight and does not investigate. He has been here. He has not been here. His Neuretics has no opinion. The rain comes down, redistributed, deliberate, the way it always comes down. He does not count the blocks. He does not count the steps. He has done enough counting tonight.

[Beat 347]
The Pivot's back room is lit on the low setting Pixel uses when she works late. The diagnostic array is powered down, the work surface long since cleared — she cleared it when she got the message, not when she heard the lift. She is standing at the counter with her hands still, not pretending to do anything else, and she turns when she hears the weight of the cargo arm on the threshold. She does not say anything about the wet, or the arm, or the blade in the borrowed grip, or the cauterizer bands that have not been changed since the facility. She has already done the math. She is waiting to see if it was right. He crosses the room.

[Beat 348]
She takes his hands.

[Beat 349]
The E.L.F. moves first.

[Beat 350]
The cleaver goes down and does not clear the radius cleanly and has to come up and come down again across the half-severed bone, and the hand falls onto the chair arm and rolls, palm up, and someone catches it, and his body does the thing his discipline does not catch in time — one full-body shudder, involuntary, a seismic event below the level of will — and the man with the cleaver, who has been on the receiving end of nothing tonight, takes one step back. *He felt that.* The cauterizer band on the left hisses longer because the stump is not as clean and the smell is worse this time, more of it, and Kyle is aware that his jaw is aching at the chrome bracket with a frequency that is now the most information-dense sensation still coming through a channel he can consciously access, and he holds onto it because it is the cleanest signal still running, and the cleanest signal is the one thing the discipline knows what to do with. The band model is visible at the edge of his peripheral vision as it seals across the stump: EMT-spec, the civilian first-responder model, blue stripe, fourteen-hour nerve cluster preservation window. The syndicate does not buy them for mercy. *That gives me fourteen hours.* He files it. He files all of it, in the part of himself that the facility built for exactly this — the part that treats catastrophic information as operational data, to be used later, assuming later.

[Beat 351]
*First*, he thinks, and the word does not belong to the tunnel or the sound or the approaching warmth. It belongs to a table, and a hand on a saya, and a debt already structured. He holds it where the facility taught him to hold the things that exceed bookkeeping — not gone, not suppressed, just *held* — and he turns north, into the dark, away from the sound.

[Beat 352]
He has been refusing that upgrade for eight years. Hua offered it again six months ago and got the same answer — a flat *no* and then silence, and she gave him the look she reserves for answers she has filed and found insufficient, and then she let it go, because Hua has learned over the years to let things go that she cannot yet move. He is not going to explain the arithmetic to her tonight. He keeps that arithmetic out of rooms with people who remember things.

[Beat 353]
The crocodile does not move. Kyle has been watching it for four minutes — his Neuretics timestamps involuntarily, a facility habit he never unlearned — and the animal has not moved once, its jaw resting on the heat rock with still the way only an old predator is still. He understands that. The tank glass is clean. Someone wipes it daily. That detail costs more than the crocodile.

[Beat 354]
The maintenance landing is three levels up — a poured ferrocrete shelf, six feet wide, with a cable trough running its length and a junction box at the north end that still has a status light. Green. Something in the Depot's infrastructure is still drawing power from this node, and the cable trough runs upward toward the south arm. On the lip of the trough a cat sits with its body squared toward the cable run and its head turned all the way around to face him, watching him climb; he never catches the head moving. Kyle stands on the landing with his right stump coupled to the cargo arm and his left stump bleeding slowly through the glass-seated seal and he says it aloud because the E.L.F. has not yet learned to read the question from inside.

[Beat 355]
She lets go. Not all at once — she releases his wrists in two stages, the right first, then the left, then her hands fall to her sides and stay there. She steps back. She does not wipe her face. She does not perform composure. She just stands in the hall with her tears on her own cheeks and on his cuffs and lets the moment exist.

[Beat 356]
He lies down. The blade across his chest does not pin him; it composes him. The hardware logs the bio-battery at thirty-one percent, the lateral array's three thermal stress markers active, the reattached hands at sixty percent grip and recovering, the right shoulder at fifty-seven percent function and worsening, the cargo arm absent from inventory, Silence present, Manifest present-and-quiet, the analgesic finding its plateau. He closes his eyes.

[Beat 357]
He wakes without a clock. The room is dark in the way of a room that has not decided whether to be day or night yet — no recycler exhaust angle, no streetlight frequency through the ferrocrete, nothing he can use to locate himself. Somewhere under the floor a grate breathes, slow and even, carrying up the smell of lake water and rust and machine oil from sub-basements he cannot place. He does not know where he is. He had not known when the autocab arrived. He had not known when it stopped. He knows now: somewhere that has no current events outside its walls, which means somewhere she chose for exactly that quality. The hands are at the ends of his arms. They are pink in the bracelet pattern at both wrists where the cauterizer bands held tissue against tissue while she worked, the seam clean, the perfusion finding its rhythm. He flexes the right hand. The fingers move. He flexes the left. Same. The grip is at sixty percent of baseline. Silence is on the side tray. The cargo arm is on the floor by the wall, shell-dark. He takes the blade. He leaves the cargo arm.

[Beat 358]
He picks up Silence.

[Beat 359]
*"Oh my god,"* she says, and the words are pressed through the same wet thickness as before, *"I'm sorry — does that hurt?"*

[Beat 360]
He stops on the far side of the table. Silence is between them, exactly arranged — saya toward her, handle toward the room's center, the way you leave something you plan to collect — and he brings the cargo arm up and lays the borrowed fingers across the saya, palm down, the way a man places his hand on a document to stop it from being filed. Hua looks at his hand. Then she looks at her own. Then she puts her real hand on the saya above his — one second, flat and deliberate, the principle of it — and the geometry the room has been waiting for closes: two unaugmented hands on the same blade, except his are in the chum barrel six feet away, and the hand on the saya is ninety pounds of Axiom industrial hardware and salvaged power cable and whatever is still resident behind his left eye, and hers is the kind of hand that does not need chrome because everyone in the room has always been afraid of it. She holds for one second. She lifts her hand.

[Beat 361]
*She does not know what his hands are. He is not going to tell her.*

[Beat 362]
The glass is at his foot. Was at his foot. He does not remember picking it up. The E.L.F. does not remember picking it up either — it is not in the arm's movement log, which means he did it, which means some part of him that operates below his Neuretics' timestamp threshold picked up a hand-sized shard of cargo crate glass while the arm was swinging and missed, and the glass is in his left stump now, end-on, the cauterizer band's residual heat conducting into the cut edge and the cut edge into his grip, which is not a grip because he has no fingers to grip with, but the glass does not fall. The heat holds it. He drives it up through the soft seam under the beak, through the loose connective tissue where the jaw plates hinge and the chitin sheath never grew, and the blade finds something arterial and opens it. Hot blood comes in three quarts and then continues coming. The mutant's hind legs go first. Then the front. The beak plates work twice more, air, and then stop. The shelf is dark with it. The smell is copper and rot and something pharmaceutical, whatever the Depot's stock was eating before it ate nothing.

[Beat 363]
The morning holds. The blood continues. He watches it find the low spot in the shelf and pool there the way water does — patient, without opinion. He does not move. He does not need to move yet.

[Beat 364]
It does not get louder. It gets *specific* — a shift in the harmonic, something in the frequency resolving toward signal — white noise becoming a voice. The status strip along the cargo arm's housing blooms: not the amber maintenance pulse, not factory-default, but a sequence that does not correspond to any initialization pattern in his Neuretics' archive. Kyle watches it. He does not reach for it. The wire comes out of the cable bundle at the arm's shoulder collar on its own — a maintenance lead, spider-thin, the kind a fixed pedestal would have driven in a service bay eighteen years ago — and it crosses the arm's housing slowly, without urgency, with the patience of something that has been here longer than the facility's survey teams were willing to go. It finds his left ear. It finds the auditory induction contact behind the lobe, the one that is part of the standard NeoCortex package, the one he has never let anything use from a non-trusted source, and it seats home with a click that he feels in his jaw at the chrome bracket. He lets it. He does not know why he lets it. He lets it anyway.

[Beat 365]
*The tank,* his Neuretics offers, dry, without affect, flagging the geometry the way it flags exits. *The animal has not moved.* Six goes down when the arm's elbow finds his temple. Seven goes down when Kyle drives the conduit pipe butt-end into his solar plexus and leaves him folded on the floor. Eight runs for the service hatch behind the tank — the one Kyle catalogued first, before the chair, before the table — and Kyle watches him go and does not pursue. He has left Hua there on purpose. He has left her there. He already knows what she will do. The room smells like copper and salt water and the particular ammonia of a disturbed crocodile enclosure. Silence is on the table between them, exactly where she left it, saya toward her, handle toward the room's center, the arrangement of something already appraised. Kyle stands with the cargo arm hanging at his side, the terminal fingers still holding the conduit pipe, and he breathes, and the arm breathes with him — the E.L.F. matching his rhythm in the firmware, the status strip dark, the trickle current almost gone. Hua's unaugmented hands are flat on the table edge. She is looking at him. She is looking at him the way you look at a ledger entry that has come back to dispute itself.

[Beat 366]
The door is not locked. He does not try the handle — the E.L.F. routes a pulse through the junction box in the door frame and the magnetic catch releases before his stump reaches the panel, and the door opens inward and he walks through it and does not stop walking.

[Beat 367]
The man in the rubberized apron lays the cleaver alongside the inside of his right wrist and adjusts the angle. The adjustment is small. Calibrated. The notch in the block accepts the angle the way a lock accepts a cut key, and Kyle understands in that moment that the notch was made by exactly this adjustment, repeated, until the wood remembered it. He does not look at the cleaver. His Neuretics is routing hard — pain channels partitioned, autonomic responses flagged for suppression, the hardware doing what the facility trained it to do, which is keep the operator functional — and Kyle is aware of all of this the way you are aware of your own heartbeat only when something threatens to stop it. Two men peel his fingers back from the chair arm. The fingers resist. Not from him. From the tendons, which have their own opinion about being opened flat against lacquered wood, and the men know this and compensate with the particular patience of people who have done this enough times to know where the resistance is. His palm goes flat. The wood is cold. *The wood is always cold by now.*

[Beat 368]
The arm reaches in without instruction — the E.L.F. already knows where they are, has known since the maintenance lead seated behind his ear, has perhaps known longer than that — and lifts his hands out palms up, exactly as she placed them. The twine is in his jacket pocket, scavenged from a cable bundle on the service landing, and the terminal fingers work it through the cauterized wrist channels with the patient articulation of something that has, for the first time in eighteen years, a reason to be careful. The knot is functional. Not clean. The cargo arm lowers the loop over his head and the hands settle at the center of his chest, palms outward, dripping onto the flagstone, and the weight of them is the weight of something he already knew was true. He turns toward the surface. He does not look at Hua. Silence is in the cargo arm's grip. He has not let go of it. Behind him, at her own table, in her own room, surrounded by her own men, Hua is alive and she said it, and the debt is open-ended by his choice and runs on his clock and has no ceiling he is prepared to name yet, and that is the worst thing he could have done to her, and he did it, and he walks toward the door.

[Beat 369]
Kyle sleeps. Pixel stands in her doorway for a long time, looking at the closed door across the hall, before she goes back inside her unit and shuts the door behind her without a sound.

[Beat 370]
The status strip on the arm's housing resolves. Not a sequence — a shape. He knows the shape. It is a stencil he has seen on Axiom freight containers, on loading-dock signage, on the documentation the facility used when it transferred equipment between its own sub-programs: a stylized bracket with a directional arrow inside it. *Salvage rights*, in the Axiom Industrial Logistics standard, before the standard was retired.

He looks at it for a long moment. The shape does not change.

The E.L.F. has been resident in him since the maintenance lead seated behind his ear. The cargo arm is not its body — the arm is a tool it picked up, an old home briefly borrowed. So the claim stenciled on the arm is not about the arm.

The salvage is Kyle.

He sits with that for a full count in the dark, and when he nods it is not agreement — it is the nod of a man who has understood something he is not yet prepared to say out loud. They keep climbing.

[Beat 371]
Then another. She is approaching him the way you approach a fence-pinned animal — slow, with the angle adjusted, no quick movement — and Kyle understands, in the time it takes her to cover the six feet between them, that she is going to touch him. He has time to step back. He has time to put up his right hand in the small gesture that has worked for four years to indicate *not now*. He does not step back. He does not put up the hand. His arms are very heavy and the AutoDoc's analgesic has not fully cleared and the night has been long. None of it was complicated. All of it cost, and he is so tired he cannot remember what he is supposed to be doing instead of letting her come.

[Beat 372]
*Pull away. Pull away. The convention is the convention.*

[Beat 373]
Her mouth opens. Closes. Opens again. The hands she values are flat on the table edge and they are shaking — not the tremble of cold or exhaustion but the specific frequency of a body that has decided something and has not finished deciding it — and she says: *Eighty —* and stops, and swallows, and says: *eighty-five thousand.* Kyle nods once. The arithmetic resolves. He says: *That is more than my life is worth. So now you owe me.* A pause, the length of one breath, hers. *Do you understand.* She nods and the nod has the quality of something irrevocable. The cargo arm does not move. The E.L.F. is very still in the firmware.

He screams: *SAY IT.*

The room goes quieter than the fight made it. The crocodile, somewhere in the dark behind the broken tank, goes still. Hua, who did not flinch at a cleaver, flinches. She says it. It comes out almost small: *I owe you.* The echo of it moves through the flagstone and the ferrocrete and the 19Hz hum that is still running under everything.

Kyle stands in the sound for one full breath. He presses his left stump against the cargo arm's housing until the pressure grounds him. Then the discipline comes back the way water comes back into a vessel that has been briefly tilted — not returning so much as resuming — and his voice goes flat and level and colder than the scream was hot: *I will come to collect. One favor at a time. Until I deem we are even.* He does not say *you do not get to deem it.* The only arithmetic that closes this debt runs on his clock, and they both know it, and only one of them has to say it aloud. Hua nods, smaller this time, the principle from a minute ago entirely evacuated from the gesture. The cargo arm lowers Silence to its side.

[Beat 374]
Kyle stands over it. The cargo arm holds the pipe at low ready, the terminal fingers resettled at the correct grip point, the E.L.F. quiet in the firmware now — not correcting, not compensating, just *present*, the way a second pair of hands goes still when the work is done. His right stump is still bound to the coupling at the shoulder. His left stump is bleeding through the cauterizer band where the glass has seated itself in the seal, and the glass is not coming out here because here is not where that problem gets solved. The bio-battery is at eighteen percent. He has not eaten since before the takedown. The fourteen-hour window has eleven hours and forty minutes left. He does the math the way he always does the math: one variable at a time, in order, the most time-constrained first. Then he says it aloud, because the E.L.F. is behind his eyes and it has not yet learned to read what he is going to do from the inside, and he is not yet willing to let it try.

[Beat 375]
A second tear lands. Then a third. They fall on both wrists now — she has not adjusted, she is just standing close enough that the geography works out — and each one sits warm on the pink for a second before the perfusion equalizes the temperature and the moisture wicks into the analgesic-thinned skin. Pixel's grip tightens on his wrists. Not hard. Carefully. The way you squeeze a thing you have been afraid of losing.

[Beat 376]
The status strip pulses twice. The E.L.F. is reading it — not the operational catalogue of temperature differential and perfusion rate and grip pressure — but something in a different register, something that does not have a procedure for itself. Kyle does not ask what it is registering. He does not want to know what it is making of this moment.

[Beat 377]
*Automaton, Industrial — Loader/Stevedore.* The classification surfaces from somewhere in his Neuretics' datasheet archive — the facility cross-trained everything, every piece of hardware the operators might encounter, because the facility's definition of encounter was broad. Axiom-era. Pre-Depot salvage cleanup, which puts the disposal date at eighteen years ago. The manipulator interface at the terminal end of the arm uses the Axiom industrial bus, and Kyle stares at the coupling for a moment in the amber pulse-light and something in the back of his processing recognizes it the way you recognize a face you have seen in a different context. The bus crosswalk is not in the surface tooling. It is not in any contractor's kit he has ever seen, because the contractors who work this tier do not carry it, because there is no commercial reason to carry it, because the only systems that use the civilian-derivative version of the same signaling layer are the systems that someone modified eleven years ago in directions that were never documented, in a facility whose name he does not say out loud. He has not thought about the modification in years. He thinks about it now. *This is going to work.* His Neuretics offers, without being asked, a procedure for separating the arm at the rotator coupling — step one, locate the primary torque fastener at the posterior shoulder plate; step two, apply lateral pressure at the distal end to break the corrosion seal; step three, route the integration handshake through the left optic thread interface — and Kyle reads the procedure the way you read a sentence you already know is true, and then he turns his back to the unit and fits his left stump against the posterior shoulder plate and begins to push.

[Beat 378]
Both at once. Both wrists, at the pink seam, palm up — the way you take something fragile that has just been put back together. Her hands are the same hands they always are: precise, minor scarring, the faint outline of the diagnostic port at the left wrist base where she has been calibrating his hardware for the last three years. The temperature differential is the part Kyle is not prepared for. Her hands are warm. The pink seam at his wrists is warm too — perfusion still finding its rhythm — but in a different register, a register that does not yet feel like *his*. Her warmth and his warmth meet at the bracelet line, and the part of his brain that has spent four years filing Pixel under *neighbor / tool / quiet across the hall* goes quiet and does not offer a correction.

[Beat 379]
The smell is chum and bleach and something underneath both that he is choosing not to identify. The channel widens at the junction — ten feet across, the current running in the deliberate pattern of municipal drainage design rather than flood geometry, which means he is in the old network now, the part built to spec rather than patched over. The E.L.F. raises the cargo arm to keep Silence clear of the water surface without being asked. Kyle registers this and says nothing.

[Beat 380]
Two more mutants in the next forty minutes. The first is on the maintenance ramp at the north-east junction, its heat signature arriving in his thermal trace before its sound arrives in the water, and the E.L.F. routes him left without consultation — a flicker in the spatial overlay, the ghost-motion in his shoulder — down a sub-level drainage cut that runs parallel to the ramp and rejoins the main channel forty feet north. He goes. The mutant's patrol cycle takes it south and then back north and then south again, the same arc, the same interval, and the E.L.F. has the interval memorized. The second is sleeping on a heat duct at the cargo lift shaft, its body temperature three degrees above the ambient, the chitin plates loose and expanded in the warmth the way armor relaxes when the wearer sleeps, and they pass it in the dark at a distance of thirteen feet and it does not wake. Kyle does not ask how the E.L.F. knew it was sleeping.

[Beat 381]
The sound is wet at the front — the dermis and fascia giving before the bone does, a compressed syllable of tissue displacement — and then dry at the back, a dense crack through the carpals and the distal ends of the radius and ulna, the bones offering more resistance than the flesh and then none at all, and then the arterial pulse comes, one strong systolic spray across the chair arm, and the fourth man is already moving, the cauterizer band coming flat across the stump before the second pulse, pressing hard into the meat, and the hiss is the sound of a wet steak hitting a pan that is already too hot and the smell that follows is pork. Kyle has smelled burning pork. He has smelled it in kitchens and in the field and once in the facility under circumstances he does not examine. The smell now is the same smell. His Neuretics has routed the pain off the conscious channel but the smell is not on a channel and the smell arrives through the back of his sinus cavity, which does not have a partition, and he smells himself burning. He files it. He does not look. He looks at the crocodile, which has not moved, and he files the smell, and then the man with the cleaver is breathing harder on his left side and his rhythm is different now — slightly off, the way a man's rhythm goes when his arms are tired and the adrenaline is metabolizing — and Kyle has time to think *the second one will be slower* before the block slides under his left forearm and the fingers go back and his palm goes flat.

[Beat 382]
Hua's hands are the first thing he catalogued when they brought him in, before the chair, before the table, before the long lacquered surface where Silence lay with its friction sheath unbuckled and set beside it like a deed already filed. Real skin. Real nails, cut short and unpainted. His Neuretics flagged them the way it flags fire exits and secondary egress points — as a structural anomaly in an environment where chrome is the baseline. In the Gray Zone, unaugmented hands mean poverty or principle. In this room, at this table, they mean neither. They mean she does not need to be faster than anyone in the room, because everyone in the room is already afraid of her.

[Beat 383]
Not cleanly. The terminal fingers close around the conduit at the wrong point — too far toward the bend, where the corrosion has widened the diameter and the grip cannot seat — and the E.L.F. corrects in the firmware, a half-second lag that Kyle feels as a stutter in his own shoulder, a ghost-motion that his nervous system reads as his and isn't. The pipe swings up to low guard. The mutant is on the shelf by then, the beak-plates grinding against each other as the jaw mechanism works the air, three-day decay wet under the chitin in the thermal trace — the patches where the sheath has split show hot and dark, the meat below still metabolizing, still running its own slow clock. Ten feet of it. The hind legs spread wide for purchase on the wet ferrocrete and the head drops and it lunges.

[Beat 384]
He woke before the city.

This was not unusual. What was unusual was that the first thing he did, before checking the time, before listening for the building's sounds, before his Neuretics ran its startup diagnostics — the first thing he did was open his right hand.

All five fingers. Full extension. Held it.

The reattachment seams were there if you knew where to feel for them — faint lateral ridges at both wrists where the AutoDoc's bonding medium had set overnight, still processing at the cellular interface. His hardware was running the reintegration protocol at a steady twelve percent of available compute, which meant the hand was technically not fully his yet, not in the firmware sense, and the precise grip strength on the right side was at ninety-four percent of the left. He flexed twice to confirm the reading and found that the instrument was correct and that he did not especially care. Close enough for a sword. Close enough for everything else.

[Beat 385]
In his Neuretics, something stirred.

Not a diagnostic. Not the reintegration protocol. Something else — an attention that was not entirely his own, oriented toward the window sill with the focused interest of an entity that found the pigeon there more interesting than Kyle did.

The pigeon was ordinary. This was the problem. It was a standard GLMZ roof bird, the grayish-brown variety that nested in the gutters of six-story walkups and ate whatever fell from the elevated gardens on the Spine. Kyle had seen this exact bird, or birds indistinguishable from it, every morning of his occupancy of 2F. Puppeteer had been in his Neuretics for less than twenty-four hours and had already determined that this specific bird was worth sustained observation.

*Interesting,* Kyle thought, in the direction of his own architecture.

*Yes,* the part of the Neuretics that was not Kyle agreed, and went quiet again.

[Beat 386]
He sat up. The window gave him the pre-dawn GLMZ in its characteristic state: the northern sky slightly lighter than the south, the elevated rail a dark line against the gradient, the maintenance drones doing their circuit checks while the street below was still in the low-traffic window between the last club crowd and the first delivery routes. The substation on the corner wore an Axiom Industrial collar on its main feed, the company thirty years dead and the mounting bracket still carrying current for whoever flew the flag this month — and this month the checkpoint two blocks east had gone Crucible green, which meant the gray zone had moved again and Kyle's own door was on a different side of someone's map than it had been last week. Somewhere in the building's infrastructure the climate system was running its morning adjustment. The smell of the building was concrete and the particular mix of recycled air and something organic from the vertical garden two floors up.

For a moment the light along the elevated rail sat at the wrong frequency — not brighter, not dimmer, just slightly off the color it should have been, the way the block south of the substation took eleven steps to cross when it should have taken nine. His Neuretics flagged a 19Hz signature under the deep concrete, the hum that arrived a half-second before the Pulse resonance it belonged to. Then the resonance came and the light settled and the math was nine steps again. Kyle noted it, filed it, did not look harder. Some mornings the city was just like that.

Silence was on the nightstand. He hadn't put it there — he'd put it on the side tray of the AutoDoc and not thought about it again until he was under anesthesia, and then Pixel had been there when he came back, and Pixel had brought the sword home, and she had set it on the nightstand and he had not moved it. He looked at it now. It was not looking back. It was a length of matte black meta-alloy with a saya that had been re-lacquered twice in fourteen years and had been on the table in Hua's chamber with someone else's hand on it.

[Beat 387]
He stood. The floor was cold through both feet — both — and he found that this was the kind of data that was worth noting.

Across the wall that was the wall of Unit 2E, there was no music.

Pixel ran music when she slept. He had learned this in the first weeks of their tenancy-overlap, the low bleed of sound through the shared wall that he'd eventually stopped registering as anything except confirmation that she was home and alive. It was the same wall that separated the landing from both their doors. It was always there when he came back to it. He had oriented on it in the trench, in the sluice water, in the AutoDoc with a foreign thing threading its architecture through his Neuretics — the wall and what was behind it. It had been there and then it hadn't, when the anesthesia took him, and then she'd been in the room when it came back.

No music now. First time in years that he could recall.

[Beat 388]
He put the kettle on.

While it heated he stood at his kitchen window with his right hand open against the glass, checking the seam lines by feel, running the flex protocol twice more. Somewhere below, the first wok of the morning came down onto a burner ring and rang like a struck bell, twice, and then stopped. His Neuretics noted that the pigeon had not moved. Puppeteer's attention, distributed across Kyle's sensor array in a way that felt less like intrusion than like a second pair of eyes he hadn't asked for, seemed to find this equally interesting. Kyle had not yet determined what to do about the E.L.F. in his architecture and was proceeding under the working assumption that the problem could be deferred until his hardware was running at full capacity.

The kettle was a manual pour-over. It was not the kind that whistled. He poured when the temperature read ninety-three on his Neuretics' thermal overlay, which was approximately the correct temperature for the coffee he made.

He made two cups.

[Beat 389]
He left his door open and stood in the kitchen and waited.

The pre-dawn city was doing what it always did — adjusting, recycling, moving freight and maintenance operations through the windows of low surveillance. From 2E, nothing. No creak of the workbench. No sound of bare feet on concrete. He would not knock. He had never knocked — she had always been the one to knock, or to open the door before the knock, and the convention was hers to change, not his. He put her cup on the counter in the same spot he always did when he made coffee and she wasn't here.

Puppeteer's attention came off the pigeon and settled, briefly, on the cup.

*I know,* Kyle said to the architecture.

The pigeon departed the sill without ceremony and the morning came in and the cup was there and the door was open and the kettle, already cooling, would stay warm enough for a while yet.

## Across the Hall

[Beat 390]
The Pivot sits two blocks past the Arcturus checkpoint that bleeds amber across the wet street all night, the booth that scans you for plate and intent and waves you through if you're nobody worth stopping, and tonight Kyle is gloriously nobody. He leaves the slug-rail hum of the gray zone behind him at the door — that low ferrocrete thrum the Pulse pushes up through everything this close to the lake — and trades it for the warmer ruin of home.

He makes it up the stairs of The Pivot on a banister and a bad idea, leaving a dark smear on the wall that he'll feel guilty about later. The second floor announces Pixel the way it always does — bass through her door at a volume that should bother the building more than it does, the clatter of something being tested and found wanting. Kyle stands in the hall with his hand pressed to his side, blood dark between his fingers, and he does the math he always does: knock, or bleed quietly and not be a problem.

He knocks. It comes out softer than a knock should.

The music cuts. The door opens on the chain, then all the way, and Pixel takes him in the way she takes in everything — fast, flat, already triaging. Pink hair half out of its updo, grey-blue eyes doing arithmetic on the spread of the stain.

"That had better not be yours," she says.

"It's mostly someone else's."

"Inside."

[Beat 391]
She clears the kitchen table with her forearm — a soldering iron, a half-flayed prosthetic hand, a mug — and none of it lands gently. Tools go to the floor and stay there. She doesn't look at them. She looks at him, and at the brace, and at the boot dragging, and the look on her face is the one she keeps for things that are still fixable if she's fast.

"Shirt," she says.

He gets it half over his head before the wound files a complaint, and she's there, peeling the rest of it off him like a bandage from skin, brisk, no ceremony. Then she stops being brisk.

She has seen Kyle hurt before. She has not, somehow, seen *this* much of him — the whole spread of it under the kitchen light. A long pale seam under his ribs, white with age. A burn that climbs his left shoulder, old and uneven.

"Stop cataloguing me," he says.

"Stop being interesting," she says, and reaches for the kit.

[Beat 392]
She works the way good medics work — talking to keep him present, hands too busy to shake. Antiseptic first. He hisses; she doesn't apologize. She irrigates the gash in his side, and her thumb braces flat against the muscle below it to hold the edges while she threads the suture, and he feels the small precise tugs as she closes him, one and then the next, her breath warm and uneven against his collarbone.

Somewhere in the middle of it her eyes have gone bright and overfull, and one of them spills, and the tear runs down and lands in the open wound. He doesn't tell her she's crying. She'd hate that. He just holds still and lets her finish, lets the salt find the raw of him, because there's a tenderness in being cried over that he has not let himself have in longer than he can name.

"You keep doing this," she says, not a question, knotting the last stitch. "Coming back in pieces. Making me put you back."

"You're good at it."

"That's not the part I'm complaining about."

[Beat 393]
She should be done. The wound is closed; the forearm is wrapped; the shoulder is taped. Her job, the named and reasonable part of it, is over. And she doesn't move away.

Her hand is still flat on his stomach where she braced to stitch him, and it stays, and then it doesn't stay still — it moves, slow, down the ridges and over the old burn and along the pale seam under his ribs, reading him the way her eyes read everything, except her eyes are closed now. She traces a scar from end to end like she's deciding whether to forgive it.

This is the part where Kyle pulls away. He always pulls away. There is a reason — fourteen years and every name he's carried and all the ways being found has never once gone clean — and it lives right here, in the half-second before contact becomes something he'd have to mean.

He doesn't pull away.

He puts his hand over hers and presses it flatter to him, and lets her feel that his heart is doing something undignified, and when she looks up — startled, the arithmetic finally failing her — he is already looking back.

[Beat 394]
She kisses him like she's been holding it under her tongue for a year, and he answers it with both hands in the pink wreck of her hair. She climbs into his lap right there on the kitchen chair, careful of the bad side and reckless with all the rest, and he pulls her sweater off over her head and doesn't track where it lands.

The last of the tools go off the table — screws skittering across the concrete and into the dark under the workbench — and neither of them flinches. Her hands find him everywhere they spent the last hour being clinical and aren't being clinical anymore. His mouth finds the freckled line of her throat and she makes a sound that she would deny making.

"Bed," she manages, "is eight feet—"

"Too far," he says, against her skin, and she laughs, breathless, and agrees with her whole body.

[Beat 395]
He stands with her wrapped around him — the side screams and he tells it to wait its turn — and they make it as far as the wall, which is closer and entirely sufficient. Her hand fists in his hair and she pulls him back in. Her fingers drag down his back and find new scars to learn. His hands close over the compact strong shape of her and pull her in tighter, palms moving slow across her ribs, her spine, the warm dip at the small of her back.

She gasps his name into the crook of his neck like it's something she's allowed to have now, and his breath goes ragged and stops sounding measured, and his shoulders drop.

They move together, against the wall and then down onto the cleared floor — his mouth at her collarbone, her hands flat on his ribs — until his hand finds hers on the concrete and holds it.

[Beat 396]
After, the floor is hard and they don't care.

She lies half on his good side, and the apartment ticks and cools around them, and the bass from someone else's unit thumps faintly through the brick. Down where the wall meets the floor, a thin green pulse runs the length of the baseboard seam and goes still, lumen mice settling back into the dark, and neither of them turns to look. His chest goes up and down too fast and then slower. Her pink hair is stuck to her cheek. There's a screw embedded in his shoulder blade that he'll find tomorrow and not mention.

She runs a finger along the contours of him — not triage now, not a medic's inventory, just the same practiced palpation she'd use to find what was broken, only this time pressed slow and asking nothing, learning a body she intends to come back to. The burn on his shoulder. The new line of her own stitches in his side, neat as a hem. The dip of his sternum.

He does the same to her, because for once his hands are not on a weapon — the freckles across her collarbone, the small old scar through one eyebrow, the strong unglamorous lines of the forearms that put him back together an hour ago. He traces her like she's a map to somewhere he might actually want to go. His thumb finds the pulse at the base of her throat and just stays there, not counting it, only feeling it move. She lets him. She watches him do it with her grey-blue eyes gone soft, and says nothing, which for Pixel is the loudest thing she owns.

[Beat 397]
"This can't happen again," Kyle says. He's looking at the ceiling when he says it, which is how she knows he doesn't entirely mean it.

Pixel props herself up on one elbow, forearm flat on the concrete, and considers him with the particular patience of a person who has just won an argument the other party doesn't know is over. She doesn't answer. She picks a screw up off the floor and turns it once in the low light. Then she sets it down.

His thumb is still at the base of her throat, where he left it. He doesn't move it.

## Hegewisch

[Beat 398]
Arcturus Civil Security — the CorpoSec stand-in for the law this city dissolved — comes at Kyle the way it always does when the invoice runs high: not one man but an entire kill team. Six of them, MilSpec reflex-ware threaded under the skin. Two matte SUVs fanning across the Hegewisch crossroads. A cadre of professional killers who do this for a salary and partial dental. They never even questioned why there was no name, just a description on their off-the-book kill contract.

The neighborhood reads the change in temperature. A noodle cart folds itself shut with a clatter of shutters. A kid on a stoop is hauled indoors by the collar. The street empties the way streets down here have learned to empty — fast, and without comment — until there is no one left standing in the middle of it but Kyle, his bike, and his bad options.

He'd known since morning that the day had a bad lean to it. At dawn he'd watched a flock of geese fly in formation but backward — beating north while they slid south, against themselves, against the air — Birds went wrong like that when The Weather was coming. He'd figured on a thin spot waiting somewhere up ahead. He had not figured on company.

His Neuretics returns its first assessment in a single flat word: Run. The bio-compute is grown around and through his brain and worked by thought alone, and most people run theirs to read their messages, scroll their socials, or drone out on slop. Kyle's is a long way past military grade, and even it wants nothing to do with this fight. He overrides it and drops into engagement mode, and it goes to work: cataloguing gait and stance and micro-weight shifts, triangulating muzzle angles from shoulder geometry alone, running threat-stack priority on all five remaining operators simultaneously, painting augmented target locks in colours only his optic nerve receives — and underneath all of it, reading the hundred small tells a body leaks before it fires, handing him the next half-second a hundred and twenty milliseconds before it arrives.

The team leader's finger takes up the slack on his trigger. Kyle is already moving when the first burst stitches the air he's no longer standing in. Cacophony — his five-shot revolver shotgun — clears its throat off his left hip, and a.410 slug rides an augmented-reality line only Kyle can see straight into the nearest operator, who folds around his own sternum and sits down hard in the road. Four left in the cylinder. Five shooters still up.

[Beat 399]
The team is good. They don't bunch and they don't panic — they peel back to the SUVs and start working the angles — and good is exactly the problem, because precognition against one trigger is a gift and against five at once is a budget, to be spent carefully and never refunded. His nervous system begins to cook under the load. A second slug catches the flanker breaking left, and the man drops mid-stride into a private argument with his own legs. A third punches clean through the door an operator is crouched behind and folds the steel in after it.

Then Cacophony runs dry on the fourth — the hammer falling on an empty chamber with a click he feels in his back teeth — and there is no time to swing the cylinder out and feed it fresh, so Kyle simply quits solving the problem with the gun.
