He reaches over his shoulder and draws Silence off his back. The katana is a matte-black length of steel-CNT meta-alloy, an edge that takes no light and offers no trick but the hand that holds it, and the hand has had a long time to practice. He crosses the open ground inside the half-second his Neuretics has bought him, slides under the muzzle before it can track, and takes the rifle out of the fifth man's grip at the receiver — leaving him disarmed, untouched, and loudly amazed to still be in possession of all ten fingers.

Steel for steel's problems and Cacophony for the rest: the whole of Kyle's philosophy of violence, and it has kept him breathing longer than the actuarial tables care for. Behind his eyes the overclock runs hot. In the corner of his mind's eye a small red light ticks over, the way it always does when he overdraws — a tally he stopped reading years ago — and the cost gathers somewhere out of sight, a debt he'll settle up later. A thin line of blood starts from his nose; he thumbs it off his lip without thinking, and the white creeps in at the edges of everything he can see. He ignores both, the way he always does.

Five down or out. One left — the leader, who has not moved this whole time, because the leader has been waiting.

[Beat 400]
The leader brought the solution built for men like Kyle, and he has only been waiting for the range to clear enough to use it. It is a Gauss rifle — a man-portable rail driver that throws a single hypersonic depleted-uranium dart along a line so flat it might as well have been drawn with a ruler, and cares not at all what happens to be standing behind the target. It is the one weapon Kyle's Neuretics cannot beat, because you cannot dodge a thing that is already past you; you can only try not to be standing where it was aimed.

He settles it on Kyle's chest and fires. Kyle reads the shot the breath before it leaves the barrel and is simply no longer there — and the dart, finding nothing but the air he abandoned, does not stop. It crosses the crossroads dead straight and punches through the windshield of an ambulance running its lights north, and kills the driver where he sits. With no one left to steer it, the ambulance drifts through its turn and meets a stalled hauler at fifty-two miles an hour, and the impact throws its back doors wide on their hinges.

Kyle is inside the leader's reach before the man can cycle the rifle for a second shot — one clean upward cut — and the Gauss rifle clatters loose to begin a quieter life on the pavement.

He straightens in the wreck-light and finds himself looking through the open bay doors of the ambulance at a small face looking back out at him, as though the universe had gone to a great deal of trouble to arrange the introduction. The small face lifts a hand and waves. Kyle waves back.

[Beat 401]
"Are you the new one?" the small face asks, and Kyle understands at once that this is not the question she is actually asking.

She's eight, maybe nine. Her chest is taped under a flexible biopolymer brace, and she is, by a wide margin, more composed than the one-armed second-tier corponation security officer bleeding out on the asphalt a yard to Kyle's left.

He steps up into the bay. The driver is dead. The medic slumped in the passenger seat is not coming back either. The patient tag clipped to the gurney reads IMANI OKONKWO-LINDQVIST — CRUCIBLE GENOMICS RECIPIENT — POST-OP, FIRST 6 HOURS — NORTHWESTERN MEMORIAL. Crucible Genomics prints organs to order, which means the kid is six hours into living on one of them. Kyle reads the tag twice. The math does itself.

"Hi, Imani," he says. "I'm Kyle."

"I know," she says. "My uncle says you're the one who doesn't stop."

[Beat 402]
He thumbs the emergency contact on the tag. It rings once.

"Vance," a voice says, already moving.

"Your driver's dead," Kyle says. "The kid's on the floor of a south-side ditch with a bioprinted heart wired to a battery. I have a motorcycle."

A pause on the line — not shock, recognition. "Kyle." Vance says it flat, the way you say the name of a problem you already know by heart. "Of course it's you."

"What's the brace reading?" Vance asks.

Kyle looks. "Sinus, ninety-two, paced."

"You've got sixty minutes before the vascular scaffold throws a clot," Vance says. "Maybe seventy."

Kyle does not hear the maybe. The same military-grade Neuretics that read the kill team a hundred and twenty milliseconds ahead of itself reads the brace now — the battery draw, the kid's color, the brute arithmetic of a six-hour-old heart — and hands back one cold number with no maybe anywhere in it. Sixty.

"Keep her calm," Vance is saying. "A new graft that races can tear right off the suture line. Those wires aren't there for decoration."

"Northwestern?" Kyle asks.

"Streeterville. Trauma Bay Nine. I'll have a slot waiting."

"I'm in Hegewisch."

Vance does the same math, almost as fast as Kyle's Neuretics had. "Then it's a straight line. North. Don't stop for checkpoints, don't stop for anything. And mind The Weather — there's a thin spot parked over 35th, has been since Tuesday, and your straight line runs right under it. If the read in your skull drops out, you're in it. Steady speed, eyes on a far light, and whatever you do, don't look at the edges."

"See you in a few," Kyle says.

Vance hangs up.

[Beat 403]
He lifts Imani onto the pillion, sidesaddle, and threads the medical harness through his belt loop so the battery rides snug over his kidney instead of swinging loose. He ties her wrists loosely through the back of his coat so she won't fall if she sleeps. Then he feeds Cacophony a fresh moon-clip — five new chambers to replace the five he scattered back across the Hegewisch crossroads — and lets it settle on his left hip. Silence rides across his back, where it always rides.

Her cheek finds the back of his shoulder like it has been there before.

"Hold on," he says, which is a stupid thing to say to a kid whose hands are too small to reach around his ribs, but it is what you say.

"How fast?" she asks.

"Fast," he says.

"Good."

The kid is the only new weight he's carrying, and the only one he is doing math about. The bike turns over and catches.

[Beat 404]
The first mile is gray zone. Hegewisch sprawl, no sovereign in residence, nobody to answer to and nobody to answer for you. A decommissioned delivery automaton kneels in a drainage forecourt at the corner, the rain of so many winters streaking its chassis chalk-white, and some child has tied a festival ribbon around one of its outstretched hands; it holds the ribbon the way it would hold anything, patiently, for as long as the ribbon cares to stay. It is the kind of country a freelancer learns by heart, because the seams are where the work lives; Stash had run a load of something through here last month and said the south stretch of Avenue O was clean if you took it fast. Kyle takes Avenue O at eighty. Two long rows of collapsed light poles lie across the road like felled trees, and his Neuretics flags them and lines up the gap before his eyes have finished arriving at the problem. Imani's breathing is a small warm patch against the back of his shoulder, even and slow and deliberately, carefully controlled.

"You're not scared," he says.

"I'm being efficient with my fear," she says.

Kyle almost laughs. Then doesn't.

"Where'd you learn that?" he asks.

"The lady who got me ready. She said to say it until it's true."

"Is it true yet?" Kyle asks.

Imani considers the question and answers it honestly. "No."

"Then keep saying it," he says. He does not tell her that it never quite comes true.

## Crucible Genomics

[Beat 405]
The checkpoint is two guards in soft-helmet armor and a third tucked behind a blast-shield, and all three wear the bored faces of men expecting a manifest. Kyle does not have a manifest. He has a child, a battery, and a smile, and he has learned that on the right night, presented with enough confidence, the three of them together pass for paperwork.

He brakes hard and throws both hands off the bars into the universal posture of a man holding nothing, and he gets the words out before any of the three can finish reaching for a weapon.

"POST-OP TRANSIT," he shouts. "LACUNA COOLER. NORTHWESTERN."

The left guard looks at the brace. The right guard looks at the cooler's logo. And the man behind the glass — Crucible internal, a white coat worn under his armor — pulls Imani's face up against a phenotype registry, because in a genomics zone the gate reads the person and never the plate. The bike is nobody here. It's the blood they care about. Whatever the registry hands back to him drains the color clean out of his evening.

"Let him through," he says. "Phenotype clearance."

The barrier sinks into the road.

"Have a great evening," Kyle says, and means every word of it.

[Beat 406]
A mile and a half of Crucible private artery — manicured, palm-lit, the river running black on the right. Kyle pegs the throttle. Workers in beige smocks glance up from the greenhouses and decide not to look long.

He works the geometry of it as he rides. The corporation has him tagged as a courier hauling outgoing inventory — the only reason the gate opened, and only good while he keeps moving. Nobody who wants what's wired into Imani's chest can follow him through here; they don't carry whatever got him waved in. So they won't follow. They'll call ahead. And the ones they wake on the far side won't need her breathing to collect, because a six-hour heart travels fine in a cooler.

He keeps moving.

"Why are the trees in rows?" Imani asks.

"Because the company likes to know where things are," Kyle says.

"That seems anxious."

Kyle has nothing to add. The kid is correct.

[Beat 407]
The north exit is a sally-gate — a slab of layered ballistic shutter that lifts for a scheduled manifest and nothing else, and Kyle's manifest was a lie he told a mile back. He does not slow down. His Neuretics reads the gate the way it reads everything, sorting the part that's load-bearing from the part that's only confident, and it paints the hydraulic spine where the shutter rides its track. Kyle draws Cacophony left-handed, thumbs the cylinder around to the breaching load, and puts one round exactly there.

The actuator gives up its entire career at once. The slab jams a third of the way up, shrieking on a dead track, and Kyle flattens to the tank and takes them under it with inches to spare — close enough that the cold edge of the shutter combs the air over his spine. They drop off Crucible's glass-smooth pavement onto the broken asphalt of the gray zone, and the suspension finds out about Chicago all at once. Imani laughs. Small, clean, surprised out of her — a tuning fork struck somewhere behind his ribs.

"Loud part," she says.

"Told you," he says.

## The Pullman Seam

[Beat 408]
Pullman Works glows up on the right — restored row houses, modern cameras hidden inside period housings, an Axiom company town keeping perfect time for people who don't own a single minute of it. Kyle threads the gray-zone seam along its edge, the unincorporated strip Axiom never bothered to fence, the kind of line he has spent a career learning to ride. Where the factory rain had dried, the pavement had gone soap-slick, and he fed the bike a little less lean through the stretch without thinking, the way you learn to trust a road that won't hold you.

Heads come up as the engine passes. One kid shouts something the engine swallows whole, but the shape of the word in his mouth is unmistakable — legend — and Kyle lifts a hand off the bars like a man in a parade.

Other heads come up too, and these ones don't spare a look for the bike. They're fledglings: pledges to the Vultures, the crews who scavenge the dead and the not-quite-dead, the ones who'll cut a transplant back out of your chest the day you miss a payment and sell off whatever's left of you for parts. A fledgling runs salvage on spec — no patch, no blessing — hoping to drag something home worth a permanent place in the flock. A post-op transplant riding pillion through open country isn't a target to a kid like that. It's a winning lottery ticket. The exact score that turns a fledgling into a Vulture. Two of them are already on their bikes. Already on their phones.

[Beat 409]
Imani's hand finds the side of his coat and clutches a fistful of it. She isn't afraid, Kyle realizes. She's absorbing. She has never once been outside Crucible's walls in her life, and he understands this about her without being told.

"First time off the block?" he asks.

"First time on the ground," she says.

"Well," he says. "Buckle up."

"Buckle up to what?" she asks.

"Existentially," he says.

She thinks about that one for a long moment, the city pouring past on both sides of her.

"Okay," she says.

## Ashgrave Corridor

[Beat 410]
The Ashgrave Synthesis Corridor announces itself with a wall of haze you can taste a block before you can see it. The perimeter lights glow the wrong color — too yellow, too low — the way a refinery glows when it is having a feeling about something. This is somebody's commute. A shift-change line of workers in cheap half-masks trudges the shoulder, heads down, lunch pails swinging, walking a thing they made their peace with a long time ago, because the only alternative on offer was not eating. A stall at the mouth of the corridor sells filter cartridges, and beside the cartridges a hand-painted board keeps a tally — DAYS SINCE — the number low, the way the number is always low here.

Kyle has a respirator clipped to his jacket. Imani does not. His Neuretics throws the air up in front of his eyes as a number he doesn't like and a clock he likes a great deal less. He reaches back without slowing, finds her face with the flat of his palm, and presses his bandana over her mouth.

"Breathe shallow," he says. "Through this. Not your nose."

She nods against his hand. A masked worker on the shoulder lifts two fingers as the bike passes — not a wave, just the nod one night-shift gives another across a bad stretch of road — and Kyle returns it. Then he bites down on his own mouthpiece, snaps the seal, and rides into a sky the color of an old bruise like he was personally invited.

[Beat 411]
Two refinery towers exhale orange fire on either side of him, close enough that he could count the rivet patterns if he had a second to spare. Sixty feet up, a vent stack blows downward all at once, and the pressure slams sideways across the road. The bike yaws hard. He corrects before the yaw has finished happening, because the correction was already waiting in his hands a half-second before the problem arrived. Imani's grip tightens into a small steel claw at his belt.

"We're fine," he says, out loud.

"I know," she says through the bandana. "I can tell from your shoulders."

Kyle has met grown adults — armed, paid, supposedly professional — who could not read his shoulders. He is starting, against every instinct he owns, to take this kid personally.

[Beat 412]
Halfway through, the corridor's throat begins to close.

A leak somewhere upwind trips a containment lockdown, and Ashgrave's answer to a leak is a blast-rated bulkhead coming down across the only open lane on a dead-man timer — slow as a guillotine and about as open to negotiation — built to seal whoever's caught on the wrong side of it in with the very air they were trying to outrun. This is not the sally-gate. There is no actuator to find and shoot, no override, and no time to look for one, because a hydraulic spine already in motion is a different animal than one sitting at rest. His Neuretics tells him so before he's even finished forming the question.

He can't beat the door. He can only beat it there.

He drops two gears and pins the throttle, and the read in his skull measures the shrinking gap against the height of a man lying flat on his tank with a child folded down his spine, and hands him back a number with no margin anywhere inside it. He takes the number anyway. Something behind his eyes gives a warning lurch for the one full second he leans that hard on the read — the body presenting its invoice in real time — then steadies.

They go under the falling edge at speed. The lip catches the battery brace and shrieks down the entire length of it in a fan of orange sparks — a hand's width higher and the night ends here, with both of them — and then the bulkhead seats home behind them with a boom he feels in the floor of his chest. Through. He throws one look down at the brace. Still green. The cooler held.

Imani isn't laughing this time. Both fists are buried in his coat, her face pressed flat between his shoulder blades, and she has gone very, very quiet. Kyle files that away with the other things tonight keeps teaching him about her.

[Beat 413]
He clears the corridor's north ramp at a hundred and ten, rolls off the throttle on the way down the far side, and spits the mouthpiece out so it swings free on its lanyard. Reaching back one-handed, he tugs Imani's bandana down off her face.

"You with me?" he asks.

She coughs once, twice, and then says, "I'm with you. Your shoulders said we were okay."

"My shoulders are working overtime," he says.

"Give them a raise."

Kyle laughs — a real one, surprised out of the bottom of him — laughs so hard he nearly loses the line.

## Englewood

[Beat 414]
Englewood at night looks like a city that has been told a joke nobody is laughing at. Kyle means to run 63rd straight across to Halsted, but 63rd dead-ends in a viaduct that finished collapsing sometime back in the spring — a long slope of broken deck and bent rebar where an underpass used to carry the road through. No way over it the easy way.

There is a hard way. A tilted slab the locals have braced and shimmed into a ramp, because in a gray zone every disaster eventually gets repurposed into infrastructure by the people who have to live on top of it. He knows the ramp is sound because Echo had run a job out this way a season back and tapped him the wave-map after, the whole broken shelf of Englewood charted in clicks and echoes the way only Echo bothers to chart anything. Some debts in this trade get paid in roads you'll never have to learn the hard way.

He takes it at speed. The bike goes light at the crest, weightless for one long held breath, Imani's battery thudding once against his kidney — and they land hard on the far side with thirty-four minutes left on the clock and a fresh ache settling in behind his teeth. Two headlights take the ramp a beat behind him. Then a third. His Neuretics tags them, ranges them, and tells him the single useful thing there is to know about them: they are matching his speed on purpose.

He does the single useful thing in answer. He goes faster.

[Beat 415]
They come in a flock this time — the ones the phones woke, fresh bikes and no patience between them, fanning out across both lanes to take him at speed. The lead pair don't reach for guns. They reach for their belts and throw, and the air ahead of him fills with fat little cylinders that burst into white glare and dead static. Flashbang. Chaff. And the ugly third kind — the EMP-and-interference rounds a salvage crew carries to kill a mark's bike out from under him and blind whatever he's running behind his eyes.

It is a good plan. It would work on almost anyone alive.

Kyle's bike stumbles, coughs, catches, and keeps running on hardened ignition. And the read behind his eyes never so much as flickers — because a military Neuretics is shielded against a great deal more than static. Against EMP. Against the whole spectrum of battlefield interference. And against the low, wrong pressure at the very bottom of the band that would fold a civilian rig clean in half. You do not harden a mind against that last thing unless you already know, for a fact, that it is out there waiting. Kyle has never told a living soul that his is. Where a pack of fledglings came by rounds that carry it is a question for a later night. He stops the bike — because running is the one thing they are built and ready for, and standing still is the one thing they are not.

He picks the narrow place under the 63rd Street viaduct where the lane necks down between two dead cars, thumbs the bike into auto-balance, and feels it take its own weight, the gyros holding it upright and patient at a crawl with the kid still aboard.

"Don't move," he tells her.

Imani goes still as furniture.

Then he steps off into the road and draws Silence off his back.

The first one commits to his swing — a length of chain, whipping flat — before he understands that the geometry of the whole fight just changed. Kyle reads the arc early, the way he reads everything, and simply isn't quite where it lands. The chain's tail still opens a thin red line across his forearm, the tax for standing in this close; he takes that, and on the same motion plucks the kid off his saddle, shallow and deliberate, and cuts the bandolier of grenades off his chest, and tosses it back underhand.

"Hold these."

Imani catches it against herself like a schoolbag.

The second one tries to simply run him down, which is how the second one comes to study the asphalt at close range. The third has the sense to draw a pistol — and that is the moment the Neuretics paints the thing no briefing can ever quite sell you: the shot itself, a full beat before the trigger finishes pulling it, a hard red line laid from the muzzle straight through the center of Kyle's chest.

So Kyle takes his chest off the line. He drops his weight back off his heels, far enough to feel the round part the air just above his sternum and travel on to find the fourth fledgling — who had done everything right tonight except stand directly behind the man he was hunting. The shooter stares at what his own hand just did. Kyle relieves him of the pistol, and of any further interest in the fight.

The rest of the flock runs the math and doesn't care for the product. Two peel away north and don't look back. The ones already on the ground will walk home when their legs come back around to the idea.

Kyle swings onto his bike — still upright, still warm, still holding Imani exactly where he left her — kills the balance, and lets the weight settle into his hands like something he was born holding. Imani is looking down at the bandolier in her lap.

"Are these ours now?" she asks.

"Until some bigger kid wants to try and take them," Kyle says, threading her free hand back through the back of his coat.

She thinks about that, and finds it fair — which is, Kyle suspects, the most truthful thing anyone has ever bothered to tell her. He brings them back up to speed, and for the first time since Pullman the mirrors stay empty behind him. They are done being chased.

## The Cinderfall Anomaly

[Beat 416]
Halsted north of the tracks runs straight over the bones of Cinderfall Energy — eleven subterranean thermal installations whose only face to the world is a grid of steam-vent grates and the convection waves that ripple up off them, bending the streetlights into water.

"Why is the air doing that?" Imani asks.

"Because the corporation underneath us is cooking," Kyle says.

"Oh." Then, smaller, with all the cleverness gone out of it: "I don't like it here."

"Me neither," Kyle says, and doesn't dress it up, because that one is simply true.

He does not tell her that the air is about to do something Cinderfall has nothing whatsoever to do with. With the road behind him finally empty, his Neuretics has nothing left to track but what waits up ahead — and two blocks on, the streetlights stop agreeing with one another about where they stand, and the read in his skull, which has had a clean answer ready for everything tonight, goes quiet. Quiet in the one specific way that means only one thing.

The Weather, Vance had called it.

[Beat 417]
He should turn west. West is Bridgeport, and Bridgeport is normal. He goes straight, because straight is what the call is for.

At 35th and Halsted the air changes temperature with nothing anywhere to account for it — a dry, settled cold, and underneath the cold the smell of a room sealed shut since before he was born — and the world quietly stops being a place. The headlight reaches out and touches something, and the something touches back. The world dims at its edges. His stomach drops a full foot without taking the rest of him along for it; the wheels are still on pavement, but the pavement is running half a second behind where his eyes swear it ought to be.

And then comes the worse thing. The thing the briefings never sell hard enough.

He knows this corner.

He has never once been here, and he knows it anyway — knows it the way you know a hallway from a dream — one you have had your whole life and never once on purpose.

Behind him, Imani makes a sound he has never heard a child make. Not fear. Not pain.

Recognition.

[Beat 418]
It has a name on the forecasts and an exclusion flag on the maps. Kyle knows the names are a courtesy. Whatever this is, it isn't bad air and it isn't radiation. It is faintly and patiently aware of him.

His Neuretics tries to map it and gets back nothing but garbage: distances that won't hold still, a clock running three directions at once, his own engine note reaching his ears a half-second late, off a street he's already left. So for once in his overclocked life he flies on the one instrument no build has ever managed to improve — the old animal at the base of the skull.

He fixes on a single streetlamp out on the far rim and keeps the only rules anyone keeps and survives. Constant speed. Eyes on the light. Don't look at the edges. Don't trust a turn — because in here, right is sometimes left, and left is sometimes down.

"Don't look left," he says.

"I already wasn't," Imani says.

[Beat 419]
At the far edge of his vision something moves that shouldn't be able to: a shape carrying one shadow too many, and the extra shadow arrives a half-beat ahead of the shape that throws it. Where it passes, the air goes cold — a cold with no source — and it makes no sound at all, just a pressure in the ear like water at depth.

He does not look at it directly. Some animal floor of him knows that looking straight at the thing is not something a mind comes back from. Imani's breath catches against his back — his only warning — and he throws the bike right, which is the wrong word for the direction it actually goes. The miss should have cleared a foot. It clears less than an inch. He feels it pass like a current turning in deep water, and a line of cold opens along his ribs under the coat — deep, clean, not yet bleeding, because in here the blood runs half a second behind everything else.

Then the far lamp swims up close and steady and yellow, and the pavement grabs back at his tires all at once, and the engine note collapses into itself and becomes an engine again. The cold line under his ribs — the place it passed within an inch of — burns now, properly, the way real wounds do when the blood catches up to the moment. He does not look back. He rides.

And behind him, the borrowed calm finally fails her. Imani comes apart — all the way apart, the way an eight-year-old is built to — sobbing into his back, both small fists twisting in his coat. "It knew my name, it knew my name, I want to go home, I want to go home." And the brace at his kidney picks it up and starts to chirp, fast and climbing, because a six-hour-old heart was never built to run this hard. That, and not the thing back in the dark, is what can kill her now.

Keep her calm, Vance had said.

Kyle has no calm left in him and no time to counterfeit any, so he gives her the only thing he's got — his voice, flat and low and boring as a readout, naming the next ordinary thing and the next. The bike. The bridge coming up. The loud part she likes.

"You're out of it. It's behind us. It can't come where we're going."

The chirp slows. Slows again. Steadies. By the next block she has herself back, mostly, in the wrung-out way a kid does after the worst of it has finally let go.

"You're hurt," she says, very quietly.

"I'm fine," he says.

It is the first time he has lied to her.

[Beat 420]
Twenty-one minutes, and the mirrors have stayed empty since the viaduct. What he hasn't lost is the Loop — the one part of the city no corponation gets to switch off, too public and too packed, the heart every zone plugs into to stay alive. So when it goes wrong, it goes wrong in the open. Tonight it is going wrong.

He hears it from the mouth of the tunnel: the flat clap of small-arms under a crowd-roar. Imani lifts her head off his back.

"Is that the loud?" she asks, a thread of the thin spot still in her voice.

"Different loud," he says. "Just people."

The long way costs eleven minutes he's already bleeding through, so he goes in. Halfway down the cathedral dark, a corporate security line has a municipal picket pinned behind an overturned cleaning rig — both sides too far gone to clock a motorcycle until it's the most interesting thing in the room. Kyle reaches back and Imani knows without being instructed to hand him a flashbang; he launches it with sword arm. It strikes the ceiling and goes off. The tunnel goes white, and he rides the median straight through the blind seconds — and one of those frightened people gets lucky. A round furrows the meat across the top of his shoulder, a hot graze that nearly tears the gun from his hand. He keeps the throttle. Adds the shoulder to the rib. Behind him Imani watches the rivers of light pour past overhead, not the blood she can't see.

"It's beautiful," she says.

Kyle gets his breath back around the burn before he answers, so it lands level.

"Don't tell the advertisers," he says. "It'll go to their heads."

[Beat 421]
River North is a thirty-second wedge of gray zone where the Loop's grid loses its nerve at the river. The bascule bridges are still there, but the river hasn't been managed in eighteen years; the lift machinery seized a decade back, and the spans sit permanently down and half-drowned, Lower Wacker a black canal beneath them where a road used to run. People live here anyway — laundry strung span to span, a man fishing the canal off a fixed deck with a hand line, a kid working a cooler of something cold down a line of cars that never moves. Down the throat of a stairwell that ran below the waterline, a crack in the wall gave off a faint blue-green light, the way the oldest blocks do once they've been wet long enough; the air that came up out of it was cold and close and smelled of a cellar that had made its peace with the river, and nobody passing it down here took that for anything but a comfort.

Imani turns her head to watch the water slide past underneath. "The street's drowned," she says — not scared, just filing it.

"Most of them down here are," Kyle says, and takes the upper deck of the Michigan Avenue span, where Tollgate Systems strung a credit-gate across a public ruin because someone always finds a way to charge rent on a thing nobody owns. He doesn't have a Tollgate badge. He has twelve minutes and an opinion about Tollgate. The barrier is a swinging steel boom; it folds off its hinge without comment, the least dangerous thing to happen to him all night.

What he thinks about instead is the camera reading his face across the span. Not the bike — the bike is nobody — it's him the system wants, and it bills the only identity it can match: a credit account that hasn't existed since 2207, one he built for exactly this kind of evening and then deleted. The invoice will draw an automated rejection, the rejection will be filed and archived and purged in a routine sweep — six weeks and seventeen credits of Tollgate's money chasing a ghost. He laughs, and it pulls at the cold line under his coat.

"Why are you laughing?" Imani asks.

"Because the most expensive thing I did to them tonight was the paperwork."

"Good," she says.

## Streeterville

[Beat 422]
Streeterville is a forest of glass towers wearing crowns of advertising, and at their feet sits the brutalist concrete slab of Northwestern Memorial. Tonight the whole district is packed wall to wall with people — the strike spilled north into the one part of the city nobody is allowed to lock down. Which is, of course, exactly why the strike chose to be here.

Kyle picks out the bay's red light six blocks out. And between him and it, wading through the crowd on six legs with the patience of a thing that has nowhere better in the world to be, is an Arcturus crowd-control unit. A crab the size of a panel van, matte riot-grey. A Bulwark. Arcturus builds everything into a crab eventually — low, omnidirectional, impossible to tip over — and a crab, it turns out, is what has been waiting at the end of his night all along.

Kyle has put this exact unit down twice before. He knows it's the same one because it slows the instant its optics find his face, and recalculates, and comes for him first out of a crowd of a thousand easier problems.

A machine can't hold a grudge. Kyle stopped believing that the second time.

[Beat 423]
The Bulwark is a crowd-control unit, built to work shoulder to shoulder with civilians, so it carries nothing that goes bang — no slugs, no rockets, nothing that doesn't care where it comes down. What it has instead is hands. Tanglefoot. Kinetic foam. A sonic throw that turns your inner ear to surf. And four hydraulic claws built to fold a person up neat and hold them there for collection. It wants Kyle folded up. It does not care about the kid at all, except as part of the same package.

That is the opening. It is a thin one.

Kyle frees Imani's hands from the back of his coat without setting her down — she stays on the pillion, she stays with the bike — and pulls two fat cylinders from the bandolier she's carried since the viaduct. The fledglings' own flash and chaff. Finally good for something.

"It sees in light, and it hears in sound, and it can't shoot back," he says. "Left one's flash — that blinds it. Right one's chaff — that makes it deaf and stupid. Pull the tab, count two, throw it at the face."

Imani looks at the crab the size of a panel van. Then Imani looks at the two grenades in her own two hands.

"It's a really big crab," she says, in the voice of a kid who has just been handed the best and worst job of her entire short life.

"It is," Kyle agrees. "Throw at the face."

[Beat 424]
Then everything is very loud and very fast at once.

Imani's first flash goes off a half-second early and a yard wide — and the Bulwark flinches from it anyway, optics whiting out — and Kyle is already moving, not away from the thing but under it, because the one place a crab cannot reach is the spot beneath its own belly. He lays the bike flat and slings the two of them through the gap between its legs, drawing Silence as the world tips sideways, and opens a leg out clean on the way through. The Bulwark drops a quarter of its height with a sound like a building deciding to sit down.

Then, for the first time all night, the read fails him.

Whatever the thin spot did to him at 35th, the overclock picks now to cash the bill. The light in the corner of his eye flares red, and the half-second he's leaned on since Hegewisch just doesn't come — and his body, built to arrive early, arrives late instead. The world greys at the centers. His knee buckles half a step. For one full second he is only a tired man with a sword, slow as everyone else in the world.

So he never sees the claw. It whips back over the fallen flank, closes on the bandolier across Imani's chest, and tears it loose — lifting her half a foot into the air before the strap gives and drops her hard.

Something bigger had wanted them. Exactly the way he told her it would.

But she'd pulled the chaff's tab a heartbeat before it took her, and the two-count runs out now — point-blank, in the crab's own grip, bursting in the cluster of sensors the claw reached out with. The legs stop agreeing with one another and start hunting for a floor that isn't there.

Kyle gets her down behind the engine block and puts himself between her and the machine, and finishes it the slow way — on grip and memory and the bare animal at the base of the skull, with nothing out in front of him at all. A claw widens the cut on his forearm. The bad rib tears on a blind pivot. He eats both, because he never saw them coming. That is the difference. The Bulwark folds into the intersection — blind, deaf, beached, legs working and going nowhere. The bike does not survive the dismount.

But the bay's red light is three blocks off now. And three blocks is nothing.

"Good count," he tells her.

Imani — shaking, fierce, wrung all the way out, staring at the claw that took her grenades and choked on them — says, "I know."

[Beat 425]
The bike is dead and the bay is three blocks off, and three blocks is nothing; he has bled a great deal more than this for a great deal less. He sheathes Silence across his back. Holsters Cacophony on his hip. And picks her up — harness, battery, the whole small weight of her gathered in against his chest.

Imani's hand fists in his collar. "You're hurt," she says again, and this time he doesn't bother with the lie.

"Yeah," he says.

He carries her. A red light in the distance growing larger and brighter.

[Beat 426]
Vance is already out in the lane with the gurney — tall, brown-skinned, hair tied back, scrubs hanging off a body that has clearly run a long way today — and he does not waste a single syllable on hello.

"On the gurney."

Kyle is already lowering her onto it. The harness comes off in two practiced motions. Imani's hand stays knotted in his coat a half-second after the rest of her is down, and when he gently pries it loose, she asks him the question every kid eventually has to put to somebody.

"Will I see you again?"

Kyle does not lie to her. He has been lied to by kinder people than himself, and it never once helped.

"No," he says. "Probably not."

She takes it the way she has taken everything else tonight — straight, no flinch, filing it away somewhere. Good, he thinks. Better she learns it now, while she's still young enough for the scar to set smooth: that sometimes the person who comes for you is a stranger, who does the thing, and then is gone. He learned it late himself. It set crooked.

"Thank you for the loud," she says.

"Anytime, kid," he says — the gentlest lie he has told all night, and the only one she lets him keep. "Tell the heart I said hi."

[Beat 427]
Vance reads the brace, the wires, the cooler — and then reads him. The hand pressed flat to his side. The dark spreading at the forearm and the shoulder. The boot that won't quite take his full weight.

"Forty-one minutes," he says. "I had you at fifty-five."

It's an old argument between the two of them. He has put Kyle back together before, more than once, and they both already know exactly how the next part of this goes.

"I cheated," Kyle says.

"You're bleeding into my ambulance lane," Vance says. "Stay. Half the people in my bay tonight are in better shape than you are."

Kyle is already turning away. "Don't worry about me," he says. "I'll just rub some dirt in it."

[Beat 428]
He walks.

The bay door drops shut behind him and its red light goes white, which is the hospital's quiet way of saying they have her now — which is the only confirmation he was ever going to get that the run worked. No alarm follows him out into the street. No name. Whatever he tore open tonight to get her here has already closed up seamless behind him, like it was never there at all.

[Beat 429]
He turns south, on foot, the long way, because south is no longer urgent and there's no bike left to make it quick. The read has come back — slow, shaky, but back. The white that rode the edges of his sight all night is pulling away, the bleed at his nose gone tacky and dry. The body, at least, has stopped sending up its bills for the evening.

The bad news keeps its own count. Where a civilian rig would show a battery bar, the build in his skull shows one small light. Every time he runs the read past the red, that light goes red with it.

He doesn't look at it while he's working. He looks now, and counts what he can stand of tonight's — and the thin spot alone is most of it. He files the rest under the cost of the evening, starting with a name he still doesn't have. A kill team that never asked why the contract carried only a description and no target name, on a job too clean to have a careless human anywhere behind it — no listed broker, no client that would survive a second look, just a shape that fit together a little too well. He has been seeing that shape on the boards for months. He does not say the word for it yet, even to himself. He walks on.

He names the next ordinary thing, and the next. The curb. The corner. The long block south. Until the street is just a street again.

## Half-Rate

[Beat 430]
Sable sends the contract the way she sends the ones she doesn't like — short and flat, with no markup on it, which is about as close as Sable ever comes to saying she's sorry. There's a name, a tower, a number, and the word AXIOM, and that last one is a weather report all by itself.

The granddaughter meets him under the Ashland viaduct, because there is nowhere else she can think to bring a man like Kyle that isn't simply a stretch of sidewalk. She is maybe twenty, with two jobs sitting in the set of her shoulders, and she pays him with a credstick warm from being held too long, its balance ticked down to the last decimal — Φ300, half his usual rate, and she checks the readout twice, the second time more slowly, as though the number might have grown larger if she only gave it the chance.

"They're sunsetting the Roost," she says.

Kyle already knows; everyone in the Glooms does. Axiom filed the clause a full season ago and posted the notice in a typeface no one who actually lived there could be expected to read.

What she means is floor eighty-four, and what she means by that is her grandmother — the one who has not answered her comm in two days, who will not take the buyout, and who will not leave.

"The power comes off the top first," the granddaughter says, "floor by floor, and it's already past ninety." She works through the arithmetic she has plainly been doing all night. "She's got maybe an hour of lights left. After that the lifts stop reaching her floor, and then the men come."

Kyle pockets the stick without checking the balance again, because reading it in front of her would be its own small theft.

"What's her name?" he asks.

"Comfort," the granddaughter says, and her face does something complicated around the word. "Mrs. Okwuosa-Brandt. She won't come down for me — she told me, specifically, not to send anyone." She pauses. "So I sent someone."

"Yeah," Kyle says. "You did."

## Vertical Asset 9

[Beat 431]
The Roost is a hundred stories of public housing that Axiom bought for the land beneath it and the column of air above it; they have been planning to take what was in between for a very long time, and it seems that their time has finally come. Its people named it for the pigeons. Axiom's filings call it Vertical Asset 9, which is the sort of name you give a building only after you have already decided to stop heating it.

Tonight you can watch the clause doing its work from the street. The top of the tower has gone dark — eight or ten floors of black windows where there were lights only last week — and the darkness has a clean lower edge to it, a tide line that descends one room at a time. Below that line the building still glows, crowded and stubborn, and every lit window is a person who has decided that the floor of a corporation's patience must lie somewhere lower than this. At the curb a storm grate breathes up at him in slow exhalations — lake water and rust and machine oil, warm against the cold coming off the street, the breath of something underneath the city that keeps its own hours.

Kyle reads it the way his Neuretics reads everything — the bio-compute grown through his skull and worked by thought alone, no jack required — sorting the part of the scene that is load-bearing from the part that is merely confident. It counts the lit floors, measures the rate at which the dark is coming down, and hands him a number he does not much care for: the cut will reach eighty-four at roughly the moment he does, assuming nothing slows him down, and something always slows him down.

Down at the base, the scavengers have already gathered — not the building's own people, but the other kind. Vultures, and the fledglings who would like to become Vultures, because a hundred floors of old bodies kept alive on financed organs, all of it about to be relocated into a gray zone that keeps no records, is not a tragedy to them so much as a clearance sale. Two of them watch his bike roll in and price it without much interest; it is the cargo they are waiting on, not the machine. One is telling the other about a board posting from earlier in the week — a salvage run on this exact tower, listed clean, the operator field blank where there is always a name, the client resolving to a shell that resolved to nothing when he chased it. Came from nowhere, the fledgling says, half spooked and half delighted, like the building hired itself. Kyle catches it on the way past and adds it to the count: four times this season he has heard a job described that way.

And between the scavengers and the door stands an Arcturus relocation detail — Civil Security, the closest thing this city still keeps to law, which is to say a service corporation that happens to bill by the hour. There are soft helmets and clipboards and the unhurried manner of men doing paperwork, and the sheer banality of it is the threatening part.

[Beat 432]
He could fight his way in through the lobby. He has the inventory for it, and the night is still young. But a fight at the door would wake the whole nest, and he has an old woman to bring back down through these same people on the way out, so he spends the smarter currency instead and goes around.

The smarter currency is Sparrow, who lives in the seam between what a corporation bothers to lock and what it forgets to lock — a whole economy, that seam, and Sparrow pays the rent on a life inside it. Kyle pings a number that only works on alternate weeks and gets a single line back: a service door on the loading side, a freight car still drawing power on Axiom's own circuit because the sweep crew has been using it to carry people out, and a price that is fair, which from Sparrow is its own small miracle.

On the loading side a dog is making its rounds — a rangy gene-stray gone the color of nothing in particular, nosing the dock and the alley mouth and the dead foot of the freight ramp in the same order it has plainly nosed them a thousand nights, the way another man's watch would do it. It takes the half-strip of jerky Kyle offers from his coat with the neutral courtesy of a toll collector, no more grateful than a turnstile, and goes back to its rounds. Two relocated tenants sit on their cases by the cordon and one of them says, without looking up, that the dog is early tonight, and the other one answers that it always comes round before the lifts quit, and neither of them finds anything in that worth saying twice.

The service door opens on the second wrong knock. The freight car is a steel room that smells of other people's lives being carried out of it — wet cardboard, and a child's chalk drawing taped at toddler height that nobody ever came back for. The control panel is original Axiom, the dead corp's wheatsheaf logo worn pale under forty years of thumbs, still wired to a bus standard nothing has manufactured since the company went under and somehow still load-bearing for the building's whole spine. Kyle thumbs the panel past the floors that still have power, and the car begins to climb.

It dies at sixty-one. The doors part on a hallway already gone half-dark, the corridor lights dropped to the dim red of emergency cells and the air turned close and warm where the vents have quit, and above him the remaining twenty-three floors to eighty-four are nothing but stairwell and a clock that is still running. So he starts up them on foot. Somewhere around the sixty-eighth landing the count goes wrong on him — he climbs nine flights and the read insists he has climbed eleven, the numbers stenciled on the concrete disagreeing with the ones in his skull, and under it all a low hum arrives a half-second before the boot that should have made it. He notes the discrepancy and keeps climbing.

[Beat 433]
He meets the sweep on the seventy-second landing — two Arcturus officers in soft armor, walking an entire floor's worth of residents down ahead of them. An old man carries a birdcage; a woman carries a sewing machine she will be made to set down somewhere before the lobby. The Civil Security pair are not cruel, which is the part people never quite believe. They are polite, and bored, and certain, and they have a quota of floors to clear before the dark catches up to them as well.

The lead officer raises a hand. "Building's decommissioned, friend. You're going the wrong way."

Kyle lets him see the katana riding over his shoulder — matte black, an edge that takes none of the light, nothing remarkable about it at all except the hand that has had a very long time to practice with it — and lets him do the arithmetic for himself. Most encounters end right about here, and this one does.

"Medical retrieval," Kyle says, which is true in the way he needs it to be true. "Eighty-four. I'll be back down before you are."

The officer looks at the blade, and then at Kyle's face, and then at the clipboard that has no line on it for any of this, and decides that his contract does not pay nearly enough to find out how the rest of it goes. "We cut eighty-four in forty minutes," he says, stepping aside. "Anything still up there when it goes is salvage. That's not me deciding that — that's just the schedule."

"I know exactly whose schedule it is," Kyle says, and keeps climbing.

[Beat 434]
Floor eighty-four is hot and close and still has its lights, which means it still has its people. Some doors stand open, where the persuaded have already gone; others stand shut and chained, where the rest have decided to make Axiom come and do the work by hand.

Behind the door of 84-12, a man is talking to the door itself.

"Is that the lift?" the voice asks — old, and courteous, and hopeful in a way that costs Kyle something to stand there and hear. "Wait — wait just a moment, I can't find my other shoe. Tell them I'm coming. Tell them I only need my shoe."

Kyle stops, and the clock stands there at his back with its hand on his shoulder. Twelve doors down the hall there is a woman he has been paid half-rate to carry out of here, and below them both a tide of dark is coming up to meet everyone on the floor, and right here in front of him is a closed door with a courteous voice behind it, asking the night to please wait while an old man finds his shoe. The decision costs him one heartbeat, and the code wins, the way it always wins. Tonight he hates it more than usual.

"I'm not the lift," he says, to the door. "Stay inside, lock it, and don't open up for the soft helmets." It is the best he has to offer. It is nowhere near enough.

"I only need my shoe," the voice says again, to no one in particular.

So Kyle leaves him there, and goes to find Comfort.

## Eighty-Four

[Beat 435]
Mrs. Okwuosa-Brandt's door is not chained. It stands open, propped with a cinder block, because she has decided that a woman who has done nothing wrong does not hide behind her own door. She sits small and upright in a chair that faces the hallway, dressed as if for church, a hard-sided case balanced on her knees and her hands folded on top of it. The first thing Kyle's Neuretics does — the read he stopped saying out loud at nineteen and has never once managed to switch off — is tell him that her people came from four different places that have no earthly business sharing a single face: Igbo, German, somewhere in the Caribbean, and somewhere cold. A whole century of the world's mixing, sitting in a chair in a tower that a corporation is in the middle of switching off.

The second thing he notices is the smell of citrus — green and bright and frankly impossible, eighty-four floors up a concrete column that has not smelled like anything but other people in forty years.

The source of it is out past the open balcony glass, where there is a tree. An actual tree: a Meyer lemon in a barrel she must have carried up here in pieces, taller than she is, with four fruit on it gone gold in the dark and its leaves turning in the wind that never quite stops at this height.

"You're not my granddaughter," Comfort says. She has a voice like a closed door.

"She sent me anyway," Kyle says. "She's stubborn. I can't think where she gets it."

[Beat 436]
"I'm not going," Comfort says, before he can open with whatever it is she expects him to open with. "I know what's coming. I've known since I was younger than you are now. They take the top of the building, and then they wait for the bottom to come down on its own, and they call the whole arrangement a clause." She says the word the way a person holds something that has bitten them. "I am eighty-four years old. I have lived in this building longer than that boy downstairs has been alive. I am not going to be carried out of my own home so that a company can put a number where my window used to be."

"Okay," Kyle says, and sits down on the arm of the other chair — which surprises her, because as a rule men who climb eighty-four floors with swords on their backs do not sit.

"Okay?"

"I'm not going to carry you anywhere you haven't agreed to go." He nods at the case on her knees. "What's in the box?"

She studies him for a long moment, deciding. "My husband," she says at last. "What's left of him that the city would let me keep."

Kyle does not tell her he's sorry. People say that to fill a silence, and the silence belongs to her, so he waits.

"And the tree," she says, and her voice changes on the word — the only crack in the door all night. "He grew it from a pip in a coffee cup the year we moved in. It has been alive up here longer than half the people in this building. I am not going to leave it behind to be — relocated."

[Beat 437]
The lights brown out, hold, and brown out again — the tide line, four floors up and still coming. Kyle's Neuretics hands him the number with no maybe left in it: the men who cut eighty-four will be in this corridor in under thirty minutes, and the dark will beat them here by ten.

There is no version of tonight in which that barrel goes down the stairs. He weighs it anyway, the way he weighs everything — two hundred pounds of wet soil and forty years of a dead man's care, against a stairwell that is a black corkscrew full of people who would cut the cardiac assist out of her chest for the financing still owed on it. He cannot carry both the woman and the tree. With the clock where it is, he is not sure he could carry the tree alone.

So he does the thing he is worst at: he tells her the truth, and then he offers her the lie that might just hold it together.

"I can't take the tree," he says. "I can take you, and I can take him" — the box — "and I can take that." He nods at the tip of the lowest branch, where the new growth has come in green. "A cutting. One branch. You start it in a cup of water the way he did, and inside a year you've got the same tree standing in a different window. You wouldn't be leaving anything behind. You'd just be moving it."

She knows it is not nearly that simple, and he can see that she knows; a cutting is a coin toss that would make a botanist wince, and they are both of them too old to pretend otherwise — she in years, and he in mileage.

"Will it take?" she asks.

"Yeah," Kyle says, which is the first lie he has told her, and he catches himself in the telling of it. Some lines you say anyway.

[Beat 438]
She makes him do it properly. There is no time and she makes time regardless — a clean knife from the kitchen, a cut taken on the angle just below a node, the way her husband taught her and the way she will not allow a stranger to do badly even now. She wraps the cut end in a square of wet paper towel and then a sandwich bag, and holds it cupped in both hands the way she holds the box, which she has refused to let Kyle carry. The box itself rides in a cloth sack knotted across the front of her. She has done some version of this before, Kyle thinks — not this exactly, but the shape of it, the leaving of a place with only what your two arms can hold around — and the whole tower is full of people who learned that particular lesson young.

He gets her as far as the door, and the lights go.

It is not a brown-out this time. The whole floor drops into black at once, the way a held breath ends, and the warm dark fills up with the sound of eighty-four's life spinning down — the pumps, the vents, the refrigerator hum you never once notice until the moment it isn't there. Somewhere down the corridor a chained door begins to rattle as the soft helmets test it. The tide has arrived, and the two of them are standing in it.

"Stay on my left," Kyle says, and his Neuretics floods the black with what it can still give him — heat, motion, the remembered geometry of a hallway it mapped on the way in. Her hand finds the back of his coat. "Keep your eyes on my back, and don't look at the doors."

"Why not?" Comfort asks.

"Because some of them are open," he says.

## One Shoe

[Beat 439]
They pass 84-12 again on their way to the stairwell.

The door is open now. The cinder block that should have held it shut is sitting out in the hall where someone kicked it aside, and the dark inside the unit is the dark of a room already cleared. On the floor by the threshold, where a man sat down to put his shoes on and managed only one of them, there is a single shoe.

Kyle's Neuretics, which has had an answer ready for everything tonight, has nothing at all to add. The soft helmets came while he was sitting on the arm of a chair being told about a lemon tree — or perhaps it was the other ones who came, since eighty-four is salvage now and the schedule said as much on the stairs. He had told the man to lock the door. The man had wanted his shoe.

Comfort starts to turn toward the open door, toward the small wrong shape of the shoe on the floor, and Kyle puts his body between her and it, gently, the way you would turn a child away from a window. "Keep walking," he says, in the flat and low and deliberately boring read-out voice he keeps for exactly this. "Eyes on my back."

He does not tell her whose door it was. He walks her on to the stairs.

[Beat 440]
The stairwell is a black concrete throat with the emergency cells dying in it one landing at a time, and it is not empty. The fledglings have come up to meet the salvage halfway, because carrying a thing all the way down is work, and letting the building deliver it into your hands is not.

They come down the stairs the way the young come at everything — too fast, and from above. Kyle hears them well before the read paints them in: boots, and the eager quiet of people certain the dark is on their side. He sets Comfort against the inner wall, one flight below a landing, where the geometry does the work of a second man for him. The shaft narrows to a single body's width at each switchback and the turns are all blind — a place one man could hold.

"Don't move," he tells her. "Whatever you hear."

The first one comes around the turn with a salvage hook in one hand and a headlamp on his brow, and the headlamp is a mistake, because it tells Kyle exactly where the rest of him is. He doesn't draw the katana for this; he takes the hook out of the boy's hands at the wrist-lock, turns him into the wall, and lets the next two trip over the lesson on their way down. Cacophony stays on his hip and the blade stays on his back, because these are children with debts and bad ideas, and the code has a line about children that he has never once crossed.

And then the fourth one rounds the turn with a gun in his hand, which changes the shape of everything that comes next.

[Beat 441]
The fourth one has a gun, and a gun rewrites the next three seconds entirely. He's young under the headlamp — a jacket too new for the work, the price tag very possibly still tucked in a pocket — and his hands are not steady, which is worse rather than better, because a frightened hand pulls early. Kyle's Neuretics paints the shot before the trigger has finished the thought: a hard red line laid from the muzzle, down the length of the landing, through the space where Kyle is standing and on into the stairwell below, where Comfort is pressed against the inner wall a single flight down. Precognition tells him what it always tells him — vacate the space the round is going to fill — except that the space behind him is the only thing standing between that line and the old woman, and if he steps off it the round he so neatly dodges finds her half a second later. For once in his overclocked life he cannot simply move out of the way, and so he does the only thing the geometry leaves him: he moves the way instead.

He draws Cacophony left-handed, the squat five-shot revolver shotgun clearing its throat off his hip, thumbs the cylinder around to the breaching load, and puts a round straight into the stair-rail bracket beside the shooter's knee. The whole structure up there comes apart in noise and orange sparks and a length of railing that was load-bearing half a second ago, and the kid does what any body does when the floor it has been trusting starts to shout — his shot goes wild into the ceiling. Kyle is up the flight and on him before the echo has finished, and he takes the gun, and the fight, off the boy.

It costs him. Behind his eyes the overclock runs hot enough that he can feel it, a slow pressure building at both temples as though a thumb were being pushed into each one, and a thin line of blood starts from his nose. He thumbs it off his lip without thinking about it. The white crowds in at the edges of his sight and he blinks it back by main force. He has been spending the read all night like a man who is certain he will never need it again tomorrow.

Below him, Comfort has not moved an inch. The cutting is still cupped in both her hands.

"Still with me?" he asks.

"I buried a husband, and I outlived the state I was born in," she says, from the dark below him. "A boy with a hook is not going to frighten me."

## The Loading Side

[Beat 442]
They come out of the stairwell on the loading side, into the half-lit dark of the loading dock, and after eighty-four floors of dead ventilation the night air tastes almost like a second chance. Two of them have already moved off the bike and onto the dock, because they understand the trade rather better than Kyle would like: salvage doesn't leave a building by the front. It leaves by the back, and the back is exactly where they have been waiting.

These are not children this time. One of them wears a patch — a Vulture grown well past the fledgling stage on precisely this kind of evening — and he carries the calm of a man who has done the cutting before and slept soundly enough afterward.

"That's a financed unit you're walking off with," the Vulture says, with a nod toward Comfort and toward the cardiac assist he can apparently read on her clear across a dark dock. "Cardiac, a couple of genemod joints, corneas. She's underwater on every bit of it. By rights—"

"She's not a unit," Kyle says.

[Beat 443]
"By rights," the Vulture says again, and his hand drifts toward the hook on his belt — and that word, together with the gesture, is what finally draws Silence.

It comes off Kyle's back in the one motion he has practiced more than any other, matte and edgeless, and settles level in front of him. Here is the thing the Vulture cannot see in the dark: Kyle is white at the edges and down to the dregs of a battery he has been overdrawing since the sixty-first floor, and his right hand wants very badly to shake — the fine tremor of a man running on empty, which happens to be the exact tell this particular Vulture has built an entire career on reading off of marks and merchandise. A man who can price the cardiac assist on an old woman from clear across a black dock can certainly read a shaking hand at four feet.

So Kyle simply does not let it shake. He spends the last of something he does not actually have left to spend, holding the blade dead still — as still as the flat of it, as still as a man with all the time in the world and nothing left to lose — and the stillness is the entire lie, and it is a very good one.

The Vulture looks for the tremor, the way he looks for it on everyone he meets. And because he does not find it, he does something the easy marks never make him do: he takes a step. One unhurried foot forward, the hook coming off his belt now, his grip closing around the handle with the particular confidence of a man who has decided the body in front of him is already inventory. He has weighed it. He is coming.

The blade does not move. That is all Kyle has — the not-moving — and he holds it through the whole length of that step, through the half-second where the arithmetic could break either way, while every overdrawn cell in his right arm screams to betray him. The hook stops a foot short. The Vulture's eyes go to the edge of the katana, then to Kyle's wrist, then back, looking for the one twitch that would tell him he can afford this. He weighs a steady blade against a financed corpse one last time, runs the arithmetic he has run on a hundred easier nights — and finds, to his evident irritation, that it does not quite come out in his favor. He takes his hand back off the hook.

"Not worth the paperwork," he says, to save his own face, and steps back, and pulls the fledgling along with him.

"No," Kyle agrees. "It really isn't."

[Beat 444]
The granddaughter is waiting two blocks out, beyond the building's cordon, where Kyle told Sable to tell her to wait. She doesn't run when she sees them; she walks, fast and careful, the way you walk toward someone you are afraid of finding changed from how you left them.

She stops a foot short of her grandmother, as though that last step was the hardest one of all. "Grandma." "I sent him anyway."

"I know what you did," Comfort says. She lets the girl take the box from her back, and she does not say thank you, because between the two of them a thank you would land as an insult; letting her carry it is the thank you. What she does not let the girl take is the cutting. The girl's eyes go to the bundle — the one thing her grandmother will not hand over — and she does not reach for it, because she has learned which things you do not ask for. The wet paper-towel bundle stays cupped in Comfort's own two hands, and Kyle understands that it will stay there until it is standing in a cup of water on some windowsill lower down.

"You came up for an old woman who told you not to," Comfort says to him.

"I was paid to," Kyle says, which is true, and is also the lie hiding underneath the true thing, and she is eighty-four years old and hears both of them.

"Mm," she says. She looks at him a moment longer — the reading-back look, the one he usually only gets from Sable — and then she says the thing he is going to keep. "There was a boy on my floor. Twelve doors down from me. Some nights I could hear him through the wall, talking to his radio." She does not bother to make it a question. "You couldn't carry everyone."

"No," Kyle says. "I couldn't."

"I know," she says — and it is not forgiveness, because she is not the one with the standing to give it, and they both understand that too.

## The Receipt

[Beat 445]
He watches them go — two women, a box, and a branch sealed in a sandwich bag, walking south into the part of the Glooms that still has its lights — and then he turns back to look at the thing he climbed.

The Roost is most of the way dark now. The tide has come down past eighty-four, so that her window is black along with all the others, and on the balcony behind that black glass a lemon tree he could not carry stands in the wind that never stops at that height, four gold fruit still on it, waiting on a power that is not coming back and a cold that will find it by morning. Forty years of a dead man's patience, and it ends tonight. On a lit sill a few floors below the tide line someone has folded their relocation notice into a paper crane and set it back down on the windowsill it was served on, beak to the dark, the official Axiom letterhead folded into one wing — and Kyle walks past it without slowing. He thinks about the boy with the one shoe, and about the tree, and about the distance between what he was paid to save and everything there actually was to save.

His Neuretics, slow and shaky and only now coming back online, offers him the one comfort it has to give, which is a number. The small light in the corner of his mind's eye is still red from the stairwell — one flash, the man who seeded this hardware in a child too young to be asked once told him, for about a year off the far end of his life. He spent a year tonight, and what he got for it was an old woman, a box, and the coin-toss of a branch.

He runs the arithmetic. The branch alone would have been enough. He waits for that to bother him. It doesn't. Then he gets back on the bike and turns south, and in the dark he does the thing he would tell anyone to do in it: he names the next ordinary thing for himself, and then the next, the curb and the corner and the long block home, and he keeps on naming them until the tower is finally behind him and the street is only a street again.

[Beat 446]
The contact request came in at 11:40 on a Tuesday, Evanston prefix, residential channel, clean encryption. Not how work usually found Kyle. Work usually found him through a referral chain long enough that by the time it reached him the original source was three people removed and the job description had accumulated all the creative liberties that passed through six hands tend to acquire. This was direct. His Neuretics flagged the source address automatically, no prompt required: Tier 4, North Shore, residential block, registered to a property with a tax assessment in the range where people heated their floors and called it practical.

He sat with it for forty seconds, which for him was a long time. The channel was clean, the tone in the opening message was professional without being practiced — the kind of professional that comes from actually having done professional things for a long time, not from learning what professional sounds like. The message said: we need to locate a family member, we were given your name by a reliable source, please advise on availability.

No one who runs an organ operation opens with please advise on availability.

He accepted the contact and sent back a time.

[Beat 447]
He took Milwaukee north, which was the long way, because the long way had fewer checkpoints and he was not in the mood for Arcturus's toll architecture that morning. The one he couldn't avoid sat at the Damen seam, where the district had flown Libation Corp's mark for as long as he'd ridden it — the stylized cup that meant cheap hydration credits and expensive everything else — and now wore a fresh Arcturus banner over the old signage, the bolt holes of the previous flag still visible at the corners. The reader there clocked his neuretics, not his plate; it wanted to know who was passing through, not what he was driving. He let it look. The city changed in the way it always changed going north: not dramatically, not all at once, but incrementally, the way a conversation changes when someone becomes aware they are being recorded. The buildings got newer. The transit infrastructure got quieter — fewer jury-rigged additions to the Pulse access points, fewer hand-painted schedules posted at street level because the displays were working. The commerce changed character. Storefronts replaced by curated storefronts — same business, better lighting.

By Evanston the money was old enough to look quiet. He noted the building from half a block out: fourteen stories, glass and poured concrete, the kind of construction that did not have to apologize for itself. There was a human doorman in the lobby. Kyle parked and sat with the engine cooling and looked at the doorman.

A human doorman meant either old money or theater. The distinction mattered in different ways depending on which it was. Old money meant a client who had resources and was used to having them handled. Theater meant a client who wanted to be seen as having resources, which meant someone who needed to win something. He put on his jacket and crossed the street. He supposed he'd find out.

[Beat 448]
The apartment was on twelve. The elevator was fast and quiet and smelled like nothing. Money smells like nothing. The corridor it opened onto held the dead-air smell of a place cleaned constantly and inhabited never, the air of a corridor that someone scrubbed daily and no one had a reason to walk down.

Douglas Hartley had prepared a briefing on actual paper — printed, collated, paper-clipped at the upper left corner. Kyle hadn't held a physical briefing document in four years. The last one had been from a Corponation legal team that communicated exclusively in hard copy for security theater reasons, which was at least honest about what it was doing. Douglas's paper read differently: the pages were from a home printer, the content was carefully organized, and the act of preparing it had the quality of someone who believed that the format demonstrated the seriousness of the problem. Kyle accepted it when it was slid across the table.

Douglas had the posture of a man who had decided the meeting's outcome before it started. Not arrogant — Kyle had been in rooms with arrogant clients, and arrogance was a different shape — but settled in his conclusions in the way a structure is settled on its foundation. The conclusions had set like concrete days ago. The meeting was for confirming them.

Mei-Lin Hartley stood at the window with her arms crossed. Not the arms crossed of cold. The arms crossed of someone containing something with physical effort. She had looked at Kyle when he came in, and the look was the look of someone reading a person, not appraising one. Kyle noted the distinction.

In thirty seconds he had what he needed. Douglas had conclusions. Mei-Lin had information. These were very different things to carry into the same room, and the room was small enough that neither of them seemed to have fully registered the difference.

[Beat 449]
The daughter's name was Celeste. Twenty-two. She had come to them six days ago and said she wanted cosmetic genework — functional cat ears, a melanin shift, some expression mods. Nothing structural, Douglas said, as though this were relevant. Nothing that would compromise her function. Kyle waited to see if he would finish the sentence with a subject — her function as what — but he didn't. The word sat there by itself like a load-bearing wall someone had forgotten to connect to anything.

Kyle asked where she'd gone.

Douglas said that was what he'd hired Kyle to determine.

Kyle asked when she was last in contact.

Four days, Douglas said.

Kyle asked if there had been any attempt to reach her through her neuretics — a missing ping, a location request, anything.

The expression that crossed Douglas's face was brief and specific. It was the expression of a man for whom the question had implied something he found unpleasant. He said: she's not responding.

Kyle said: that's different from no attempt. He said it without inflection. He said: can you send me her neuretics ID?

Douglas wrote it on a piece of paper — actual paper, consistent with the briefing — and slid it across. Kyle looked at it. His Neuretics flagged it, queued it. He said: I'll need an image and any last known locations.

Douglas opened his tablet. He'd prepared those too.

[Beat 450]
Douglas had a tablet. He slid it across without asking whether Kyle wanted it — the gesture of a man presenting evidence to a subordinate, not a contractor he'd hired that morning.

The screen held a curated collection. News items, carefully chosen: body-mod shops that had fronted for organ operations, three of them, two with documented arrests, one with a Meridian 88 investigative segment that had run eight months back. A Pulse Network report on missing-persons cases where the last known activity touched the unlicensed corridor. A Pilsen community alert, six months old. Each item saved with care.

Kyle read through them. He gave each the time it deserved, which was enough to read it and not enough to perform thoroughness. He kept his face in the register that read as thoughtful. His Neuretics was doing something else underneath: logging the shape of the selection, which was not random.

The articles were real. He had no quarrel with the facts inside them. But the selection had been assembled to support a conclusion reached before the assembling started — which meant the evidence hadn't produced the conclusion. Something else had. Fear, most likely; the particular fear of a parent who'd read the wrong story about where his child might have gone. The articles came after. You don't go looking for kindling until you've already decided to be afraid of the dark.

He held that for later. He handed the tablet back and said: I'll look into it.

[Beat 451]
Douglas took a call in the other room. The moment the door closed Mei-Lin was already moving — not urgently, not with the performance of someone who had been waiting for permission, but with the directness of someone who had been waiting for the obstacle to clear.

She showed him a social feed on her own device, held out flat so he could read it. Not Celeste's public profile. A semi-private one, accessible only if you knew it was there and had a social link through a mutual acquaintance. She said: I found it through a friend of a friend of hers. Eight months of posts.

Kyle read. He took his time.

The posts were not what a kid looks like when they discover something new. There was no breathless enthusiasm, no overclaiming, no the world has been hiding this from me energy that tends to come with a fresh conversion. What he was reading was a research log. Careful, specific, incrementally building. The language was precise in a way that took months to develop: baseline drift, willing flesh, integration stays, the unlicensed corridor, motor map formation, ghost period. The terminology of someone who had spent a long time learning how to talk accurately about something they'd been thinking about for longer.

Eight months of documented research. Which meant the thinking had come before the documentation.

Mei-Lin said: she knew what she was doing.

Kyle said: yes.

She said: find her.

He said: I will. He meant it both as a professional statement and as a fact. He said it the same way for both.

[Beat 452]
Douglas came back from his call and sat down and, almost in passing — the way you mention something you assume everyone already knows — said that he had also retained Meridian Consulting.
