Corporate investigative firm, he said. Comprehensive service. Two days ahead on the case, working the same file, full team deployment. He said this the way people mention insurance: coverage, redundancy, the responsible thing to do when the stakes are high enough.

Kyle didn't react. He kept his face in the same register and his posture in the same configuration and let Douglas finish the sentence and let the pause after it exist without filling it.

Mei-Lin, across the room, watched him not react.

That was the thing. She watched him not react, and something in her expression changed — the look of a hypothesis confirmed. Her husband had hired a corporate investigative firm two days ago and had retained Kyle this morning, and she had worked out what he actually wanted from each. For one moment there were three people in that room and two of them understood what was happening.

Douglas said: I assume that's not a problem.

Kyle said: no problem. He was looking at Douglas when he said it. He was aware of Mei-Lin when he said it.

[Beat 453]
He took the stairs down. Twelve floors, because the elevator felt wrong for the math he needed to run.

By the fourth floor it was already clear. Douglas had hired Meridian Consulting to confirm a conclusion: that Celeste had been taken by someone in the unlicensed modification corridor, harmed, and needed to be recovered by force or negotiation. Meridian would execute that brief competently and expensively and without ever asking whether the conclusion was correct, because that was not what they were paid to do.

Douglas had retained Kyle — two days late, as a hedge he wouldn't have called a hedge — because some part of him wanted coverage on a different outcome without admitting there was a different outcome to cover.

Mei-Lin had told Kyle to find her daughter. No conditions. No conclusion pre-loaded. She had shown him the feed. She had said: she knew what she was doing. She had said: find her.

Two different jobs in one contract. The invoice would say the same thing. The work would not.

He pushed through the lobby door. The human doorman held it without comment, without eye contact. Old money, then. Theater would have wanted to be thanked.

He crossed to his bike. A girl to find, and a community he'd lived beside his entire adult life without once turning to look at it directly. He started the engine and went south.

[Beat 454]
He called Pixel from the bike, still parked at the curb outside the Hartley building.

First contact since the night in her workshop. He'd thought about what to say for most of the elevator ride down and had the answer before the third floor: keep it flat, keep it professional. The alternative was a conversation that needed more room than a parking spot in Evanston.

He said: I need someone with inside knowledge of the unlicensed mod scene. The parts of it that don't register as addresses.

A pause. One beat longer than thinking. He knew the difference. He let it sit.

She said: parts shop on Damen. One hour.

He said: thanks.

She was already gone. She ended things the second she'd decided them, no ceremony, because the ceremony was wasted motion. Today the beat she'd held before answering was still sitting in his ear.

He put the bike in gear and headed south, taking his time. He had an hour, and Damen was twenty minutes off, and the route through Wicker Park gave him room to think. He'd half-expected to need someone with deeper corridor reach — Stash, who knew every back channel south of the gray zone, had worked Pilsen routes last month — but Pixel was the right call and he'd known it before he placed it. Riding was sometimes the same as thinking and sometimes a way of not. He wasn't sure yet which one he'd come for.

[Beat 455]
Milwaukee going south was a core sample, if you knew how to read it. The city had been laid down in layers — not by time, exactly, but by the accumulation of arguments about what the city was for and who it was for, each argument settling into the strata when nobody had the authority to enforce a winner.

The North Shore's glass and order gave way at an altitude that had nothing to do with elevation. The buildings stopped apologizing for ambition and started showing their maintenance — the hand-patched infrastructure, the Pulse access points wearing their repair histories openly, the communal decisions about which problem got solved this week with the money that existed. The commerce moved laterally. Φ that didn't climb the chain but went sideways, block to block, in exchanges that didn't need a corponation's processing fee to be real.

By the time he crossed into Pilsen the city felt like itself. Not lesser. The North Shore was the trim. This was the framing. It had rained while he was up north, and the ferrocement was still dark with it, throwing up the smell it threw up wet — cold mineral and old water, like a cave that had learned to be a city and kept the smell of the original.

He noted the feeling and didn't trust it — a man who'd spent the morning in a building that heated its floors should be careful about deciding which part of the city was honest. The Hartley apartment had left something on him, a film he could taste. He parked on Damen and went to find Pixel.

[Beat 456]
Pixel was already at the counter when he arrived, one hand inside a disassembled something he couldn't identify from the door — could be a junction box, could be part of a neuretic calibration rig, could be something that had no official product category and lived in the space between what was built and what she'd made. She didn't look up when the bell over the door announced him.

He came to the counter. He laid out what he knew in the order he'd arranged it on the bike: Celeste, 22, Evanston, functional cat ears, went offline four days ago, parents haven't heard from her, father has the organ-harvesting theory and has assembled evidence for it, mother thinks the daughter knew what she was doing.

Pixel pulled the component free from whatever it was part of. Set it down on the counter. Turned to give him her full attention, which meant she stopped doing anything else entirely, the way she was capable of stopping and most people weren't.

She asked three questions: Was the feed activity consistent or recent-spike? Did the parents have the neuretics ID? Did Kyle know the specific modification she'd requested, meaning the actual structural scope, not the parent's version of it?

He answered: consistent, eight months, yes he had the ID but it was cold, and functional ears with independent movement.

She said: she's not harvested.

She said it the way she said things that were obvious to her and she suspected were not obvious to him — without condescension, but without softening it either.

[Beat 457]
Pixel picked up a stylus and sketched directly on the counter's surface — the counter had a reactive coating she used as a working surface, covered in the palimpsest of previous diagrams. She drew an ear. Then she labeled the parts with the economy of someone who had explained this before and knew where people's comprehension usually fell off.

Functional cat ears — not decorative, not fixed-position, but independently mobile, tracking sound — required: auricular cartilage reshaping at the structure level, which meant the existing ear framework had to be rebuilt, not added to. Tragus repositioning to allow the new pinna range of motion. Follicle transplant with growth programming for the fur, which was a dermal procedure with its own integration timeline. And — she tapped the last item — neural re-routing of the pinna muscles, so the ears actually moved in response to sound rather than just sitting there doing cosmetic work.

That last part, she said, was the hard one.

The brain did not have a pre-existing motor map for ears that moved independently. It had the vestigial map it had inherited — humans had the muscles, they just didn't do anything — but activating and expanding that map to allow genuine directed movement required the brain to build new pathways from the neuretics up. That took time. That took stillness. Neuretics suppressed, external stimulation minimized, because stress during the integration window could spike rejection. 72 hours minimum. Some people needed 96.

She said: she's in an integration stay. She's not missing. She went offline on purpose, because she had to.

Kyle looked at the diagram. He said: I didn't know any of this.

Pixel said: I know.

[Beat 458]
Kyle asked how he'd spent his entire adult life in the modification world and never once seen it.

Pixel gave him the look. He'd learned to read it as the one she made when she was deciding how direct to be — not whether, because with Pixel the answer to whether was always yes, eventually, but how fast and with how much cushioning for the landing.

She said: you have military-grade SNT bonding, full exoskeletal integration, an Atlas NeoCortex that costs more than most buildings in this corridor, and a brace doing load-bearing work on your leg. You've been living in the modification world your whole adult life. You just never called it a world. You called it maintenance.

He considered this. His Neuretics ran its periodic inventory underneath the thought, the way it always did — brace status, cortical load, SNT bridge calibration, the same read every six hours, numbers he'd stopped registering years ago. Brace at 94%. Cortical load at a level that would be a red flag on any standard installation and was routine for Atlas-grade. The bridges adapting. The negotiation that never closed.

He'd been paying attention to whether the hardware worked. He had never once paid attention to whose it was.

He said: point taken.

She turned back to the counter. She said: I'll ask around. Give me an hour.

[Beat 459]
Pixel drew two circles on the counter surface beside the ear diagram.

The first circle: cosmetic layer. Melanin shifts — the pigmentation algorithms, the ones that could take you from baseline to a new skin tone over eight to twelve weeks, or do it in three days if you wanted the inflammatory response. Hair follicle programming. Eye color. Bioluminescent patches, scale patterns, ear shapes on the cosmetic end — the ones that didn't move, the ones that sat there looking like what they were and changed over time as the immune system reasserted baseline. The body's default position on anything foreign was: we'll tolerate this for now. The cosmetic layer was in ongoing negotiation with that position. Keep up the suppression protocol and the change held. Drop it and you drifted back. Replaceable if you changed your mind. Identity on the surface.

The second circle: structural layer. Functional extra limbs. Sensory expansion — additional photoreceptor arrays, echolocation implants, the kind of input that required the brain to grow new processing infrastructure. Joint replacement with SNT-bonded synthetic. Neural rewiring for new motor pathways, which was the category Celeste's ears belonged to. Longer integration, real medical risk in unlicensed settings, but once the brain built the map, the map was permanent. You could remove the hardware. You could not remove the map. The brain did not unlearn a pathway it had built and used for two years.

She said: Celeste's feed reads like someone who started in the first circle and figured out she was actually interested in the question the second circle asks.

Kyle said: what's the question?

Pixel said: what does my body want to be.

She capped the stylus.

[Beat 460]
Pixel named three operators. She named them in the way she named things she knew from inside — no hesitation, no qualifier, no I think it might be, just the names and the geography attached to them. Pilsen, Bridgeport, Back of the Yards. The corridor that ran south of the gray zone, where the unlicensed work happened in the spaces left by the licensed industry's routing choices: where the licensed supply chain didn't run, where the regulatory enforcement was inconsistent enough to breathe in, where the community density provided the kind of distributed social accountability that worked better than most oversight boards she'd encountered.

One of the three did cat ears, she said. She knew them from inside because she had done unlicensed calibration work for some of them — the SNT bridges that the licensed supply chain wouldn't service, because the hardware was installed by someone without a current corponation contract, which was a category distinction that had nothing to do with the quality of the installation or the health of the patient. She had calibrated SNT bridges in unlicensed settings for two years. The adverse event rate was something she could speak to personally.

She said she would make calls. She would ask about a client matching the description — not the name, she said, not the name — and let him know.

Kyle said he would canvas the first two addresses while she worked her connections. He said it as a division of labor. It was also the more dangerous half of the work, and he was aware of that, and he didn't mention it.

[Beat 461]
The first address was a recessed door off Ashland, behind a building that had been a pharmacy before the local pharmaceutical distribution model had been absorbed by Arcturus's Wellness Division — one of the quieter acquisitions of the early 2210s, a corponation deciding that the gap between what people needed and what licensed channels provided was large enough to qualify as infrastructure, and infrastructure could be owned. The door was the kind that didn't announce itself.

A Meridian Consulting van was already parked on the block.

Clean. Wrong for the neighborhood the way a new installation is wrong when it hasn't been weathered yet. The van was the correct color and the correct size for this street and had managed, through some quality of newness and precision maintenance, to stand out anyway. Kyle read it from the corner before he committed to the block, his Neuretics logging it without prompting.

The man stepping out was Aleksei. Kyle had crossed his path twice on contract adjacencies — not working together, working in the same operational space, which was a different thing. The kind of professional you nodded at in lobbies. Tier equivalent, no discernible ego about the equivalence.

Aleksei saw Kyle. He produced no visible reaction. He said: Hartley girl.

Kyle said: Hartley girl.

Aleksei said: I'm two days ahead of you.

Kyle said: I know.

They looked at each other for a moment. Neither of them had anything additional to add to this accounting. Aleksei's expression said he found this reasonable. Kyle's expression said the same thing.

[Beat 462]
Neither of them wanted to pool resources. Not unfriendliness — operational clarity. Pooling meant sharing contact networks, which meant burning contacts through redundant approach, which meant neither of them could work this corridor again with the same ease. The unlicensed community kept a long memory for investigators who treated it as a seam to mine. Pixel had told him this; he'd understood it as information. He was understanding it now as cost, which was a different kind of understanding.

They stood on the sidewalk and arrived at the minimum viable arrangement in about forty-five seconds, neither of them having to say it. Negative findings: shared. If one cleared a location — not Celeste, not the integration stay — he flagged the other. No further coordination. No report, no check-in, no touching base. Just: not here, clean, move on.

Aleksei said: reasonable.

Kyle said: reasonable.

He said: you take this block, I'll take Bridgeport.

Aleksei said: I was going to say the same thing. He said it without staking a claim on having thought of it first. Kyle went south. Aleksei went to the door. Kyle didn't look back. The arrangement would hold or it wouldn't — but Aleksei was the kind who made the arrangements hold, and Kyle had stopped checking over his shoulder for that kind a long time ago.

[Beat 463]
The first operator ran out of the back of what looked like a textile repair business. Out front, under a sodium lamp that flickered on a cycle too slow to be a fault and too regular to be anything else, sixteen cigarette butts had been set in a precise circle on the curb, filters turned inward, each one spaced from the next at a distance someone had clearly measured by eye and gotten right. Kyle stepped around it on his way to the door. The front room smelled of heat-bonding compounds and recycled fiber and the particular quality of air that builds up in a room where focused work happens over many years. The woman at the front desk did not ask what he needed. She looked at him for three seconds and said: back room, knock first.

He knocked. The woman inside was mid-fifties, with the precise hands of someone who worked small — the kind of precision that comes from doing detailed work for so long that the hands develop their own calibration, a steadiness that didn't require conscious attention. She had the manner of someone who had possessed a license once and had lost it in the specific kind of dispute that occurs when a corporation's definition of whose body an installation belongs to and a practitioner's definition of the same thing arrive at different conclusions and neither one has the political capital to make the other one move.

She did limb attachment. Sensory expansion. She did not do cat ears — she was clear about this, without hedging, without the defensive quality that comes from expecting accusation. She simply did not do it. Different specialty.

She was willing to talk because she had looked at Kyle and determined he was not Arcturus and not a journalist, two things she said were equally dangerous to her business in different ways. She said this without anger. She showed him the front room of the operation without asking why he wanted to see it, which was either trust or a demonstration of confidence, and Kyle couldn't tell which and suspected it was both.

[Beat 464]
In the back room, a young man slept in a reclined chair with neural dampeners over his eyes. The dampeners were the low-profile kind — not the blunt instrument that full neuretics suppression required in older hardware, but the calibrated version, which worked by narrowing the input bandwidth rather than shutting it off, the way you dim a light rather than kill the circuit. His breathing was even. His face was still.

His new arm rested on a foam support beside him.

It was fully formed and fully attached, joining the body at a point just below the left shoulder where the junction site showed the calibration markers the SNT bridge required during initial bonding — small printed indicators the color of fresh bruising, temporary, they'd fade when the body stopped needing the external reference points. The arm was indistinguishable from the other two in construction. It was not indistinguishable in one quality: it was still. Not relaxed in the way of sleep. Still in the way of a thing that was present in the body but not yet claimed by it.

The operator said: he'll be in ghost period for two to four weeks. The arm won't feel like his yet. The brain knows it's there but hasn't built the map that makes it feel like it belongs. Some people panic in ghost period, she said. Some cry. She paused. She said: some people feel complete for the first time.

She said the last thing the way she might say the sun comes up in the east. As a fact that happened to be good.

[Beat 465]
Kyle asked her to explain the SNT difference. He said: I have military-grade. I know the maintenance cycle. I've never known the alternative.

She walked him through it without performing surprise that he'd ask. She had the quality of a person who spent very little energy managing other people's comfort with their own ignorance.

Military-grade SNT, she said, uses a bandwidth-brute protocol. The synthetic pathway is forced into connection with the organic nerve endings. The immune system identifies it as foreign and fights it perpetually. Monthly calibration keeps the bridge from degrading — because without calibration the immune response would win, and the bridge would fail, and the hardware would go dark. The body tolerates the installation. It does not accept it.

Consumer-grade SNT works differently. The synthetic tissue is seeded rather than forced — it's introduced in contact with the organic nerve endings and left to grow. Over six months it integrates. The nerve endings extend toward it. The body stops reading it as foreign and starts reading it as self. Once accepted, no maintenance. No monthly calibration. The prosthetic reports to the brain the way flesh does: as something that was always there.

Better than flesh, in some configurations, she said. No involuntary pain response unless the client programs one.

Kyle asked why the military didn't use it.

She said: the military doesn't want its hardware to feel like your body. If it feels like your body, you might start making decisions about it the way you make decisions about your body. It wants you to know the hardware belongs to them.

She said it without particular heat. As another fact.

[Beat 466]
Kyle asked directly. He said: there's an operation in this corridor that the community knows about. Not a modification shop.

She did not pretend not to know what he meant. She did not check his face for evidence of a setup. She had already decided what he was and the decision held.

She said: yes. And she said: I won't name it. The community has been working to push it out for two years. The problem — she used the word problem the way you use it for something that is accurate and insufficient at the same time — is that every time something like that surfaces, it becomes the story. The unlicensed modification corridor, the organ operation, the harvested client — that becomes the story that people tell about this work. The community's two years of trying to push it out does not become the story. The journalists don't cover the community's enforcement of its own code. They cover the violation.

She said: it poisons every conversation about unlicensed modification with the word they all dread.

Kyle didn't ask what word. He knew what word.

She said: Ashland, near Cermak. Former clinic. The signage is gone but you'll read the building.

He said: thank you.

She said: find what you're looking for and leave the rest of us alone.

He said: that's the plan.

[Beat 467]
The building was a former licensed clinic. The signage was gone but the ghost of it stayed — the mounting hardware, the shadow lines on the brick where the letters had blocked weather for years, the anchor points set at a height that read medical-professional, not retail. Someone had taken the sign down and left the evidence of the sign. An operational decision that made sense in the short term.

From the outside it read body-mod. The window display had the right vocabulary — the curated visual language the community used, the same photographs, the same aesthetic, done correctly. Correctly was its own kind of information.

From the outside it also had a loading dock on the side street taking traffic at hours no cosmetic practice kept. Kyle clocked two deliveries in six minutes, neither one the scale of consumables. The refrigeration behind a ground-floor grate ran larger than a mod shop needed — cold storage sized for quantity, not for patients.

And the absence. A working body-mod operation had a patient rhythm: arrivals, appointments, departures, people in the before-state and people in the after. This building had traffic and no rhythm. People went in. The street never got them back in any shape worth photographing.

He read it for six minutes. He did not go in. He had enough.

He called Aleksei.

[Beat 468]
He told Aleksei: organ operation, Ashland near Cermak, former clinic, loading dock live on the south side, refrigeration at scale. He said: not the Hartley girl. He said: clean negative on the locate. Different kind of problem.

Aleksei was quiet a moment. Not the quiet of surprise — the quiet of a man re-filing something into the right drawer.

He said: I can route this to Arcturus's organized-crime unit. They'll move. The synthetic-organ manufacturers have been lobbying enforcement for two years; this is the exact operation that hands them the narrative they want about unlicensed modification. They'll jump.

Kyle said: I know.

Aleksei said: you cleared it anyway.

Kyle said: I cleared it.

He'd spent six minutes reading a building and the byproduct was a thing enforcement could use. He'd routed it because not routing it would have meant deciding why not, and he didn't have an answer to that he could stand behind. The community had been trying to push this operation out for two years. Handing it to Arcturus wasn't the community's method. But it was a method that worked, and a method that worked argued for itself, and he'd learned to be suspicious of every time it did.

Aleksei said: I'll make the call.

Kyle said: all right.

He put the bike in gear and didn't feel clean about it.

[Beat 469]
Pixel's message came through while he was still on Ashland, the device buzzing once against his chest pocket.

Storefront on 18th in Pilsen. Operator named Ines Vásquez-Okonkwo — she spelled it out, which meant she expected him to say it right when it came time to introduce himself. Known in the community, she wrote. Good reputation. Cosmetic and light structural. Runs integration stays. Six years at it.

Then: I called ahead. She knows you're coming.

Then: she won't confirm or deny clients. That's standard. Just talk to her. Don't be a cop about it.

Kyle read the last line twice. A note on something she'd seen in him, or a thing she'd tell anyone walking into that room — it was both, and she'd known it was both when she wrote it, and she'd sent it anyway. He put the device away.

He went south on Ashland, turned east on 18th, looking for the storefront. There was a stretch just past Wood where the block ran longer than it should have — he counted the storefronts on the way in and the count didn't agree with the one he'd kept on a hundred prior passes, and the afternoon light over that one length of sidewalk was a half-shade off the light on either side of it, and under the ordinary street noise there was a low hum that arrived a fraction before anything he could name as its source. He'd ridden over a few of these in his years in the city. You learned to log them and keep moving; the ones who stopped to look usually came back with less than they'd brought. He logged it and kept moving. Pilsen in mid-afternoon ran on its own clock, indifferent to the corponation transit rhythm that organized the rest of GLMZ's day. The deliveries were local. The commerce stayed in the neighborhood.

He found the door. It was the kind of door that was there for people who already knew it was there.

[Beat 470]
Inside, the storefront resolved into a different conversation than the street had let on. Clean lines. Equipment that ran better than the unlicensed category was supposed to allow — the air filtration quiet and steady at a throughput that meant someone took it seriously, not someone faking the look of taking it seriously. Clear surfaces. Light that told the truth about colors instead of flattering them.

The walls had photographs. Printed on actual paper, mounted square in plain frames — documentation that cost effort in 2225, when everything defaulted to a display surface you could wipe with a thought. People who had agreed to be kept.

A man with cat ears the color of good wood, alert, tracking something past the edge of the frame, ears that were plainly listening. A small smile that said he knew about the camera and was choosing to be amused by it.

A woman with bioluminescent lines under her skin, running the map of her own veins, faint blue, the glow even and unwavering in a way that said the body had stopped arguing and started counting it as self.

A person whose arms ended in something that was not quite hands and not quite tools and was unmistakably both. The photograph didn't apologize for it. The photograph didn't explain it. The photograph had decided it didn't have to.

Kyle stood in front of them longer than he'd allotted. He was still counting the ways they looked back at him when Ines came through from the back.

[Beat 471]
She was forty-something, with the particular steadiness of someone who had done precision work for a long time in conditions that punished mistakes. Not a performed steadiness — not the calm of someone who had learned that calm was the right register for this kind of meeting. The steadiness of a person who had decided, a long time ago, to do this work carefully.

She did not ask why he was there. She said: Pixel said you were looking for someone and that you had enough judgment not to make this harder than it needs to be.

Kyle said he was looking for a young woman, 22, Evanston background. He would not use her name.

Ines said: I won't confirm or deny clients.

Kyle said: I know.

She said: then what is it you actually want from me?

He said: I want to understand the operation well enough to know whether the person I'm looking for is here voluntarily. He said: I'm not interested in what you're doing here as a business matter. I'm not enforcement, I'm not Arcturus, and I'm not a journalist. Someone who matters to real people is not answering her neuretics and I want to understand why before I accept any particular explanation.

He said: tell me how this works.

Ines looked at him for a moment. The assessment was the same one the first operator had done, but slower. She said: sit down. She went to make coffee.

[Beat 472]
She explained without preamble and without the performance of someone translating a complicated thing for someone who wouldn't understand it. She explained it the way she explained it to clients: as a factual account of the structure they were navigating.

Licensed channels required ID registration. For anyone under 26, structural modifications — anything involving neural re-routing, joint replacement with SNT integration, functional limb addition — required parental consent. The liability provision from the 2218 insurance revision. A twenty-two-year-old was an adult in every sense except this one: the structural modification clause ran to 26, and between 18 and 26 you needed a parent's signature to get neural work done through any licensed channel.

After parental consent, she said, there was the waiting period. Six months, mandatory, for anything involving neural re-routing, on the grounds that neurological modification required observation for adverse effects before the modification could be classified as stable. The adverse effects rate for modern SNT-bonded modifications, in her practice, across six years, was 0.1%. She said this without flourish. The industry number, she said, was 0.3%. The waiting period was written into the regulatory framework in 2220, two years after the 2218 insurance revision, and the language of the provision had been drafted with significant input from the licensed modification industry. The adverse-effects observation window it required corresponded precisely with the industry's standard six-month product refresh cycle.

She said: I have a 0.1% adverse-event rate. She let that sit. She said: the waiting period is not about patient safety.

[Beat 473]
He did not threaten. He did not invoke Arcturus as a weight on the table. He did not use the name Celeste, not once, and Ines did not ask.

He pulled out his device and placed it on the counter between them, screen up, showing the semi-private social feed. He let her read it.

She read it the way he'd read it in the Hartley apartment — not skimming, actually reading, moving through it in the sequence the person had built it in over eight months. The language was specific. The research was careful. The terminology was correct, which meant it was the terminology of someone who had learned it from the inside, not from a search query. Baseline drift, willing flesh, integration stays, the unlicensed corridor. Ghost period, she'd called it. The person who had written this had learned that term from someone.

Ines read it twice. Then she looked up at Kyle. She did not ask who it was. She asked: what are you going to do when you find what you're looking for?

He said: I'm going to return a girl to the people who are worried about her.

She said: what if she doesn't want to go?

He said: then I tell the people who are worried about her where she is, and that she's alive, and that she's safe, and that she's making her own choices. That's the job I was actually hired to do.

Ines looked at him for a long moment. She had heard that claim before, from someone who had not meant it the same way. He did not add anything. He let it sit.

[Beat 474]
Ines said: the integration window closes in eighteen hours. I won't wake her early. A stress event during integration — elevated cortisol, adrenal spike, fear response — can spike rejection. The SNT bridge is in the most sensitive phase of the bonding sequence right now. An interruption is not just uncomfortable. It is a medical risk. I will not create that risk for anyone's timeline.

She said it without anger. She said it the way she said everything: as a fact about how things worked.

She said: if you want to be here when she surfaces, come back in eighteen hours. I will tell her someone is waiting. I will tell her it is her choice whether to speak to you. If she says yes, you'll meet her. If she says no, you'll have confirmation that she is alive and here voluntarily, and you'll carry that back to whoever needs it.

Kyle said: that's fair.

She said: yes.

He stood up. He said: the social feed — she wrote the term ghost period. Where did she learn that?

Ines said: she found one of the community's documentation resources. Someone has been maintaining a careful description of the integration process — not a clinical manual, something readable — and it's been circulating in the semi-private channels for about a year. She'd read it multiple times. She quoted it correctly.

Kyle said: I know the terms because someone explained them to me today. She learned them on her own.

Ines said: yes. She said: there's a difference.

He went to get his bike. He had eighteen hours.

[Beat 475]
He called from the sidewalk outside Ines's, facing the street. Traffic on 18th went about its business. A woman walked past with three children and a dog and managed all five of them with the ease of someone who ran this route daily and found nothing about it worth her attention.

He said: your daughter is safe. She's not missing. She's in a voluntary medical procedure and she'll be able to talk in eighteen hours.

He did not give the address.

Mei-Lin was quiet long enough that he checked the connection. The indicator said it was live.

She asked: is she okay?

He said: yes.

She asked: did she know what she was getting into?

He said: she knew. Looks like she'd been planning it for months.

Another silence. Not the silence of someone taking a hit. The silence of someone setting down a thing she'd been carrying braced.

She said: don't tell Douglas the address.

He said: I wasn't going to.

She said: thank you.

He said: all right. He ended the call. The woman with the three children and the dog had turned the corner. The street was exactly as it had been. He stood on the sidewalk a moment longer than he needed to, then went to find somewhere to spend eighteen hours.

[Beat 476]
He found a diner near 18th with booths and bad coffee and the specific virtue of containing no one who wanted to talk to him. In the recessed doorway beside the entrance someone had built up a shrine of paper bus transfers — the perforated kind, in the faded ink of the old Loop-Pilsen line, the line that had stopped printing paper in 2161 — fanned and layered and weighted down with a stone, and on top of the stack a few that looked printed yesterday, the ink still sharp. He ordered something off a menu that had given up on being appetizing sometime in the last decade and settled for being accurate, and took it to the back booth where the window showed him the sidewalk and the sidewalk showed him nothing alarming.

Two booths up, a pair of freelancers he didn't know were working through a job board between them, low, the way you talk about a thing that's bothering you more than you want to admit. One of them kept circling back to a contract that had come in clean — too clean, he said, listed client, listed scope, full retainer up front, and when he'd run the client it resolved to a shell that resolved to another shell. Came from nowhere, the freelancer said. Paid like somewhere. The other one told him to take the money and stop reading into it. Kyle filed the shape of it and went back to his coffee. He'd seen the shape before, on his own boards, more than once this year. He hadn't decided yet what it was a shape of.

His Neuretics ran its inventory. He let it run. Brace at 94%. Cortical load nominal — nominal being a word that meant one thing on a standard install and something else on Atlas-grade. SNT bridges adapting, the way they were always adapting, body and hardware locked in the same slow negotiation that never closed.

They'd told him at the last calibration that 94% was fine. He'd nodded. He had not asked when the number that was fine became the number that was not.

He ate. The food was unremarkable. He was grateful for the unremarkability.

[Beat 477]
Aleksei called back two hours later.

He said: Arcturus moved on the Ashland operation faster than either of them would have predicted. He said: faster than I predicted, which I want to be accurate about.

He said: it turns out it had already been reported to Arcturus's organized-crime whistleblower line three days before Kyle or Aleksei touched it. Someone from inside the community had called it in. The report was specific and well-documented — the caller knew the layout, knew the refrigeration infrastructure, knew the operational pattern. Arcturus had been building a case file for three days and were waiting for a pretext to move.

Kyle said: the community was cleaning its own neighborhood.

Aleksei said: looks like. He sounded like he found this interesting — not surprised exactly, but interested in the same way you're interested when a system you expected to be simpler turns out to have more internal structure than the outside view suggested.

He said: the organ operation had been in violation of something. Not a legal statute. Something internal. A code the community enforces that has no official standing but apparently has significant practical standing.

Kyle said: they have a code.

Aleksei said: apparently. He said: clean resolution on his end. The organ-trafficking comp plus the Hartley locate fee was a better day's work than expected. He said the father would be unhappy with the outcome but would pay, and that client satisfaction meant different things to different clients. He said: I'm calling you square on the negative-findings deal.

Kyle said: good.

Aleksei hung up. Kyle looked at his coffee. He ordered another one.

[Beat 478]
He turned the Aleksei information over in the back booth with his second bad coffee.

The Mod Scene — Celeste's term in her feed, the term the operators used among themselves, a name that carried the weight of people who'd chosen it rather than a bureaucratic category that had been assigned to them — had no formal structure. No incorporation, no legal standing, no board, no charter, no advocacy organization. It lived in the gaps left by the licensed industry's routing and the regulators' enforcement priorities. In the technical sense, it was invisible.

And yet. The Ashland operation had run for years. The first operator had said two years of community effort to push it out. Two years, and someone had finally made a specific, documented, actionable call to Arcturus's organized-crime line — built carefully over time, carrying exactly what enforcement needed to move.

He'd spent his whole life inside systems with formal structures. Charters, oversight boards, ethics committees, compliance frameworks. He had watched every one of them violate its own code at every level and every scale, in ways that were either invisible or officially excusable, while holding the full legitimate standing to enforce the codes they were breaking.

The Mod Scene had no standing at all. It had enforced its code anyway.

He drank the coffee. It had gone cold somewhere in the thinking and he drank it anyway. The thought stayed. He turned the cup a quarter-turn on the table and left it where it was.

[Beat 479]
Eight months. He kept coming back to eight months.

The feed documented eight months of deliberate research. Which meant the thinking had started before the documentation, which meant the decision had been building longer than that. Years, maybe. Since some age at which she'd understood a thing about herself that the official channels had no mechanism for hearing.

She'd tried the licensed route. The parental-consent provision had stopped her at 21 — adult in every functional sense, able to vote, sign contracts, enlist, buy property, but the structural-modification clause ran to 26, and between 18 and 26 you needed a parent's signature for neural work through any licensed channel. Her parents' answer hadn't been no. It had been we'll discuss it. She'd understood what that meant. She'd waited a year. At 22 she could consent without them, but the waiting period caught her instead — six months, mandatory, clocked from a first licensed consultation she'd never had, which meant starting from zero.

So she'd planned for two years. Saved the money. Researched the corridor. Found the right operator. Made the appointment. Gone offline on purpose, because the protocol required it.

He thought about what that took. Two years of building toward one thing while every official structure in your life keeps telling you: not yet, not like this, not without supervision.

He had done worse for smaller reasons. He was not sure he had ever done it with that much care.

[Beat 480]
Pixel showed up.

He hadn't called her. He hadn't told her where he was. The diner on 18th wasn't a place she knew through any channel he'd ever given her. He looked up from his coffee and she was there at the end of the booth, looking at him with the arithmetic face — the one she wore when a calculation had more variables in it than she'd budgeted for.

She sat down across from him.

He said: how'd you find me?

She said: calibration access. She said it the way she said true things — not defensively, just accurately. She had neuretics calibration access for his installation; she'd had it for months. Standard arrangement for the grade of hardware he carried, the kind of thing you hand an independent tech when the alternative is quarterly trips to a licensed facility that charges double and asks half the questions. She said: I can see your ping when your neuretics are live. I shut off the passive when you asked me to. The query still works.

He said: I didn't ask you to come.

She said: I know. She picked up the menu, read it the way she read a schematic somebody else had drawn badly, and set it down. She said: coffee. Whatever you're having.

He waved the server over. The coffee came. She took a sip and made the exact face he'd made on his first cup — the involuntary one, before the discipline caught up.

He didn't comment. They sat.

[Beat 481]
She told him about her hands.

Not offered, exactly. Told — the way she told him things when she'd decided the relevant context had arrived, that the moment and the person across from it had earned it. She said: the neuretic-enhancement stitching. The thing that lets me feel current when I'm working. I got it unlicensed, at nineteen. Pilsen, actually. Different shop, different operator, same corridor.

She said: I got it because it makes me better at my job. She paused. She said: I got it because it was mine. At nineteen there wasn't much in my life that was mine in the sense of chosen, and done, and finished. My mother was already sick. The decisions were all being made by people bigger than me — the installer who'd done her modification, the insurance company that named what happened after, the licensed channel that had all the paperwork and none of the people who'd answer for it. She'd gone through the right channels. Standard-grade SNT. Approved. Stamped.

She said: the bridge degraded at month eight.

Kyle said: did you report it?

She looked at the table. She said: to whom?

He held that. He didn't put anything in the space. She hadn't said it for effect — she'd said it as a real question she'd spent real time chasing an answer to and had never found one that led anywhere.

He said: I'm sorry about your mother.

She said: I know. She picked up her coffee. She drank it.

[Beat 482]
He asked what she made of the case. The girl, the parents, the choice.

Pixel looked at her coffee. She said: the parents are scared. That's real, I'm not dismissing it. It's the specific scared that comes when someone you've known as one thing has been quietly turning into another thing for a long time, and the whole turn surfaces in one afternoon. They're not afraid for the daughter standing in Pilsen. They're afraid for the one who was supposed to come to them with the decision and let them weigh in. She made the decision two years ago. The consultation was a formality she already knew the answer to.

Kyle said: is that bad?

She turned it over the way she turned over a hard calibration — not ducking the question, weighing it before she'd let herself answer.

She said: I don't know. I think it costs everyone the same, from the inside. The daughter, doing the only available version of the thing she needed. The mother, watching the gap between what she knew and what was true slam shut in one motion. The father, scared in a different shape, pouring concrete around it.

She said: the right thing and the thing that hurts are sometimes the same thing. She said: in my experience you can't pull them apart once you're inside them.

He said: yeah. The coffee had gone cold. He drank it anyway.

[Beat 483]
He arrived at Ines's at the agreed hour. The door was as it had been — visible to him now as something that had a function and was doing it.

Aleksei was already there. Leaning on the side of his car with his arms crossed in the specific posture of someone who had been waiting with professional patience for a calculated length of time and had found the waiting acceptable. Not tired. Not impatient. Just present, committed to the wait.

Kyle stopped on the sidewalk.

Aleksei said: the father's file included the shop address. The Meridian team got there through a different trail this morning — they were running the regulatory databases, looking for integration-stay operators in the corridor, cross-referenced licensed-adjacent practitioners. Took them two days to build the methodology and six hours to run it. He said: I got here twenty minutes ago. The operator wouldn't let me in.

He said: you have better rapport. He said it without ego in it, as an operational assessment. The same way he'd said he was two days ahead on the case, the way he said anything about relative position — as a fact about the situation, not a claim about himself.

Kyle said: I'll go in.

Aleksei said: I'll be here.

It was not a threat. It was logistics: the father had hired Meridian, Meridian had found the address, Aleksei was there to complete the contract, and completing the contract required someone to be present when the client's daughter came out. He would wait. Kyle went to the door.

[Beat 484]
She was sitting in a chair in the back room with a blanket around her shoulders and a cup of something warm held in both hands. She looked young and exhausted and completely calm, the calm of someone who has finally done the irreversible thing. The tension of the building period was gone because the building was finished. What was left was the thing itself.

The ears were visible above the blanket. Pale fur — lighter than he'd expected, the color of winter grass — curved and shaped and clearly mobile in a way that didn't look prosthetic so much as it looked complete. As Kyle's footsteps crossed the threshold of the back room, both ears pivoted toward the sound. Not together — independently, each tracking in its own arc, the way ears do when the audio processing is active and the direction is specific.

She was not directing them. The brain was directing them. The map was already working.

She looked up when the ears had already found him. Her eyes were doing the micro-adjustment thing — the slight unfocus of someone receiving sensory input they don't have the prior experience to normalize yet. Every sound in this room was arriving at a different angle, with more dimensional information, than the same sounds had arrived with yesterday. The brain was sorting new data against a map it was still drawing.

She looked tired and complete. He had walked in expecting one and found both.

[Beat 485]
Kyle stood near the door and let her take the time she needed to locate him in the room before the eyes had fully caught up with what the ears already knew.

The micro-adjustment pattern was going to resolve in two weeks. The brain would finish sorting the new input channels and would start treating them as baseline — as what sound had always been, even though it hadn't, even though the map was three days old. In a month she probably wouldn't be able to reconstruct the before-state from memory. The old pathways would not be erased, just abandoned — and from inside, abandoned and gone look the same.

He knew this because Pixel had told him this morning. He knew it from the inside because his Neuretics had a similar relationship with whatever cognitive topology had existed before it. He had been installed young enough that the before-state was theoretical for him. Something he knew had existed. Something he had no experiential access to. The Atlas installation had mapped over something, and whatever it had mapped over was not recoverable.

He considered whether that was the same thing that was happening to Celeste right now. New map drawn over the old routing. The brain reorganizing around what it had been given.

He decided: similar enough to think about. Different enough to matter. He didn't know where the line was. He kept this to himself and waited for her to be ready to speak.

[Beat 486]
She asked who sent him.

He said: your mother. Not your father — your mother.

He said it that way on purpose. He watched her shoulders drop a half-inch. The drop was not relief exactly — it was the resolution of a specific braced tension, the tension of someone who has been waiting to find out which version of the next thing was coming. Her father's version would have looked different. She had been prepared for her father's version.

She asked: is he here?

Kyle said: a man from the firm your father hired is waiting outside. Not your father. The firm.

She processed this. She asked: is he going to make me leave?

Kyle said: he can't. You're 22. You came here voluntarily. You have documentation from a licensed-adjacent medical operator who is prepared to confirm the nature of the procedure and the nature of your consent to it. He said: the man outside has no authority to compel you to go anywhere. He is waiting because his contract requires him to confirm you are located. He will confirm it. What happens next is your decision, not his.

He said: I'm not here to take you anywhere. I'm here because your mother needed to know you were alive.

She looked at him with the ears still tracking small sounds in the room — the filtration running, the refrigeration in the front, the faint traffic on 18th. Her eyes had settled. The micro-adjustment thing had quieted. She said: okay. She said it the way you say a word when you need a moment to sit in what you've just heard before you figure out what to say next.

[Beat 487]
She said: I tried the licensed route.

She said it without preamble, the way people say things they've rehearsed alone enough times to find the version that carries the most in the fewest words.

She said: the parental-consent provision stopped me at 21. I know I was 21, not 26, but the structural-modification clause in the 2218 insurance revision runs to 26. I researched it. I found the statutory language, found the carve-out for emancipated minors that didn't cover me, found the precedents. My parents' answer wasn't no. It was we'll discuss it. I knew what that meant.

She waited a year. She said: at 22 I could consent without them. The six-month waiting period caught me instead. I know what the waiting period is. I know where it came from. I read the regulatory record.

Eight months researching the corridor. Two years saving. She said: I came with my own Φ. I paid in full.

She said: I didn't tell them, because I knew what my father would hear. And I thought my mother would hear the same thing.

Kyle said: your mother showed me your feed. The semi-private one.

She said: how did she find it?

He said: she was paying attention.

Celeste went still. The ears stilled with her. She looked at the cup in her hands. She said: I didn't think she was.

That was the part the eighteen hours hadn't braced her for. It had cracked something open that her father's verdict would have left sealed. She didn't look relieved. She looked like someone who'd just learned the ground was somewhere other than where she'd planted her feet.

[Beat 488]
She asked: is he going to force me to go home?

He said: I answered that already.

She said: I know. I'm asking again.

He said: no. He said: you're 22. You're standing in a building in Pilsen. You completed a voluntary medical procedure. The only person who gets to decide what you do next is you.

She looked at her hands around the cup. The room's sounds continued to arrive at angles she was still adjusting to — the filtration, the traffic on 18th, something shifting in the front room where Ines was moving quietly. The ears tracked each one in its own brief arc and let it go. Working. Not under her conscious direction. Just working.

She said: I'm not going back.

He said: I know.

She said: you know, or you're —

He said: I know. He said it flat and complete, the way you say something when you mean exactly that and nothing negotiated around it. He said: you're going to have to have a conversation with your parents eventually. Not because of anything I'm in a position to require. Just because you're going to want to, at some point, when you've decided on your own terms what you want to say. That's your business and your timeline.

She said: my mother found the feed.

He said: she did.

A silence. Then she said: okay.

He said: call your mother.

[Beat 489]
She called. He stepped out into the front room and gave her the door.

He stood in front of the photographs. The man with the cat ears — brown, alert, the slight smile. The woman with the bioluminescent veins, the glow tracking the visible path under her skin, patient and steady. The person whose arms were both hands and tools, unambiguously both, the photograph not trying to explain it.

He read the photographs the way he read rooms. Looking for the information they contained, the things they told him about the people who had made them and the people inside them. What the photographs were doing, as objects in this space. What Ines had wanted when she printed them on paper and mounted them carefully.

What he found: everyone in every photograph was looking at the camera directly. Not posed — not the performed straightness of someone who has been told to look at the camera. Present. Looking at the lens the way you look at a person you're in conversation with. The person behind the camera was someone they were in a relationship with, a relationship that included the possibility of being documented, and they had said yes to that, and yes was the face they were making.

He looked at the photographs for a long time. He did not look at his device. He did not run the Neuretics inventory. The voice from the back room was too low to make out words. It went on for a while. He stood and looked at the photographs and waited.

[Beat 490]
Forty minutes. Kyle did not move from the front room.

Ines came out at some point in the middle of it — not to check on him, but to make coffee. Real coffee, the kind that announced itself. She set a cup on the counter near him without commentary and went back to whatever she'd been doing in the side room before all of this had arrived on her Tuesday. The coffee was good. Good beans, good machine, somebody paid to care.

He drank it. He looked at the photographs.

On the counter, near where she'd set the cup, a thin stapled zine lay open — the community documentation, by the look of it, the kind that circulated hand to hand. He turned a page out of something between idle and habit. The paper had been recopied so many times it had gone soft as cloth at the folds, the creases worn to felt, the text on the most-handled pages bleeding gray at the edges where the contrast had given up. He set it back the way it had been lying.

The man with the cat ears. He had probably been in ghost period when those photographs were taken — the ears were fully present, the integration visible as complete, but there was something in his posture that had the quality of someone still locating himself inside a new version of what the room sounded like. Not distress. The opposite of distress. The quality of someone paying attention to something they are still learning to pay attention to.

Kyle thought about the young man sleeping through his ghost period in the first operator's back room. The arm on the foam support. Still in the way of a thing that was present but not yet claimed.

He thought about the sixteen-year-old version of Celeste, who had not yet known what she was building toward but had known something. Starting to know something. The kind of knowing that is not knowledge yet — and does not reverse.

The voice from the back room continued. He drank his coffee.

[Beat 491]
Aleksei was still at his car. Kyle came out and stood on the sidewalk and the late afternoon light on 18th had gone horizontal, the low November angle that meant the day was nearly done.

Aleksei said: clean resolution. He said it with the satisfaction of a person for whom clean resolution was the goal and not a consolation prize. He said: the organ-trafficking comp plus the Hartley locate fee is a reasonable day's work. He said it as accounting, no boast, no performance of indifference. Just the number.

He said: the father's going to be unhappy with the outcome. He said: he'll pay. He said: client satisfaction means different things in different contracts, and this client's contract was with Meridian, and Meridian delivered the locate, and the locate is done.

He looked at the floor of his car for a second, at nothing, at the place where the cargo had been. He said: I came up doing locates because the alternative was being one of the people who sourced the parts. He said it the way you'd give a coordinate. Then he was back, the seam closed before it had finished opening. He said: Meridian pays on time and doesn't ask what I'd do for free.

He paused. He said: the outcome itself — the daughter, what she chose, what she's going to do next — that's not in the contract scope. He said: I don't have a position on it. He said this last thing with the specificity of someone clarifying a thing that could be misconstrued.

He said: you acquitted yourself well. He offered a drink, sometime, unspecific as to when, specific as to meaning: I wouldn't object to operational adjacency again. In their professional language this was the equivalent of a handshake at the end of something that had gone correctly.

Kyle said: yeah.

They left in different directions.

[Beat 492]
While Celeste was on the call, Kyle sat at Ines's counter and she talked about the business climate with the manner of someone who'd decided the information was worth sharing and that he was worth sharing it to.

The corpo-pharma sector — the licensed industry's institutional arm, the companies whose supply chains and product lines and regulatory relationships had built the licensed corridor and kept it — had started publishing studies. Adverse-event-rate studies. Licensed installations against unlicensed ones. The methodology, she said, folded equipment failure into practitioner error: hardware defect and procedure mistake, counted as one thing. In a licensed setting you could separate them. In an unlicensed setting the infrastructure to tell them apart didn't exist, so every adverse event got filed under the practitioner, and reported that way.

She said: they've worked out the unlicensed market is bigger than their licensed one.

Kyle said: how much bigger?

She said: bigger. She said it the way you say a number you've sat with long enough to stop flinching at.

He said: so they push for criminalization.

She said: they already are. There's a proposal sitting at the GLMZ Commerce Authority right now to reclassify unlicensed SNT work from a civil violation to a criminal one. It's in the consultation phase. Consultation closes in six months. The studies are the public-opinion groundwork for what they file the day after it does.

He set it next to the cold coffee, the community that policed itself, the manual that used the word *mortality*. None of it had asked anything of him yet. It would.

[Beat 493]
Celeste came out of the back room. The blanket still around her shoulders, the cup gone, set down somewhere behind her. She looked like she'd been talking for forty minutes and the talking had taken something and handed something back in the same motion.

She said: my mother is coming. Not my father. My mother, alone, and we're going to meet at the coffee shop on the corner and talk. She said: that's all I can commit to. I don't know what comes after that.

Kyle said: that's enough.

She looked at him a moment. The ears moved — small independent adjustments, tracking the room. The filtration. Ines somewhere in the back. The 18th Street traffic, changed in character as the evening came on.

She said: thank you for not making this harder than it had to be.

He said: that's the job.

She said: I don't think that's what it was.

He didn't take that up. She might be right; saying so out loud wasn't what the moment was asking for. He said: go meet your mother.

She looked at him another second. She nodded.

She went to find her jacket. He went to find the door.

[Beat 494]
He didn't go to the coffee shop. He crossed 18th and found a position across the street with a clear line of sight and stood with his jacket collar up and watched. The wind had come around to the north while he was inside, and a glass tower a few blocks east had started up its one low note — a single tone, below speech, that the building made when the air hit it from that quarter, the sound that put the singing floors at a premium on the lease. It sat under everything else on the street like a held breath.

Mei-Lin's car arrived four minutes later. A rental, neutral in color, the kind of unremarkable vehicle a person rents when they don't want to announce themselves or be recognized, which in Evanston would mean something different than in Pilsen and which Mei-Lin had probably thought about before choosing it. She got out without the driver she would ordinarily have. She had come alone.

She saw Celeste on the sidewalk outside the shop and stopped walking.

Fifty feet of sidewalk between them, and neither of them moved into it.

Kyle read their posture from across the street. Mei-Lin's hands were at her sides — still, but the stillness of hands that wanted to rise and hadn't yet resolved the question of whether rising was the right response or the premature one. Her chin was level. She was taking in the ears, which were visible above the collar of Celeste's jacket, and she did not look away from them. She was looking at her daughter. She was looking at the ears as part of her daughter.

Celeste's chin was slightly elevated — the posture of someone who has braced for a verdict and cannot yet read the expression across fifty feet of street.

Something in Mei-Lin's posture decided. Kyle could not have said exactly what changed. Some small configuration in the shoulders, the hands. Whatever it was, it was the thing.

They closed the distance. Mei-Lin said something Kyle was too far to hear, and the ears turned toward the sound before Celeste did — the soft grey curve of them swinging to find her mother's voice a half-second ahead of the girl's own face, the way a thing built to listen finds what it was built to find.

He turned. He had enough.

[Beat 495]
The motorcycle north through Pilsen. The gray zone. The West Side grid. Milwaukee.

He ran the inventory out of habit — brace at 94%, cortical load nominal for Atlas-grade which was a relative term, SNT bridges adapting, the body perpetually in negotiation with the hardware it had been given — and then stopped. He ran it again, but slower. Not performing a check. Actually reading it.

The brace at 94%: the 6% was two years of use above the rated operational load. The rating was calibrated for a standard adult male at standard operational intensity. Both numbers were theoretical when applied to him. He had been told at the last calibration that 94% was fine. He had not asked when *fine* became *not fine*. He noticed himself not asking.

The cortical load: nominal. The Atlas NeoCortex running at a level that would register as critical emergency on a standard installation. He had known this since the first briefing document someone had given him about the installation, which he had read once and filed. He had read it once and put it away — information he had, not information he sat with.

The SNT bridges: adapting. Body fighting hardware. Hardware holding. The perpetual truce.

He thought about the man sleeping through his ghost period. The arm on the foam support. Still in the way of a thing that was present and not yet claimed. The brain building the map while the body healed.

He thought: every piece of hardware in me was installed before I had a word for what it meant to have it.

He rode north. Milwaukee in the evening had its own rhythm.

[Beat 496]
Back at The Pivot. 1554 W Chicago Ave, West Town, the building that had become his address in the way that buildings become addresses: not by any particular decision but by the accumulation of time and the gradual reduction of reasons to be elsewhere. Somewhere down the stairwell the wind found the gap it found every night and pulled one long mineral note out of the wet ferrocement, the building breathing out the smell of cold stone and old water along with it.

He put Silence on the wall — the mounting bracket catching the katana's weight with the same click it always made, the sound of a thing correctly placed — and got out of his jacket and opened the cold box. The cold box contained: some condiment in the lower left corner, three items he had been meaning to throw out for a week, and nothing that constituted a meal. He made a note to buy groceries.

He did not buy groceries. He sat on the edge of the bed in the quiet of the apartment.

The apartment was not quiet. Pixel's bass was coming through the wall at the volume she kept it when she was working late — not performance volume, working volume, the rhythm that filled the space she thought in and had become as ambient to the building's soundscape as the HVAC or the elevator. He had lived next to it for long enough that he navigated it without registering it, the way you navigate anything that has been there long enough to become infrastructure.

He sat on the edge of the bed and let the bass come through the wall and ran nothing and thought about nothing in particular and did not conclude anything. The building went about its business around him. He sat with it for a while.

[Beat 497]
The message from Douglas Hartley came in at 19:42. Formal, correctly structured, the kind of message that had been composed by someone who communicated in the register of documentation. It confirmed payment. It acknowledged resolution of the contract. It said: we appreciate your professionalism.

There was no mention of what the resolution had actually consisted of. No mention of Celeste's location, her choice, the nature of the procedure she'd completed, the condition in which she'd been found. No mention that Mei-Lin had made a separate arrangement and that Kyle had honored the separate arrangement rather than the invoice. No mention that Douglas's theory — the curated articles, the organ-harvesting conclusion — had been wrong in a way that mattered.

The message had the specific quality of a communication from a man who had decided that the outcome was acceptable. The acceptable outcome was: the file was closed, the contractor had been paid, the daughter had been located. What the daughter had done and what she was going to do next and what Mei-Lin's meeting on the sidewalk had produced — these things existed outside the contract scope, and Douglas Hartley had a clear relationship with things that existed outside the contract scope. He filed them outside the perimeter of his concern.

Kyle marked the contract closed. He did not reply. He put the device face-down on the table and looked at the wall and listened to Pixel's bass and let the evening wind down.

[Beat 498]
The thought that did not resolve.

Celeste had done it by choice. Eight months of research, two years of savings, careful selection, voluntary consent, the integration stay taken on her own terms in a place she'd chosen for its reputation. The modification she'd wanted since she was sixteen — she'd said this and he believed it because the social feed said it in the particular language of someone who had been carrying a thing for a long time. She was trying to become the person her body was supposed to map. That was the project. Building toward the configuration that matched what the inside had always been running.

His modifications had been done to him. Some before consent was a concept he had operational access to — the SNT bonding, the early NeoCortex installation, the Atlas-grade upgrade at the facility where the installer had shown him the manual once and not asked if he had questions. The brace came later, after the accident, in the specific context of there is no alternative and you need to decide now, which was the kind of consent that exists on paper and not in fact. The hardware had been mapped over him, not with him.

She was changing her body to match herself. His body had been changed before anyone asked him.

He considered whether those were the same project run from opposite ends of consent.

He did not finish the thought.

[Beat 499]
He heard the bass change.

Pixel's music had a taxonomy. Background bass was the ambient layer — a rhythm she didn't have to attend to, the sonic equivalent of leaving the lights on, something that kept the working frequency in the room without demanding anything from her. He had learned this over months of living next to it. Present-tense bass was different. A shift in the mix, something she was actively engaging with rather than occupying the same air as. The difference was not dramatic. It was just unmistakable, like the difference between an empty window and one with someone standing at it.

He heard the bass change from the first category to the second.

She was still in there. Still working, or listening, or both, which for Pixel were frequently the same activity. It meant she was present and awake and in the mode where something was interesting to her. It did not mean she wanted company.

He got up. He went to the kitchen. He knocked on the wall once — the single knock, the one that meant: I'm here, I know you're there, no claim on your attention unless you want to give it. The knock that asked nothing and stated only a fact about location.

He stood in his kitchen and waited to see if the wall had anything to say back.

[Beat 500]
No response.

The bass continued on the other side of the wall, present-tense and uninterrupted. It didn't shift back to background. It also didn't shift to anything that suggested she'd heard the knock and was deciding whether to answer it. It continued the way it had been continuing.

He stood in the kitchen for a moment. He looked at the wall. The wall was not informative.

He went back to the main room and sat down. He pulled his device off the table and looked at it and put it back down. He did not want to look at anything on it. The contract was closed. The messages he'd been carrying through the day had all resolved. The device had nothing new to offer him.

That was fine.

He sat in the apartment. The bass came through the wall. The building's ventilation ran its evening cycle. Somewhere above him, a door closed in the particular way of the third-floor unit that had a closing problem and had never gotten it fixed, the mechanism catching late and landing with more authority than it intended.

He knew the sound. He knew most of the building's sounds by this point. The third-floor door. The elevator cable sound that the building produced at 2200 every night as the automated maintenance cycle ran. Pixel's bass changing taxonomies.

He sat and waited to find out if the wall was going to knock back. That was fine too. He had time.

[Beat 501]
Fifteen minutes later there was a knock on his door.

Pixel, in workshop clothes — the ones she wore when she was doing the work that left marks, the worn canvas and the thermal underlayer she kept for the sessions that ran long and got hot. She was holding a soldering iron she had clearly forgotten to put down. She was holding it the way you hold something when you've been in the middle of a task and the transition between the task and the next thing happened faster than the object in your hand could catch up with.

She looked at him with the arithmetic face. The expression she wore when she had calculated something and had arrived at the answer and was now implementing it.

She asked: you eat?

He said: no.

She said: come on then. She turned around. She went back across the hall. The soldering iron was still in her hand.

He got his jacket off the chair and followed. The door to her unit was open. Her workshop was off to the left, the door to it also open, the work-in-progress visible on the bench in the state she'd left it — something partially assembled, the components laid out in the order she'd been working through them. The kitchen was straight ahead, the overhead light already on, the table cleared in the specific way of someone who had cleared it by moving everything that had been on it to somewhere else in the kitchen rather than putting anything away.

He sat at the table. She went to the kitchen. The soldering iron went on the counter, finally, where it had apparently decided to spend the evening.

[Beat 502]
She made something while he sat at the table. He didn't ask what, because asking would have changed the quality of the process and the quality of the process was the point. She moved in the kitchen the way she moved at the workbench — efficiently, knowing where everything was, the sequence of actions already mapped before she started the first one.

She talked while she cooked, not at him but near him, which was the distinction she made between the two modes and which he had learned to read correctly. Near-him talking was thinking out loud at someone who was present and not going to interrupt. It was the same register she used at the workbench when she was working through a problem. He was furniture, but specifically the kind of furniture that was there by agreement.

She said: the commission she'd taken today — a client who wanted subcutaneous lighting installed, a pattern that went up the forearm and wrapped around the wrist. The power cells needed to be routed around an existing neuretics cluster that sat in a location she hadn't run into before, in the space between the radius and the ulna about about an inch distal from the standard cluster position. The available space for the routing at that position was approximately half an inch. The power cells at standard spec were sixteen.

She said: so I need to either find a narrower gauge cell or find a routing that goes around the cluster rather than past it, which adds path length, which affects current consistency, which affects the evenness of the glow pattern.

She described this at some length. The food was ready before the problem was.

He ate. She sat down across from him and continued thinking out loud, occasionally eating. The food was good. It was always good.

[Beat 503]
He told her.

The cat ears. The integration stay, what it required and why, the motor map the brain was building from the vestigial architecture it had inherited and the new pathways the work had opened up. The ghost period — the two to four weeks where the ears would be present and working and not quite felt as self yet, the brain still in the process of claiming them. The photographs on Ines's wall, printed on paper, mounted carefully, everyone looking at the camera directly, present.

He told her what Celeste had said — I'd been wanting this since I was sixteen. He said it straight, without editorial.

Pixel said: sounds right.

He said: you were younger. You were nineteen when you got the hand modification.

She said: nineteen is a different age than sixteen.

He said: is it?

She thought about it. Not performing thought — actually thinking, the real consideration, the kind that takes actual time. She said: no. Not really. She said: the age when you know something is yours is not the same for everybody and it doesn't follow a predictable schedule.

He said: yeah.

She said: I was nineteen. She knew at sixteen. The gap is a different shape, not a different magnitude.

He said: yeah.

Neither of them had moved from the table. The plates had been clear for several minutes. The kitchen was quiet except for the building's ambient sounds coming through the walls and floor, the ones he'd learned to navigate without registering them. He was registering them now.

[Beat 504]
He walked back across the hall.

Pixel's door stayed open behind him until he reached his own — he heard it not click shut, heard it stay as it was. He didn't look back. He stood at his door with the key in the lock and the hall doing what halls did at this hour: quiet, the overhead strip cutting the shadows into sections, the third-floor door somewhere above in its closed position.

The ears in the photographs, tracking sounds in rooms the camera couldn't hear.

The man sleeping through his ghost period, the arm on the foam support, the brain building toward something it had never known it was missing.

The inventory his Neuretics ran every six hours — brace at 94%, cortical load nominal, SNT bridges adapting. The same numbers every time. The same negotiation. The hardware that had never been asked whether it wanted to be his. The body that had never been asked whether it wanted the hardware. Two things in one configuration, learning to pass for one entity, neither of them consulted before the combining started.

He turned the key. He went inside.
